) are problematic for
the formulation of the theory on a non-spin manifold, since if
w_{2}(X)\neq 0
the spin bundles

S^{\pm}
are not
well-defined. The field
M
is in that case a section of a Spin
c

bundle
W^{+}
. To implement this physically, we couple the massive hypermultiplet to a weakly coupled
U(1)
, whose
flux
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
equals the Chern class of a Spin
c
structure. The two terms with the same representation in
(
100
) and (
101
) have opposite charge
under this
U(1)
. After coupling to a Spin
c
bundle
\mathcal{L}
,
the holomorphic bosons are sections of
S^{+}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{1/2}
, and their
complex conjugates are sections of
S^{+}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{-1/2}
. Similarly, the fermions combine to sections of the
bundles

S^{-}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\pm 1/2}
and
S^{+}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{\pm 1/2}
.
For a mathematical description of this procedure see equation (
482
) below. Since there is
an infinite family of Spin
c
structures
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\in L+K_{X}/2
, we have an infinite family of
twisted theories. Put differently, the definition of the twisted theory in the UV
requires the introduction of the extra datum of a
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure. Moreover,
in order to write the Lagrangian one must also choose a
{\rm Spin}^{c}
connection. Two different

{\rm Spin}^{c}
connections differ by a globally defined one-form.

Once we have chosen a
{\rm Spin}^{c}
-structure and formulated the
topologically twisted theory the hypermultiplet fields become spinors in
the chiral spin bundle
W^{+}
. The
Q
-fixed point equations of this twist
are the non-Abelian (adjoint) monopole equations
[
4
,
17
]

\begin{split}&F^{+}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\bar{\sigma}_{\mu\nu})^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\left[\bar{M}_{(\dot{\alpha}},M_{\dot{\beta})}\right]=0,\\
&\not{D}M=\sum_{\mu=1}^{4}\sigma^{\mu}D_{\mu}M_{\dot{\alpha}}=0,\end{split}

(102)

Here
D_{\mu}
is the chosen
{\rm Spin}^{c}
-connection on
W^{+}
. Moreover,
in explicit computations we choose a representation of gamma matrices in an orthonormal
frame in the form

\gamma^{a}=\begin{pmatrix}0&\sigma^{a}\\
\bar{\sigma}^{a}&0\\
\end{pmatrix}

(103)

where
\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}
are the standard Pauli matrices in Euclidean space,

\sigma^{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&1\\
1&0\end{array}\right),\qquad\sigma^{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&-i\\
i&0\end{array}\right),\qquad\sigma^{3}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
0&-1\end{array}\right),\qquad\sigma^{4}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}i&0\\
0&i\end{array}\right).

(104)

while
(\bar{\sigma}^{a})^{\dot{\alpha}\beta}=\varepsilon^{\dot{\alpha}\dot{\beta}}\varepsilon^{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{a}_{\alpha\dot{\beta}}
and

\left(\bar{\sigma}^{ab}\right)^{\dot{\beta}}_{\,\,\dot{\alpha}}=\frac{1}{4}\left(\bar{\sigma}^{a}\sigma^{b}-\bar{\sigma}^{b}\sigma^{a}\right)^{\dot{\beta}}_{\,\,\dot{\alpha}}~.

(105)

We will sometimes refer to (
102
) as the adjoint-SW equations.

The equations (
102
) are invariant under the transformation
M\to e^{i\varphi}M
,
\bar{M}\to e^{-i\varphi}\bar{M}

[
66
,
67
]
, which is the
U(1)_{B}
symmetry in the
untwisted theory. We deduce from (
9
) that the mass
m

is neutral under this
U(1)
. The ghost number of
m
is 2, the same
as the ghost number of
\phi

[
66
]
.

The fixed point locus of this equation consists of two components
[
66
,
67
]
7
7

7

A closely related set of equations, the so-called “
SO(3)
monopole equations,” have been studied in the mathematical literature

[
68
,
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
]
. In this case there are two sets of
fixed point equations. The Abelian locus is a collection of Seiberg-Witten moduli spaces for the

U(1)
case, and, although these spaces are smooth they can be non-smoothly embedded into
the full moduli space.
:

•

The instanton component:
M_{\dot{\alpha}}=0
and
F^{+}=0
.

•

The Abelian component: This is the component where the
U(1)_{B}

action is pure gauge. This can only be the case if the gauge group is broken to its diagonal
subgroup, i.e. the
SU(2)
bundle is a sum of line bundles

\mathcal{E}=T\oplus T^{-1}
for some line bundle
T
. Then
M_{\dot{\alpha}}
must be of the form

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
&0\end{array}\right)
or
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&*\\
0&0\end{array}\right)
. The equations reduce to the Abelian Seiberg-Witten equations on this locus.

If we choose the canonical
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure associated to an almost complex structure on
X
then the adjoint-SW equations simplify. In this case the line bundle
\mathcal{L}
is identified with
K^{-1}_{X}
, and sections of
W^{+}\cong S^{+}\otimes K_{X}^{-1/2}
are identified with elements of
\Omega^{0,0}(X,\mathbb{C})\oplus\Omega^{0,2}(X,\mathbb{C})
(
97
). With
\alpha\in\Omega^{0,0}(X,\mathbb{C})
and
\bar{\beta}\in\Omega^{0,2}(X,\mathbb{C})
, we let

M=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\bar{\beta}\\
\alpha\end{array}\right).

(106)

The curvature equation of (
102
) then reads,

\begin{split}&F^{2,0}+\frac{1}{2}[\alpha,\beta]=0,\\
&i\,\omega\wedge F^{1,1}+\frac{1}{2}\,\omega^{2}\,[\alpha,\bar{\alpha}]+[\beta,\bar{\beta}]=0,\end{split}

(107)

while the Spin
c
Dirac equation of the monopole equations reads
[
73
]
,

\not{D}M=\sqrt{2}(\bar{\partial}\alpha+\bar{\partial}^{\dagger}\bar{\beta})+\frac{1}{4}\theta.M+\frac{1}{2}ia.M=0,

(108)

where
\theta
is the Lee form, defined through
d\omega=\theta\wedge\omega
.
The
.
stands for the Clifford action, acting on
W^{+}
as wedge product on the 0-form, and contraction
\lrcorner
on the 2-forms,

\theta.M=\sqrt{2}(\theta^{0,1}\wedge\alpha-\theta^{0,1}\lrcorner\bar{\beta}).

(109)

Here
a\in\Omega^{1}(X)
is such that the connection
\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}
for
\mathcal{L}
is
\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}=\nabla^{\prime}_{\mathcal{L}}+ia
, with
\nabla^{\prime}_{\mathcal{L}}
the Chern connection for
\mathcal{L}
. We will in the following choose
a=0
. If
X
is a spin manifold,
\not{D}
is the Riemannian Dirac operator (
P
in
[
73
, Eq. (3.5.1)]
) twisted by
\nabla_{\mathcal{L}}
.

3.5
Comparison with the Vafa-Witten twist

In Section
3.7
below we are going to show
that if we choose the canonical
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure associated
to an almost complex structure then the
m\to 0
limit of the partition function of the

\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory (without the insertion of observables)
computes, at least formally, the Euler characteristic
of the moduli space of instantons on
X
. On the other hand, we will
also show later on that the partition function of the
N=2^{*}
theory is

S
-duality covariant. These statements should remind one of the
renowned Vafa-Witten (VW) topological quantum field theory obtained by a
different topological twist of the
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM
[
15
]
. Indeed, the
S
-duality transformations of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
partition function discussed in Sections
6
and
7
are identical to those of the VW partition functions. On the other hand, the VW twist and the twist used to define Donaldson theory are decidedly different. It is
therefore interesting to compare the
Q
-fixed equations in detail.

The fields appearing in the Vafa-Witten equations are a connection on a
principal bundle
P\to X
together with a real section
C\in\Omega^{0}(X;{\rm ad}P)

and a real self-dual 2-form
B^{+}\in\Omega^{2,+}(X;{\rm ad}P)
. The
Q
-fixed equations of
the twisted theory are
[
15
]
:

\begin{split}&F^{+}_{\mu\nu}+\frac{1}{2}[C,B^{+}_{\mu\nu}]+\frac{1}{4}[B^{+}_{\mu\rho},B^{+}_{\nu\sigma}]\,g^{\rho\sigma}=0,\\
&D_{\mu}C+D^{\nu}B^{+}_{\mu\nu}=0.\end{split}

(110)

Refs
[
74
,
75
]
discuss analytical aspects of these equations.

At first sight the matter fields in the adj-SW and VW equations appear to be very different.
However, under the isomorphism (
97
) we can identify
M_{\dot{1}}\in\Omega^{0,2}(X;{\rm ad}P)

and
M_{\dot{2}}\in\Omega^{0,0}(X;{\rm ad}P)
. Note that
\Omega^{0,0}(X;{\rm ad}P)
is the space of
complex
-valued sections of
{\rm ad}P
.
One the other hand, again using the almost complex structure we can identify

\Omega^{2,+}=R\oplus\left[\Omega^{2,0}\oplus\Omega^{0,2}\right]_{\mathbb{R}},

(111)

where
R
is the space of
(1,1)
forms proportional to the almost Kähler form
\omega
.
Put differently, we can decompose

B^{+}=\kappa\,\omega+\beta+\bar{\beta},

(112)

with
\beta\in\Omega^{2,0}(X;{\rm ad}P)
,
\bar{\beta}
is its complex conjugate and
\kappa
is a
real section of
{\rm ad}P
. Under these isomorphisms
can thus identify
M_{\dot{2}}
with
C-i\kappa
and
M_{\dot{1}}
with
\bar{\beta}
.

Indeed, one can check that on
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, if we set:

\begin{split}&C=\frac{1}{2}(M_{\dot{2}}+\bar{M}_{\dot{2}}),\\
&B^{+}_{12}=-\frac{i}{2}(M_{\dot{2}}-\bar{M}_{\dot{2}}),\\
&B^{+}_{13}=\frac{1}{2}(M_{\dot{1}}+\bar{M}_{\dot{1}}),\\
&B^{+}_{14}=\frac{i}{2}(M_{\dot{1}}-\bar{M}_{\dot{1}}),\end{split}

(113)

where the other components of
B^{+}_{\mu\nu}
are determined by self-duality
then the adjoint-SW equations are identical to the VW equations. The “first” equation
of each pair of equations, which determines the self-dual part of the curvature,
becomes the same, while the four real equations of the second line of (
110
) in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
, are equivalent to the Dirac equation
\sigma^{\mu}\partial_{\mu}M=0
. If we choose a complex structure for
\mathbb{R}^{4}\simeq\mathbb{C}^{2}
by choosing complex coordinates
z_{1}=x_{1}+ix_{2}
and
z_{2}=x_{3}+ix_{4}
, the
(1,1)
component corresponds to
B^{+}_{12}\,\omega=B^{+}_{12}\,(\frac{i}{2}dz_{1}\wedge d\bar{z}_{1}+\frac{i}{2}dz_{2}\wedge d\bar{z}_{2})\in\Omega^{1,1}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
, while
\beta=\frac{1}{2}(B^{+}_{13}-iB_{14})dz_{1}\wedge dz_{2}\in\Omega^{2,0}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
and
\bar{\beta}=\frac{1}{2}(B^{+}_{13}+iB_{14})d\bar{z}_{1}\wedge d\bar{z}_{2}\in\Omega^{0,2}(\mathbb{C}^{2})
. In particular in this case the VW equations have an
SO(2)
symmetry: The
U(1)_{B}
action on
M_{\dot{\alpha}}
becomes an
SO(2)
action on the separate pairs
(C,B^{+}_{12})
and
(B^{+}_{13},-B^{+}_{14})
.

On a general almost complex manifold the above identification of
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
fields and VW
fields again reveals that the first of the adjoint-SW equations is identical to the first
of the VW equations. The relation between the second of the pairs of equations is a little
more complicated. To understand this relation we begin by writing the second equation of (
110
) in a coordinate independent way as

d_{A}C+d_{A}^{\dagger}B^{+}=0,

(114)

with
d_{A}^{\dagger}=-*d_{A}*
.

The
(0,1)
component of (
114
) reads

\bar{\partial}_{A}C-i\bar{\partial}_{A}\kappa-i\kappa\,\pi_{0,1}\circ\theta+\bar{\partial}_{A}^{\dagger}\bar{\beta}+\pi_{0,1}\circ(d_{A}^{\dagger}\beta)=0,

(115)

where we used that
*\,\omega\wedge\eta=i\eta
for
\eta\in\Omega^{0,1}(X)
.
The terms with
\beta
and
\theta
break the
U(1)_{B}
symmetry, since
\alpha
and
\bar{\beta}
are multiplied by
e^{i\varphi}
, while
\beta
is multiplied by
e^{-i\varphi}
.

If
X
is Kähler,
\pi_{0,1}\circ(d^{\dagger}\beta)=0
and
\theta=0
, (
115
) equals the Dirac equation (
108
) with
\alpha=C-i\kappa
.
For a generic ACS manifold, the term
\pi_{0,1}\circ(d^{\dagger}_{A}\beta)
in Eq. (
115
) is non-vanishing, and expressed in terms of the Nijenhuis tensor defined in Eq. (
568
). One has, in terms of components,

\pi_{0,1}\circ(d^{\dagger}\beta)=-\frac{1}{2}\,*(N_{\mathcal{J}})^{a}_{bc}\,\beta_{a\ell}\,e^{b}\wedge e^{c}\wedge e^{\ell}.

(116)

Note well that the right hand side is tensorial in
\beta
.

While a non-vanishing Nijenhuis tensor and
\theta
obstruct a direct equivalence between the adjoint-SW equations and VW equations, we can determine the deformation of the VW equations to which the adjoint-SW equations are equivalent. To this end, we consider the connection
\nabla_{A,\mathcal{J}}:\Omega^{0}\oplus\Omega^{2+}\to\Omega^{1}
, defined by

\begin{split}\nabla_{A,\mathcal{J}}C&=(d_{A}+\tfrac{1}{4}\theta)\,C,\\
\nabla_{A,\mathcal{J}}B^{+}&=d^{\dagger}_{A}B^{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,*(N_{\mathcal{J}})^{a}_{bc}\,B^{+}_{a\ell}e^{b}\wedge e^{c}\wedge e^{\ell}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,(*\theta\wedge\omega)(\omega\lrcorner B^{+})-\tfrac{1}{4}\theta\lrcorner B^{+}\\
&=d^{\dagger}_{A}B^{+}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,*(N_{\mathcal{J}})^{a}_{bc}\,\beta_{a\ell}e^{b}\wedge e^{c}\wedge e^{\ell}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,*(N_{\mathcal{J}})^{a}_{bc}\,\bar{\beta}_{a\ell}e^{b}\wedge e^{c}\wedge e^{\ell}\\
&\quad+\tfrac{3}{4}\,\kappa\,*\theta\wedge\omega-\tfrac{1}{4}\theta\lrcorner(\beta+\bar{\beta}).\end{split}

(117)

Using that
*\omega^{2}=2
, and
*\theta\wedge\omega=\theta\lrcorner\omega
, one verifies that the
(0,1)
component of

\nabla_{A,\mathcal{J}}C+\nabla_{A,\mathcal{J}}B^{+}=0,

(118)

equals the Spin
c
Dirac equation (
108
) for an ACS manifold. Thus the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
equations are equivalent to the “deformed” VW equations, (
118
) together with the first equation of (
110
).

The VW equations and the deformed VW equations (
118
) differ by a
compact operator on the space of fields. This leads one to conjecture that the
moduli space of solutions will be topologically equivalent. Indeed we will
confirm this with our computations. In the Kähler case the
Q
-fixed point equations are
equivalent without deformation and a nice check on our computations is the agreement
of our partition functions
(in the
m\to 0
limit) with the known expressions for VW theory on Kähler
manifolds given in
[
15
,
16
]
.

It is interesting to compare the instanton and Abelian components in the adjoint-SW
and VW equations. For
X
a Kähler manifold, the VW equations are left invariant under the
U(1)_{B}
symmetry, and the fixed point locus consists of two components
[
15
]
. The instanton component corresponds to
B=C=0
and
F^{+}=0
. For the abelian component
8
8

8

At other places in the literature, for example
[
16
,
13
,
18
]
this component is referred to as the “monopole branch”. Since the
Q
-fixed equations of low energy
effective theory near each of the cusps
u_{j}
are the SW monopole
equations, we have opted to refer to this component as the abelian
component.
, the gauge bundle is a sum of two line
bundles
\mathcal{E}=T\oplus T^{-1}
. Furthermore,
C=0
, and
B
is a sum of a

(2,0)
-form
\beta
and a
(0,2)
-form
\bar{\beta}
, which are of
the form
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&0\\
&0\end{array}\right)
or
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&*\\
0&0\end{array}\right)
. The results for Kähler manifolds were earlier obtained using a mass deformation to

\mathcal{N}=1^{*}

[
15
]
, which demonstrated that the abelian component of the theories
gives rise to the same abelian monopole equations
[
16
]
.

The vanishing theorem of
[
15
]
states that the abelian component does not contribute for Kähler manifolds with curvature
R\geq 0
. These manifolds include K3 and the Fano surfaces, such as

\mathbb{P}^{2}
, the Hirzebruch surfaces
\mathbb{F}_{0}
and

\mathbb{F}_{1}
. For more general algebraic surfaces with
b_{2}^{+}\geq 3
, a mathematical theory has recently developed for the
contribution from the abelian component

[
13
,
14
,
76
,
77
,
78
]
. It confirms and extends results by Vafa
and Witten
[
15
]
for such four-manifolds. Analogously, explicit results for the contribution from the instanton component have been determined mathematically for such manifolds
[
18
,
19
,
20
,
79
]
.

3.6
Virtual dimensions of moduli spaces for instantons and monopoles

We recall in this subsection a number of aspects of bundles,
characteristic classes and moduli spaces.

Bundles and characteristic classes

Let
G
be a Lie group, and
P
a principal
G
-bundle. We let
\mathcal{P}_{r}

be the complex vector bundle
r(P)\otimes\mathbb{C}
associated to
the representation
r
. We are mostly interested in this article in
the groups
G=SU(2)
,
U(2)
or
SO(3)
. For the semi-simple groups

SU(2)
and
SO(3)
, we
let
\mathcal{A}={\rm ad}(P)\otimes\mathbb{C}
be
the complex vector bundle associated to their adjoint
representation, and
\mathcal{E}={\rm sp}(P)\otimes\mathbb{C}
the complex
bundle associated to the two-dimensional (spinorial) representation of the Lie group.

Since the Lie algebra’s of
SU(2)
and

SO(3)
are isomorphic the principal bundle are closely related. If
the second Stiefel-Whitney class
w_{2}(P)
vanishes for a principal
SO(3)
-bundle, it can be lifted to
a principal
SU(2)
bundle, while it can be lifted to a principal
U(2)
-bundle
for any
w_{2}(P)\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z})
. The first Chern
class of the complex associated bundle
\mathcal{E}
satisfies
c_{1}(\mathcal{E})=w_{2}(P)\mod 2H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
.

There are a number of relations between the Chern classes of the
different bundles. The adjoint representation gives an endomorphism of
the fundamental representation. As a result, the adjoint bundle
\mathcal{A}

for
G=U(2)
can be understood as the tensor product,
\mathcal{A}\cong\mathcal{E}^{\lor}\otimes\mathcal{E}
. Using the familiar formulas for the Chern
character of a complex vector bundle
E
,
{\rm ch}(E)={\rm rank}(E)+c_{1}(E)+c_{1}(E)^{2}/2-c_{2}(E)
, and its property under the tensor
product, one finds

c_{1}(\mathcal{A})=0,\qquad c_{2}(\mathcal{A})=4c_{2}(\mathcal{E})-c_{1}(\mathcal{E})^{2}.

Moreover, if
V
is a complex bundle, its complexification
V\otimes_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbb{C}\cong V\oplus iV
, is isomorphic to
E\oplus\bar{E}
. As a
result, the Chern classes of
V\otimes\mathbb{C}
and
V
are related as

c_{1}(V\otimes\mathbb{C})=0,\qquad c_{2}(V\otimes\mathbb{C})=2c_{2}(V)-c_{1}(V)^{2}.

Applying this to the tangent bundle of an almost complex manifold
X
,
we find that the first Pontryagin
class
p_{1}(X)=p_{1}(T_{X})
gives the signature of
X
,

\int_{X}p_{1}(X)=-\int_{X}c_{2}(T_{X}\otimes\mathbb{C})=3\sigma,

(119)

where we used (
84
).

For a complex bundle
\mathcal{P}_{r}
with connection, the Chern character is expressed in
terms of the (real) field strength
F
as

{\rm ch}(\mathcal{P}_{r})={\rm Tr}_{r}[\,\exp(F/2\pi)\,],

(120)

where the trace is taken in the representation
r
. We define the instanton number
k(P)
as

k(P)=-\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{X}{\rm Tr}_{\bf 2}(F^{2}),

where the trace
is taken in the two-dimensional spinorial representations of the Lie
algebras. In terms of Chern classes
c_{j}(\mathcal{E})
,
k(P)
reads,

\begin{split}&SU(2):\quad k(P)=c_{2}(\mathcal{E}),\\
&U(2):\quad k(P)=c_{2}(\mathcal{E})-\frac{1}{2}c_{1}(\mathcal{E})^{2},\\
&SO(3):\quad k(P)=c_{2}(\mathcal{E})-\frac{1}{4}c_{1}(\mathcal{E})^{2}.\end{split}

(121)

Note the trace in the adjoint representation,
{\rm Tr}_{\bf 3}(F^{2})
, is 4 times
{\rm Tr}_{\bf 2}(F^{2})
. We have more generally for the instanton number
k(P)=-1/(16h^{\lor}\pi^{2})\int_{X}{\rm Tr}_{\dim(G)}(F^{2})
, where
{\rm Tr}_{\dim(G)}
is the trace in the adjoint representation of
G
,
and
h^{\lor}_{G}
is its dual Coxeter number. (Recall
h^{\lor}_{SO(3)}=2
.)

Atiyah-Hitchin-Singer index formula

The Atiyah-Hitchin-Singer index formula is an important tool for the
determination of the expected or virtual dimensions of the moduli
spaces. To state this formula, we consider a principal
G
-bundle
P
, and a
Dirac operator coupled to the complex associated
\mathcal{P}_{r}
in representation
r
.

{\rm\bf D}_{A}:\Gamma(S^{+}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{r})\to\Gamma(S^{-}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{r}).

(122)

If
X
is not a spin-manifold, we consider
S^{\pm}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{r}
as
Spin
c
bundles. Its index is defined as

{\rm Ind}({\bf D}_{A})={\rm dim}({\rm Ker}({\bf D}_{A}))-{\rm dim}({\rm Ker}({\bf D}_{A}^{\dagger})).

(123)

The Atiyah-Hitchin-Singer index theorem expresses the index as

{\rm Ind}({\rm\bf D}_{A})=\int_{X}{\rm ch}(\mathcal{P}_{r})\,\hat{A}(X),

(124)

where for
X
a four-manifold,
\hat{A}(X)=1-p_{1}(X)/24
.

Instantons

Let
\mathcal{M}_{\rm i}(k)
be the moduli space of anti-self dual
connections with instanton number
k
. The complex dimension of
\mathcal{M}_{\rm i}(k)

is the index of the operator

\delta_{A}=d_{A}^{\dagger}+d_{A}^{+}:\Omega^{1}(\mathcal{A})\to\Omega^{0}(\mathcal{A})\oplus\Omega^{2+}(\mathcal{A}).

(125)

To this end, we recall for
{\rm Index}(\delta_{A}^{\dagger})=-{\rm Index}(\delta_{A})

[
80
]

{\rm Index}(\delta_{A}^{\dagger})=-2\,h^{\lor}_{G}\,k(P)+{\rm dim}(G)\,(\chi+\sigma)/4.

(126)

As a result, the virtual real dimension of the moduli space of
G=SO(3)
instantons is

\begin{split}{\rm vdim}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}})&=4\,h^{\lor}_{G}\,k(P)-{\rm dim}(G)\,(\chi+\sigma)/2\\
&=8\,k-\frac{3}{2}\,(\chi+\sigma).\end{split}

(127)

Abelian monopole equations (Seiberg-Witten equations)

The monopole equations (
102
) consist of two equations,
and the deformation operator consists therefore of two operators

\mathcal{D}_{A}=(\delta_{A},{\rm\bf D}_{A})
. As discussed in Section
3.3
, the spinors are sections of a
Spin
c
bundle
W^{+}
, which can be informally written as
S^{+}\otimes\mathcal{L}^{1/2}
with
c_{1}(\mathcal{L})=c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})
. The index of
\mathcal{D}_{A}
splits,

{\rm Index}(\mathcal{D}_{A})={\rm Index}(\delta_{A})+{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{A})
.
As before, we have

{\rm Index}(\delta_{A})=-{\rm Index}(\delta_{A}^{\dagger})=-\frac{1}{4}(\chi+\sigma),

(128)

since the Chern character of the associated bundle
{\rm ch}(\mathcal{E}^{\lor}\otimes\mathcal{E})
equals 1. Furthermore, for the index of the Dirac
operator coupled to the Spin
c
bundle, the Chern character is

{\rm ch}(\mathcal{A})=e^{c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})/2}
. As a result, the index is

{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{A})=\frac{c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}-\sigma}{8}.

(129)

Adding the two contributions and multiplying by two, we find that the virtual
real (expected) dimension of the moduli space of solutions to the monopole equations is
[
4
]

{\rm vdim}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm m}_{\mathfrak{s}})=\frac{1}{4}\,(c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}-(2\chi+3\sigma))\in 2\mathbb{Z}.

(130)

Since
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})
is a characteristic vector for
L
,

c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}=\sigma\mod 8
, and
(2\chi+2\sigma)/4=1-b_{1}+b_{2}^{+}

rhs is indeed an integer if
X
is smooth and compact.
Taking
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})=c_{\rm uv}
we define

\ell:=\frac{1}{8}\left(c_{\rm uv}^{2}-2\chi-3\sigma\right)\in\mathbb{Z}.

(131)

The integer
\ell
will play an important role in many formulae below.

Non-abelian monopole equations

The non-abelian monopole equations are the
Q
-fixed equations of the

\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory, and the generalization of the
monopole equations (
102
) to an arbitrary gauge group
G
. We
are interested in
G=SO(3)
. As for the abelian monopole equations,
the deformation operator is a combination of two operators,

\mathcal{D}_{A}=(\delta_{A},{\rm\bf D}_{A})
. The contribution of
\delta_{A}
is
-1
times
(
126
). For the index of
{\rm\bf D}_{A}
,
{\rm ch}(\mathcal{P}_{r})

in the index formula (
124
) is given by
e^{c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})/2}\,{\rm ch}(\mathcal{A})
. This index then reads

{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{A})=-2\,h^{\lor}_{G}\,k(P)+{\rm dim}(G)\,\frac{c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}-\sigma}{8}.

(132)

Thus the two dimensions added together give for the virtual real dimension of
the moduli space of non-Abelian monopole equations
\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}
,

{\rm vdim}(\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}})=\dim(G)\,\frac{c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}-(2\chi+3\sigma)}{4}.

(133)

Interestingly, this is independent of the instanton number
k
.
This is closely related to the fact that the correlation functions
of any fixed topological operator
\mathcal{O}(x)
with
x\in H_{*}(X)

are infinite series in
q_{\rm uv}
. If
b_{1}=0
,

c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}
needs to be sufficiently positive for the

{\dim}(\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}})\geq 0
. We deduce moreover that if
\mathfrak{s}

corresponds to an almost complex structure,
{\dim}(\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}})=0
, and the rank of the obstruction bundle equals the
dimension of the instanton moduli space (
127
). Our
main interest is in gauge groups with
\dim(G)=3={\rm odd}
, such that

{\dim}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}})=1+b_{2}^{+}\mod 2
. Correlation
functions are therefore only non-vanishing for
b_{2}^{+}
odd,
since all observables have even ghost number for
b_{1}=0
.

Finally, we can consider
N_{f}
spinors in the fundamental
representation of

SO(3)
. Then
{\rm ch}(\mathcal{E})=2-k(P)
. The index of the Dirac
operator becomes in this case

[
81
,
82
]

-k+\frac{1}{4}(c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}-\sigma).

(134)

The dimension of the moduli space of anti-self dual connections
coupled to
N_{f}
spinors in the fundamental representation is thus

{\rm vdim}(\mathcal{M}^{{Q},N_{f}}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}})=2(4-N_{f})k+\frac{1}{2}(N_{f}\,c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})^{2}-3\chi-(3+N_{f})\sigma).

(135)

3.7
Localization of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
path integral

This final subsection discusses the geometric content of the topological correlators of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory. Before discussing the infinite dimensional path integral of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory, we first discuss a finite dimensional model, namely the Mathai-Quillen model.

Mathai-Quillen formalism

To understand the geometric content of the path integral, we review a few aspects of the Mathai-Quillen formalism

[
9
,
10
,
12
,
83
]
. Topologically twisted
\mathcal{N}=2

Yang-Mills theory is an infinite
dimensional analogue of the Mathai-Quillen formalism. Let
M
be a
compact oriented manifold of even dimension
d
, and
V
a real
oriented vector bundle of even rank
r\leq d
with
SO(r)
connection

A
, and
metric
g_{ab}
,
a,b=1,\dots,r
. Recall that the Euler class of
V

is given by

{\rm Eul}(V)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{r/2}}\,{\rm Pf}(F),

(136)

where
F
is the 2-form field strength of the connection
A
.

Let
s
be a generic section of
V
. The connection
A
defines a
linear operator for each point
p\in M
,

\nabla_{A}s(p):\quad T_{p}M\to V_{p}.

(137)

Let
E\to M
be the bundle with fiber
{\rm Ker}(\nabla_{A}s(p))
over
the point
p\in M
. Similarly, let
W\to M
be the bundle with fiber

{\rm Cok}(\nabla_{A}s(p))
over
p
.
We then have the exact sequence

0\longrightarrow E\longrightarrow TM\longrightarrow V\longrightarrow W\longrightarrow 0.

(138)

Let us recall a basic construction in supergeometry

[
84
,
85
,
86
]
. Given the
vector bundle
V\to M
. We denote by
\Pi V
the superspace,
where the coordinates of the fiber of
V
are considered odd. For

V=TM
, we let furthermore
\widehat{M}=\Pi TM
. We then have the
isomorphism

C^{\infty}(\widehat{M})\cong\Omega^{*}(M),

(139)

since the odd
variables
\psi^{i}
, are linear functions on the fibers of
TM
,

\psi^{i}(\partial_{x^{j}})=\delta^{i}_{j}
.

The MQ model has a topological symmetry
Q
, and a ghost number operator

U
. We introduce local coordinates
x^{i}
,
i=1,\dots,d
on
M
, and
fermionic partners
\psi^{i}
. The ghost numbers of
x^{i}
and
\psi^{i}

are respectively
0
and
1
. We introduce in addition the anti-ghost
multiplets for
a=1,\dots,r
, consisting of a fermion
\chi^{a}
(with ghost number -1) and
boson
H^{a}
(with ghost number 0).

The action of
Q
on the fields is

\begin{split}[Q,x^{j}]&=i\psi^{j},\\
[Q,\psi^{j}]&=0,\\
[Q,\chi^{a}]&=H^{a}-i\psi^{j}A_{jb}^{a}\chi^{b},\\
[Q,H^{a}]&=i\psi^{j}A_{jb}^{a}H^{b}-\tfrac{i}{2}\psi^{i}\psi^{j}F^{a}_{ijb}\chi^{b}.\\
\end{split}

(140)

Let

s:M\to\mathbb{R}^{r}
be a section of
V
. We consider the
Q
-exact “Lagrangian”

L=\frac{1}{2\hbar}g_{ab}H^{a}(H^{b}-is^{b})+\frac{1}{\hbar}g_{ab}\chi^{a}(\nabla_{A,i}x^{b})\psi^{i}-\frac{1}{2\hbar}F_{ijab}\psi^{i}\psi^{j}\chi^{a}\chi^{b}.

(141)

We define the partition function as

Z=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{(d+r)/2}}\int dx\,d\psi\,d\chi\,dH\,e^{-L}.

(142)

Since
L
is
Q
-exact,
Z
is independent of
s^{a}
and
g_{ab}
; it depends only on topological properties of

M
and
V
. For
s^{a}=0
, we find

Z=\int_{\Pi V}dx\,d\psi\,{\widehat{\rm Eul}}(V),

(143)

where

{\widehat{\rm Eul}}(V)=\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{d/2}}{\rm Pf}(F_{ij}\psi^{i}\psi^{j}).

(144)

Since the
\psi^{i}
are odd,
Z
vanishes for
r\neq d
. For
r=d
,
integration over the
\psi^{i}
gives
the Euler class (
136
),

Z=\int_{M}dx\,{{\rm Eul}}(V)=\chi(V),\qquad r=d.

(145)

For a non-vanishing section
s^{a}
,
Z
can also be evaluated and reduces to a weighted sum
of signs. Let
S(s^{a})\in M
be the collection of points where the
sections
s^{a}
vanish. This is also the on-shell
Q-
fixed locus of
the fields,
M_{Q}
.
Then,

Z=\sum_{x\in S(s)}\mathrm{sgn}(\mathrm{det}(\nabla_{A,i}s^{b})).

(146)

Equations (
143
) and (
146
) are well-known expressions for
\chi(V)
.
The equality is an example of the Thom isomorphism.

If we have more variables than equations,
r<d
, one can insert observables with total ghost number
d-r
to reach
a non-vanishing answer. Let
\iota:S(s)\hookrightarrow M
be the
inclusion of
S(s)
. If
\nabla_{A}s
has maximal rank for each point on
S(s)
, the
dimension of
S(s)
equals the dimension of
{\rm Ker}(\nabla_{A}s)=d-r
. For an observable
\mathcal{O}
, which corresponds
to the form
\omega_{\mathcal{O}}
on
M
with degree
d-r
, we then arrive at

\begin{split}Z&=\int_{M}\omega_{\mathcal{O}}\wedge{\rm Eul}(V)\\
&=\int_{S(s)}\iota^{*}(\omega_{\mathcal{O}}).\end{split}

(147)

More generally,
\nabla_{A}s(p)
is not necessarily surjective, and can have a non-trivial
cokernel
V_{p}/{\rm Im}(\nabla_{A}s(p))
. This gives rise to the Euler class
{\rm Eul}(W)
. For an observable
\mathcal{O}
, which corresponds
to the form
\omega_{\mathcal{O}}
on
M
, (
147
) is then modified to

\begin{split}Z&=\int_{M}\omega_{\mathcal{O}}\wedge{\rm Eul}(V)\\
&=\int_{S(s)}\iota^{*}(\omega_{\mathcal{O}})\wedge{\rm Eul}(W),\end{split}

(148)

where
W
is the cokernel bundle introduced above (
138
).
We can enhance this model by considering a model with the action of a
Lie group
G
. In that case, the Euler class becomes the equivariant
Euler class with respect to
G
.

\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
path integral

Let us first consider the path integral of the
N_{f}=0
theory with only a vector multiplet. The topological twisted vector multiplet is an infinite dimensional
version of the MQ formalism, where

d-r={\dim}_{\mathbb{R}}(\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}})
. The path integral reduces to an
integral over
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
. The Donaldson observables are

\begin{split}u_{0}&=\frac{1}{16\pi^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}[\phi^{2}],\\
I_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})&=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{\boldsymbol{x}}\mathrm{Tr}\left[\frac{1}{8}\psi\wedge\psi-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\phi\,F\right].\end{split}

(149)

where
{\boldsymbol{x}}\in H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Q})
. The first observable,
u_{0}
has ghost
number four, and corresponds to a four-form
\omega_{u_{0}}
on instanton
moduli
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
. Similarly, the integrand of the surface observable

I({\boldsymbol{x}})
has ghost number 2 and corresponds to a two-form
\omega_{{\boldsymbol{x}}}
on
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
. It is
natural to include these in exponentiated form

\left<e^{2p\,u_{0}/\Lambda_{0}^{2}+I_{0}({\boldsymbol{x}})/\Lambda_{0}}\right>.

(150)

Since
u_{0}
has ghost number 4, the fugacity
p
naturally has ghost
number
-4
. Similarly, we associate ghost number
-2
to

{\boldsymbol{x}}
. Donaldson polynomials of the pure
SU(2)
theory are homogenous polynomials with
monomials of the form
p^{s}\,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{t}
, satisfying

-4s-2t=-\mathrm{dim}(\mathcal{M}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}})
.

We consider next the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory, with the massive
hypermultiplet. The topological observables (
149
) are
closely related for this theory. While the surface operator is
essentially identical, Equation (
197
) shows that the

\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
analogue of Donaldson’s point observable
\mu(p)
is shifted,
u_{\rm D}=u+\frac{1}{8}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})m^{2}
. We will explain
this in more detail in Section
4.8
.
From the perspective of the MQ formalism, the adjoint hypermultiplet is
corresponds to a bundle over
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
together
with anti-ghost multiplet, leading to the insertion of the Euler
class of this bundle
[
8
]
. More precisely, the bundle is the index bundle
of the Dirac operator in the adjoint representation of
SO(3)
. The
hypermultiplet fields,
M_{\dot{\alpha}}
, give rise to an infinite number of
additional variables and equations, whose difference is the rank of the index
bundle (
132
).

The path integral of the UV theory, reduces to an integral over the
solution space of non-Abelian monopole equations
(
102
). From the perspective of the MQ formalism, these
equations correspond to the zero locus of sections over an ambient
space. It is most convenient to work to work with the fixed point locus of the
U(1)_{B}
symmetry. The monopole equations then correspond to
sections over the union
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\cup\mathcal{M}^{\rm a}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}
of the instanton
component and abelian component respectively. For
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
, the
hypermultiplet fields
M_{\dot{\alpha}}=0
vanish, whereas for
\mathcal{M}^{\rm a}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}

the gauge connection is reducible.

The equivariant parameter of the
U(1)_{B}
symmetry is the mass
m
. Since
m
has ghost number
2
,
correlation functions
will evaluate to sums of monomials of the form
m^{r}p^{s}\,{\boldsymbol{x}}^{t}
,
where
r
,
s
and
t
satisfy

2r-4s-2t=-\mathrm{vdim}(\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}),

(151)

with
\mathrm{vdim}(\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}})
given in
(
133
).

Let
{\rm Ind}({\rm\bf D}_{A}))
be the
index bundle of
{\rm\bf D}_{A}
. This is a formal difference
of bundles; it is an element of K-theory or a virtual bundle,
with rank
{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{A})
as in (
132
). We let
furthermore
W
be the bundle with fibers
{\rm Cok}({\rm\bf D}_{A}^{\dagger})
. This bundle
W
is the analogue of the bundle
W

introduced above (
148
) in the
Mathai-Quillen formalism. Correlation functions then evaluate to

\begin{split}\left<\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{p}\right>&=\sum_{k}q_{\rm uv}^{k}\,m^{-{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{A})}\int_{\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}}\,c_{\ell}(W)\,\omega_{1}\dots\omega_{p},\end{split}

(152)

with
{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{A})
as in (
132
).

We can express this as an integral over
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\cup\mathcal{M}^{\rm a}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}
.
The equivariant Euler class with respect to
U(1)_{B}
is given in terms of splitting classes

x_{i}
as
\prod_{i=1}^{{\rm rk}(E)}(x_{i}+m)
. We can express this in
terms of Chern classes
c_{\ell}
as

m^{-{\rm rk}(E)}\prod_{i=1}(x_{i}+m)=\sum_{\ell=0}^{{\rm rk}(E)}\frac{c_{\ell}}{m^{\ell}}.

(153)

Note that if
\mathfrak{s}
corresponds to an almost complex structure, the
index bundle
{\rm Ind}({\rm\bf D}_{A}^{\dagger})
(or obstruction bundle) is equal to the
tangent bundle of
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\cup\mathcal{M}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}^{\rm a}
. More generally, physical correlation functions evaluate to
intersection numbers of Chern classes and differential forms
corresponding to observables
[
8
, Equation (5.13)]
. In the
present case of
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
, we arrive at

\begin{split}\left<\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{p}\right>&=\sum_{k}q_{\rm uv}^{k}\,m^{-{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{A})}\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\cup\mathcal{M}^{\rm a}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}}\sum_{\ell\geq 0}\frac{c_{\ell}}{m^{\ell}}\,\omega_{1}\dots\omega_{p}\\
&=m^{-3\ell+D_{\omega}}\sum_{k}q_{\rm uv}^{k}\\
&\quad\times\left[\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}}c_{\ell}\,\omega_{1}\dots\omega_{p}+\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\rm a}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}}c_{\ell}\,\omega_{1}\dots\omega_{p}\right],\end{split}

(154)

where the
\omega_{j}
are the differential forms on either
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
or
\mathcal{M}^{\rm a}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}
,
corresponding to the topological observables
\mathcal{O}_{j}
, and
D_{\omega}={\rm deg}(\omega_{1}\dots\omega_{p})
. The latter can also be identified as
the
U(1)_{R}
-charge of
\mathcal{O}_{1}\dots\mathcal{O}_{p}
. The factor

m^{-{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{A})}
is a consequence of integrating out
the massive hypermultiplet fields
[
66
]
. We will
find that the physical partition function of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory
is multiplied by a factor
\Lambda^{3\ell}
, where

\Lambda
is the scale.

We can similarly write down the correlation function of exponentiated
observables. For example, if
\mathcal{O}
is the point observable with mass
dimension 2, then

\begin{split}\left<e^{\mathcal{O}/\Lambda^{2}}\right>&=\sum_{k}\sum_{s=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\ell\geq 0}q_{\rm uv}^{k}\,m^{-{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{A})}\\
&\quad\times\left[\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}}\frac{1}{s!}\frac{c_{\ell}}{m^{\ell}\Lambda^{2s}}\,\omega_{\mathcal{O}}^{s}+\int_{\mathcal{M}^{\rm a}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}}}\frac{1}{s!}\frac{c_{\ell}}{m^{\ell}\Lambda^{2s}}\,\omega_{\mathcal{O}}^{s}\right].\end{split}

(155)

When the UV
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure does not correspond to an almost complex structure
the obstruction bundle will be different and the cohomology classes

c_{\ell}
in (
154
) will not be the Chern classes of the tangent bundle, but rather
those of a different bundle (namely, the obstruction bundle).

It is interesting to consider the asymptotics of these formulae for
m\to 0
and

m\to\infty
, respectively. Physically, the limit
m\to 0
corresponds to the
case of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM. When we choose the UV
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure to be an almost
complex structure the leading terms in the
m\to 0
limit are expected to be closely related to Vafa-Witten theory as discussed in Section
3.5
.
Indeed, for a fixed value of
k
in the sum in (
154
) the leading term will be the
integral over
\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k}
of the highest Chern class of
the tangent bundle thus, formally, producing the Euler character

\chi(\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}})
of instanton
moduli space. This leads to a conundrum, since the moduli space

\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}

changes by a bordism under metric deformations of
X
, while the
Euler character is not a bordism invariant. On the other hand, we
expect that the path integral is a topological invariant, at least for
four-manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}>1
. The envisioned resolution
is that the abelian solutions of (
102
) contribute
to the partition function, and that this contribution is also not
bordism invariant. However, the sum of the contributions is expected
to be a bordism invariant, such that a change of the Euler number

\chi(\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}})
due to a bordism, is exactly cancelled by a
change due to the abelian solutions.
9
9

9

We thank Richard Thomas for correspondence on
these aspects.

In addition to the limit
m\to 0
, there is the limit
m\to\infty

(with
q_{\rm uv}\to 0
such that
4m^{4}q_{\rm uv}=\Lambda_{0}^{4}
is held
fixed). This limit should physically reproduce pure
\mathcal{N}=2
SYM. In the twisted theory we therefore expect the leading
term in the
m\to\infty
limit to reproduce
the Donaldson invariants. Indeed equation (
154
) makes this clear: The leading
term in the
m\to\infty
asymptotics is given by the term with
\ell=0
: The
Chern-classes drop out and, for
any
choice of UV
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure
we should reproduce the Donaldson invariants.

Equation (
154
) ignores the singularities in the moduli space

\mathcal{M}^{\rm i}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
due to reducible connections.
is that the obstruction “bundle” (really, a sheaf over a stack),
i.e., the index “bundle,” cannot be identified
with
\ker({\rm\bf D}_{A}^{\dagger})
. Nevertheless, we would conjecture that when the
index in (
132
) is sufficiently negative that the obstruction bundle
E
is
a well-defined vector bundle which can be identified with the bundle of
fermion zeromodes with fiber
\ker({\rm\bf D}_{A}^{\dagger})
.

4
Path integral of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
effective IR theory

We formulate in the
u
-plane integral for the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory in this section. We start in Section
4.1
and
4.2
with the effective Lagrangian and its coupling to the Spin
c
structure. Section
4.3
deals with the analogue of an important non-trivial phase of Donaldson-Witten theory for the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory. After determining the sum over
U(1)
fluxes in Section
4.4
, we determine the
u-
plane integrand in Section
4.5
. We demonstrate that the integral is well-defined in Section
4.6
. We discuss moreover the limit to the
N_{f}=0
theory, inclusion of observables and regularization of the
u
-plane integral.

4.1
General twisted, effective Lagrangian

Having discussed the formulation of the UV theory, we turn to the low
energy effective theory. To couple the hypermultiplet to a
{\rm Spin}^{c}

structure, we consider first the low energy effective theory for a gauge group
with rank
r>1
, whose effective theory has gauge group

U(1)^{r}
. For four-manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}=1
, the
u
-plane integral reduces to an integral over zero
modes
[
5
]
. To write this Lagrangian, we let the indices
I,J,K
run
from 1 to
r
for lower indices, and
(1)
to
(r)
for upper indices. The bosonic zero-modes of the twisted effective theory are the scalars
a^{I}
and field strengths
F^{I}
(with positive/negative definite projections
F^{I}_{\pm}
). When
b_{1}=0
, the fermionic zero modes are the 0-forms
\eta^{I}
and self-dual 2-forms
\chi^{I}
. We have in addition the auxiliary fields
D^{I}
. Together with
\tau_{IJ}=\partial_{a^{I}}\partial_{a^{J}}\mathcal{F}
,
y_{IJ}=\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{IJ})
and
\mathcal{F}_{IJK}=\partial_{a^{I}}\tau_{JK}
, the zero mode
Lagrangian reads
[
87
]
10
10

10

This Lagrangian is
multiplied by two compared to
[
87
]
, since we work
here in the
\Gamma(2)
convention for the duality group.

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}&=\frac{i}{8\pi}\left(\bar{\tau}_{IJ}F_{+}^{I}\wedge F_{+}^{J}+\tau_{IJ}F_{-}^{I}\wedge F_{-}^{J}\right)-\frac{1}{4\pi}y_{IJ}D^{I}\wedge D^{J}\\
&\quad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{8\pi}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{IJK}\eta^{I}\chi^{J}\wedge(D+F_{+})^{K}.\end{split}

(156)

The action of the BRST symmetry
Q
on the zero modes is

\begin{split}&[Q,A^{I}]=\psi^{I}=0,\qquad\qquad[Q,\psi^{I}]=4\sqrt{2}\,da^{I},\\
&[Q,a^{I}]=0,\qquad\qquad\qquad[Q,\bar{a}^{I}]=\sqrt{2}i\,\eta^{I},\\
&[Q,\eta^{I}]=0,\qquad\qquad\qquad[Q,\chi^{I}]=i(F_{+}^{I}-D_{+}^{I}),\\
&[Q,D^{I}]=(d\psi^{I})_{+}=0.\end{split}

(157)

We can express
Q
in terms of the fields as

Q=\sqrt{2}i\eta^{I}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{a}^{I}}+i(F_{+}^{I}-D_{+}^{I})\frac{\partial}{\partial\chi^{I}}.

(158)

The Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}
can be written as a topological, holomorphic term plus a

Q
-exact term,

\mathcal{L}=\frac{i}{8\pi}\tau_{IJ}F^{I}\wedge F^{J}+\left\{Q,W\right\},

(159)

with

W=-\frac{i}{4\pi}y_{IJ}\chi^{I}(F_{+}^{J}+D^{J}).

(160)

This makes it manifest that
\{Q,\mathcal{L}\}=0
.

4.2
Coupling to a
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure in the effective theory

We model the effective theory of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory coupled to a
{\rm Spin}^{c}

structure as the effective theory of a theory with gauge group

SU(2)\times U(1)
. The hypermultiplet is charged under both factors
of the gauge group, and the fields of the vector multiplet associated
with the
U(1)
are “frozen” in a specific way as discussed below.
The Lagrangian of this theory is that in (
156
) with

r=2
. We identify the index “1”
with the unbroken
U(1)
of the
SU(2)
gauge group, and identify
the index “2” with the
U(1)
factor of the gauge group.
The mass
m
of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
hypermultiplet is the vev
of the scalar field of the
U(1)
vector multiplet,

\left<\phi^{(2)}\right>=a^{(2)}=m

[
23
]
. The field strength

F^{(2)}
provides the coupling of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
hypermultiplet to the

{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})
; we set
[F^{(2)}]=4\pi{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
with
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}=c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})/2\in L/2
. We will later also frequently use

c_{\rm uv}=2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
.

We determine the values
of the other fields from the BRST variations
(
157
). We set the fermionic fields to 0,

\eta^{(2)}=\chi^{(2)}=0
, such that the BRST variation of
\bar{a}^{(2)}
vanishes. Finally, to ensure that the
BRST variation of
\chi^{(2)}
vanishes, we set
D^{(2)}=F^{(2)}_{+}
. With this
prescription, the BRST operator
Q
(
158
) of the
U(1)\times U(1)
theory
reduces to the BRST operator of the
U(1)
theory,

Q=\sqrt{2}i\eta^{(1)}\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{a}^{(1)}}+i(F^{(1)}_{+}-D_{+}^{(1)})\frac{\partial}{\partial\chi^{(1)}}.

(161)

With the vanishing of the fermionic fields
\eta^{(2)}=\chi^{(2)}=0
, the Lagrangian (
156
) evaluates to

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}&=\frac{i}{8\pi}\tau_{IJ}F^{I}\wedge F^{J}+\left\{Q,W\right\}\\
&=\frac{i}{8\pi}\tau_{IJ}F^{I}\wedge F^{J}+\frac{1}{4\pi}y_{11}F_{+}^{(1)}\wedge F_{+}^{(1)}-\frac{1}{4\pi}y_{11}D^{(1)}\wedge D^{(1)}\\
&\quad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{8\pi}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{11J}\eta^{(1)}\chi^{(1)}\wedge(D+F_{+})^{J}+\frac{1}{4\pi}y_{12}(F_{+}^{(1)}-D^{(1)})\wedge(F_{+}^{(2)}+D^{(2)}),\end{split}

(162)

where as before
y_{IJ}=\mathrm{Im}(\tau)_{IJ}
. Substitution of
D^{(2)}=F^{(2)}_{+}
gives

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}&=\frac{i}{8\pi}\tau_{IJ}F^{I}\wedge F^{J}+\frac{1}{4\pi}y_{11}F_{+}^{(1)}\wedge F_{+}^{(1)}-\frac{1}{4\pi}y_{11}D^{(1)}\wedge D^{(1)}\\
&\quad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{8\pi}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{111}\eta^{(1)}\chi^{(1)}\wedge(D^{(1)}+F_{+}^{(1)})+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{4\pi}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{112}\eta^{(1)}\chi^{(1)}\wedge F_{+}^{(2)}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2\pi}y_{12}(F_{+}^{(1)}-D^{(1)})\wedge F_{+}^{(2)}.\end{split}

(163)

The integral over
D^{(1)}
is a standard Gaussian integral after analytic
continuation. The effective Lagrangian then becomes

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}&=\frac{i}{8\pi}\tau_{IJ}F^{I}\wedge F^{J}+\frac{1}{4\pi}y_{11}F_{+}^{(1)}\wedge F_{+}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{y_{12}^{2}}{y_{11}}\,F_{+}^{(2)}\wedge F_{+}^{(2)}+\frac{1}{2\pi}y_{12}F_{+}^{(1)}\wedge F_{+}^{(2)}\\
&\quad+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{8\pi}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{111}\eta^{(1)}\chi^{(1)}\wedge(F_{+}^{(1)}-\frac{y_{12}}{y_{11}}F_{+}^{(2)})+\frac{i\sqrt{2}}{4\pi}\bar{\mathcal{F}}_{112}\eta^{(1)}\chi^{(1)}\wedge F_{+}^{(2)}\\
&\quad+\log\!\left(\frac{\sqrt{y_{11}}}{2\pi i}\right),\end{split}

(164)

where the last term is due to the integral over
D^{(1)}
.
Integrating over the fermion zero modes, we arrive at

\begin{split}&\int d\eta^{(1)}\,d\chi^{(1)}\,e^{-\int_{X}\mathcal{L}}\\
&=\left[\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{a}_{1}}\left(\sqrt{2y_{11}}\,B(F^{(1)}+\frac{y_{12}}{y_{11}}F^{(2)},J)\right)\right]e^{-\int_{X}\mathcal{L}_{0}},\end{split}

(165)

where
\mathcal{L}_{0}
is now the first line of (
164
),

\begin{split}\mathcal{L}_{0}&=\frac{i}{8\pi}\tau_{IJ}F^{I}\wedge F^{J}+\frac{1}{4\pi}y_{11}F_{+}^{(1)}\wedge F_{+}^{(1)}+\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{y_{12}^{2}}{y_{11}}\,(F_{+}^{(2)})^{2}+\frac{1}{2\pi}y_{12}F_{+}^{(1)}\wedge F_{+}^{(2)}\\
&=\frac{i}{8\pi}\tau_{11}F^{(1)}_{-}\wedge F^{(1)}_{-}+\frac{i}{8\pi}\bar{\tau}_{11}F^{(1)}_{+}\wedge F^{(1)}_{+}+\frac{i}{4\pi}\tau_{12}F^{(1)}_{-}\wedge F^{(2)}_{-}\\
&\quad+\frac{i}{4\pi}\bar{\tau}_{12}F^{(1)}_{+}\wedge F^{(2)}_{+}+\frac{1}{4\pi}\frac{y_{12}^{2}}{y_{11}}\,(F_{+}^{(2)})^{2}+\frac{i}{8\pi}\tau_{22}F^{(2)}\wedge F^{(2)}.\end{split}

(166)

An important point in the discussion we have just given is the
importance of the role of
Q
-exact terms. If we just drop
the exact terms we will not produce convergent expressions,
nor will we obtain a single-valued measure on the Coulomb branch.
For example, dropping
Q
exact terms we might expect that the
action is holomorphic in
\tau_{11}
, but if we just keep that
term the resulting theta function will not make sense.
Similarly, the sum of the last two
terms in (
163
) is
Q
-exact,
[Q,\frac{1}{2\pi i}y_{12}\chi^{(1)}\wedge F_{+}^{(2)}]
.
Without this term, the exponentiated action is holomorphic in
\tau_{12}
, and single-valued
around the large
u
limit and around
u_{1}
. However, omitting
the term is incompatible with the monodromies around the
strong-coupling singularities
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
. This will become more
clear when we discuss the modular transformations in Section

4
. Similar remarks apply to the dependence on
\tau_{12}
in (
166
).
Dropping the
Q
-exact nonholomorphic dependence on
\tau_{12}
turns out to
lead to a measure which is not single-valued, at least when
F^{(2)}\neq 0
.
Standard reasoning would lead us to believe that the coupling of the Coulomb branch measure to the background
{\rm Spin}^{c}
-connection should be holomorphic
[
22
]
.
Our example serves as a cautionary tale that this
is not always the case. It is not completely clear that one can drop
Q
-exact terms
when the object that
Q
acts on is not globally well-behaved.
This important phenomenon deserves to be much better understood, since
dropping
Q
-exact expressions is a very commonly used maneuver.

4.3
Integrating out the massive modes

The massive modes are irrelevant on four-manifolds
with
b_{2}^{+}=1

[
5
]
. Even so, they
do give rise to an important interaction in the
u
-plane integrand
for
N_{f}=0
, namely a flux dependent sign
[
21
]
, which
is crucial for the integrand to be single valued on the
N_{f}=0

u
-plane.

We perform here the analogous analysis for the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}

theory following closely Section 4.4 of
[
21
]
.
All fields of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory are in the 3-dimensional, adjoint representation of

SU(2)
or
SO(3)
. One component is neutral, while the other two
components have charge
\pm 1
under the unbroken
U(1)
gauge group
on the
u
-plane. For a generic value of
u
, the charged components
become massive. In addition, the index of a two-dimensional
SU(2)_{R}

representation labels after twisting and coupling to a
{\rm Spin}^{c}

structure, the two components of
W^{+}\simeq S^{+}\otimes K_{X}^{-1/2}
. We can then label the fields of the vector
multiplet as
\psi^{\pm\pm}_{\alpha}
, where the first
\pm
are the
indices for
W^{+}
, and the second
\pm
labels the charge under the
unbroken
U(1)
gauge group.

As explained in Section 4.4 of
[
21
]
, there
are essentially two ways to write the measure for the massive
fermions, which differ by the sign. The
natural one in the UV theory is

(\mathcal{D}\psi)_{\rm UV}=\prod_{I=1}^{N_{++}}d\psi^{++}_{I}d\psi^{--}_{I}\prod_{J=1}^{N_{+-}}d\psi^{+-}_{J}d\psi^{-+}_{J}\prod_{K=1}^{\bar{N}_{++}}d\bar{\psi}^{++}_{K}d\bar{\psi}^{--}_{K}\prod_{L=1}^{\bar{N}_{+-}}d\bar{\psi}^{+-}_{L}d\bar{\psi}^{-+}_{L}.

(167)

In addition, there is a “positive definite” measure,

(\mathcal{D}\psi)_{+}=\prod_{I=1}^{N_{++}}d\psi^{++}_{I}d\psi^{--}_{I}\prod_{J=1}^{N_{+-}}d\psi^{-+}_{J}d\psi^{+-}_{J}\prod_{K=1}^{\bar{N}_{++}}d\bar{\psi}^{++}_{K}d\bar{\psi}^{--}_{K}\prod_{L=1}^{\bar{N}_{+-}}d\bar{\psi}^{-+}_{L}d\bar{\psi}^{+-}_{L}.

(168)

These two measures differ by a sign,

(\mathcal{D}\psi)_{\rm UV}=(-1)^{N_{+-}+\bar{N}_{+-}}(\mathcal{D}\psi)_{+}=(-1)^{N_{+-}-\bar{N}_{+-}}(\mathcal{D}\psi)_{+}.

(169)

While
N_{+\pm}
and
\bar{N}_{+\pm}
are individually infinite,
N_{+-}-\bar{N}_{+-}
is
finite and equal to the index of the Dirac operator
{\rm\bf D}_{+-}
acting on spinors
with charge
+-
. Thus, they are sections of
S^{+}\otimes K^{1/2}\otimes E
with
c_{1}(E)=\bar{w}_{2}(E)\mod 2H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. We
set
{\boldsymbol{k}}=c_{1}(E)/2={\boldsymbol{\mu}}\mod L
, with
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in L/2
. With (
124
), we
find for the index

\begin{split}{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D}_{+-})&=\frac{1}{2}(K_{X}/2+2{\boldsymbol{k}})^{2}-\sigma/8\\
&=\frac{1}{2}\left(B(K_{X},2{\boldsymbol{k}})+(2{\boldsymbol{k}})^{2}\right)+\frac{1}{8}(K_{X}^{2}-\sigma)\in\mathbb{Z}.\\
\end{split}

(170)

Since
K_{X}
is a lift
\bar{w}_{2}(X)
of
w_{2}(X)
,
(-1)^{N_{+-}-N_{-+}}
changes sign under a change of
{\boldsymbol{k}}
,
{\boldsymbol{k}}\to{\boldsymbol{k}}+{\boldsymbol{\ell}}
with
{\boldsymbol{\ell}}\in L
,
if and only if the phase
(-1)^{B(\bar{w}_{2}(X),{\boldsymbol{k}})}
changes sign as well. The integration over massive fermionic modes gives therefore rise to the insertion of
(-1)^{B(\bar{w}_{2}(X),{\boldsymbol{k}})}
in the sum over fluxes for the
N_{f}=0
theory
[
5
]
,
[
21
]
.

For the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory, we must also take into account the massive modes of the fermions of the hypermultiplet, which decomposes in two
\mathcal{N}=1
copies of each chirality. We weakly couple this multiplet to the additional “frozen”
U(1)
gauge group with flux
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})/2
for
\mathfrak{s}
a Spin
c
structure. The two
\mathcal{N}=1
copies
have opposite electric charge with respect to this
U(1)
. Thus we can write the fields as
\lambda^{\pm\pm}_{\alpha}
, where the
first
\pm
now corresponds to the charge under the “frozen”

U(1)
, and the second the charge under the unbroken
U(1)
of
SU(2)
or
SO(3)
. Thus the measure for the hypermultiplet fermions
\mathcal{D}(\lambda)_{\rm UV}
, which is completely analogous to equations (
167
) and (
168
), with
\psi
replaced by
\lambda
, gives rise to another sign
(-1)^{{\rm Index}({\rm\bf\tilde{D}}_{+-})}
, where
{\rm Index}({\rm\bf\tilde{D}}_{+-})
is as in (
170
), but with
K_{X}
replaced by
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})
. Multiplying the two signs for the two multiplets gives a
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-independent sign (for fixed
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
). If we choose
c_{1}(\mathfrak{s})=K_{X}
, the vector and hypermultiplet give rise to the same sign, such that the combined sign is +1.

Since the combined sign is
{\boldsymbol{k}}
-independent, there is no insertion of the type
(-1)^{B(\bar{w}_{2}(X),{\boldsymbol{k}})}
in the sum over fluxes for
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
in Section
4.4
. This is an important difference from the integrand for the
N_{f}=0
,
SU(2)
theory. It suggests there is a canonical orientation for the integral in equation (
2
). Interestingly, in the limit from

\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
to
N_{f}=0
the sign
(-1)^{B(\bar{w}_{2}(X),{\boldsymbol{k}})}
appears in an
unexpected way, namely due to the limit of the coupling
v
(
197
).

4.4
Sum over fluxes:
\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

We consider the
u
-plane integral for
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory with gauge group
SU(2)
and
fixed non-trivial ’t Hooft flux
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in L/2
. To evaluate the integrand, we
specialize (
166
) to
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
with the
U(1)
flavor symmetry
weakly gauged. We will set
F^{(1)}=4\pi{\boldsymbol{k}}
and
F^{(2)}=4\pi{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
, with
{\boldsymbol{k}}\in L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\in L/2
. We will find that
requiring that the integrand is single-valued will put further
constraints on
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
. We identify furthermore the couplings

\tau_{IJ}
of (
166
) with the couplings
\tau
,
v
and
\xi

of Section
2.3
. In particular,
y_{11}=y
,
\tau_{12}=v
, and

\tau_{22}=\xi=\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{F}}{\partial m^{2}}
.

Summing the fluxes
{\boldsymbol{k}}
in the rhs of (
165
) over
L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and multiplying by

\partial\bar{a}/\partial\bar{\tau}
, we arrive at the sum over fluxes

\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
,
11
11

11

This sum over fluxes is
very similar to those defined for
N_{f}=0
SW theory

[
5
,
24
]
. The arguments
\tau
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}

differ by a factor of 2 to match the
\Gamma(2)
convention, while the current
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
does not involve the sign
(-1)^{B({\boldsymbol{k}},K)}
. We will explain that this sign appears
in the limit from the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory to the SW theory with
N_{f}=0
.

\begin{split}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})&=e^{-4\pi y\,{\boldsymbol{b}}_{+}^{2}}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\left(\sqrt{4{y}}\,B({\boldsymbol{k}}+{\boldsymbol{b}},J)\right)\,q^{-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{-}^{2}}\,\bar{q}^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{+}^{2}}\\
&\quad\times\,e^{-4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{-},{\boldsymbol{z}})-4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{+},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})},\end{split}

(171)

where
{\boldsymbol{b}}=\mathrm{Im}({\boldsymbol{z}})/y
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}=v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
. When we include
observables in Section
4.8
, we will also use an argument
\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}
, which is not
the complex conjugate of
{\boldsymbol{z}}
. To include these cases, we define

{\boldsymbol{b}}=\frac{{\boldsymbol{z}}-\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}}{2i\,y}.

(172)

Using the (rescaled) error
function defined by Equation (
533
), we can write the summand of
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
as a total
derivative to
\bar{\tau}
,

\begin{split}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})&=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}E(\sqrt{4{y}}\,B({\boldsymbol{k}}+{\boldsymbol{b}},J))\,q^{-{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}\,e^{-4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}},{\boldsymbol{z}})}.\end{split}

(173)

This expression does not directly allow to write the integrand as a
total derivative of a well-defined function, since the sum is
divergent if
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}
is taken out. We will later
determine suitable anti-derivatives using mock modular forms in Section

6
.

We define more generally the theta series
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}[\mathcal{K}]
with kernel
\mathcal{K}
,

\begin{split}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}[\mathcal{K}](\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})&=e^{-4\pi y\,{\boldsymbol{b}}_{+}^{2}}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\mathcal{K}({\boldsymbol{k}})\,q^{-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{-}^{2}}\,\bar{q}^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{+}^{2}}\,e^{-4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{-},{\boldsymbol{z}})-4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{+},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})}.\end{split}

(174)

The function
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})
is an example of a non-holomorphic Jacobi form. We list various of its
properties in the following with
\tau
and
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in\mathbb{C}^{b_{2}}

as independent variables.

From the definition follows for
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in L/2
and
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in L
, that

\begin{split}\Psi_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})&=\Psi_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}),\\
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},-{\boldsymbol{z}},-\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})&=-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}),\end{split}

(175)

and in particular
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},0,0)=0
.

As function of
{\boldsymbol{z}}
,
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,{\boldsymbol{z}})
is quasi-periodic
under shifts by half-integer vectors in the lattice
L
. For
{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in L/2
, we have

\begin{split}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\nu}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\nu}})&=e^{-4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\nu}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})}\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}),\\
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\tau,{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\bar{\tau})&=q^{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{2}}\,e^{4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\nu}},{\boldsymbol{z}})}\,\Psi_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}).\end{split}

(176)

To determine the transformation properties of
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
, it is
useful to introduce a characteristic vector
K
of
L
. We find then
for the
\tau\mapsto\tau+1/2
and
\tau\mapsto-1/\tau
transformations:

\begin{split}&\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+K/2}(-1/\tau,-1/\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}}/\tau,\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}/\bar{\tau})=-i(-i\tau/2)^{b_{2}/2}(i\bar{\tau}/2)^{2}\,\exp(-2\pi i{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}/\tau)\\
&\qquad\qquad\times\Psi^{J}_{0}(\tau/4,\bar{\tau}/4,{\boldsymbol{z}}/2-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2-K/4,\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}/2-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2-K/4),\\
&\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+K/2}(\tau+1/2,\bar{\tau}+1/2,{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})=e^{\pi i({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{2}-K^{2}/4)}\,\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+K/2}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2,\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2),\end{split}

(177)

where the shift of
\tau
by
1/2
on the last line is a consequence of the
\Gamma(2)
convention.
Removing the dependence on
K
on the left-hand side by shifting
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, we have
for the two transformations,

\begin{split}\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\!\left(-1/\tau,-1/\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}}/\tau,\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}/\bar{\tau}\right)&=-i(-i\tau/2)^{b_{2}/2}(i\bar{\tau}/2)^{2}\,\exp\!\left(-2\pi i{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}/\tau\right)\\
&\quad\times\,\Psi^{J}_{0}\!\left(\tau/4,\bar{\tau}/4,{\boldsymbol{z}}/2-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2,\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}/2-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2\right),\\
&=-i(-i\tau/2)^{b_{2}/2}(i\bar{\tau})^{2}\,\exp\!\left(-2\pi i{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}/\tau\right)\\
&\quad\times\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in(L/2)/L}e^{4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\nu}},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})}\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}),\\
\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau+1/2,\bar{\tau}+1/2,{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})&=e^{\pi i({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{2}-B({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K))}\\
&\quad\times\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2-K/4,\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2-K/4).\end{split}

(178)

Furthermore, we have for the two generators of
\Gamma(2)
,

\begin{split}&\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1},\frac{\bar{\tau}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1},\frac{{\boldsymbol{z}}}{-2\tau+1},\frac{\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1}\right)=\\
&\qquad(-2\tau+1)^{b_{2}/2}(-2\bar{\tau}+1)^{2}\exp\!\left(\frac{4\pi i{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}}{-2\tau+1}\right)\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-K/2}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})\\
&\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\!\left(\tau+2,\bar{\tau}+2,{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}\right)=e^{2\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K)}\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\!\left(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}\right).\end{split}

(179)

4.5
Formulation of the
u
-plane integral

We proceed by formulating the
u
-plane integral for the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory. This question has been addressed before in
[
5
,
17
]
. As for the

N_{f}=0
theory, the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
path
integral reduces for
b_{2}^{+}=1
to an integral over the zero modes of
the vector multiplet. Besides the sum over fluxes
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
(
171
) and the topological couplings
A
,
B
(
35
), we also include the
coupling to a
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure,
C^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}
(
36
), which
was first put forward by Shapere and Tachikawa
[
22
]
.
Equation (
19
) gives
non-perturbative, modular expressions
for
du/da
and
\Delta_{\rm phys}
, and thus
A
and
B
, while
Equation (
60
) gives a closed
expression for
C
as function of
\tau
,
v
and
\tau_{\rm uv}
.

Combining all ingredients, we find that the
u
-plane integral reads

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}=K_{u}\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-4\ell-2\chi}\,\int_{\mathcal{B}}da\wedge d\bar{a}\,\frac{d\bar{\tau}}{d\bar{a}}\,A^{\chi}\,B^{\sigma}\,C^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\,\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}),

(180)

where
\ell
is as in (
131
) and we included the power of
\eta
due to (
42
). Note that if
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
corresponds to an
almost complex structure,
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}=(2\chi+3\sigma)/4
, the
power of
\eta
simplifies to
\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-2\chi}
. The
factor
d\bar{\tau}/d\bar{a}
has arisen from the integration over
fermion zero modes of the vector multiplet for the unbroken
U(1)

gauge group. It carries
R
-charge 2. The leading behavior of
A
,
B

and
C
can be understood from the local
R
symmetry
[
21
,
22
]
. We
will comment on this in Section
5
.

The three parameters
(K_{u},\alpha,\beta)
in (
180
) are
normalization factors. We set

K_{u}=-\frac{i\,\kappa}{\pi\,\Lambda}

(181)

with
\kappa
a numerical factor, which we fix in Section

6.2
by comparison with mathematical results for Euler numbers of instanton moduli spaces. Since

\chi+\sigma=4
, there is an ambiguity
[
88
]

(K_{u},\alpha,\beta)\sim(\zeta^{-4}K_{u},\zeta\,\alpha,\zeta\,\beta).

The integration domain
\mathcal{B}
in (
180
) is the Coulomb branch for the variables
a
and
\bar{a}
.
A more rigorously defined integration domain is obtained by changing
coordinates to
\tau
. We let
\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{\rm uv})\gg 1
, and remove
an epsilon disk
B(\tau_{\rm uv},\varepsilon)
around
\tau_{\rm uv}
from the fundamental domain

\mathbb{H}/\Gamma(2)
. The Coulomb branch takes in these variables
the form of a “punctured” modular curve

\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}=(\mathbb{H}/\Gamma(2))\backslash B(\tau_{\rm uv},\varepsilon).

(182)

The fundamental domain
\mathbb{H}/\Gamma(2)
consists of six images
of elements of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})/\Gamma(2)

of the key hole fundamental domain
\mathcal{F}
for

SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. An example is the union of
\mathcal{F}
,

T\mathcal{F}
,
S\mathcal{F}
,
TST\mathcal{F}
,
STS\mathcal{F}
and

TS\mathcal{F}
. We have displayed the domain and
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
in Figure
2
.

Figure 2:
Fundamental domain
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
for
\tau
: the fundamental domain

\mathbb{H}/\Gamma(2)
with the

\varepsilon
-neighborhood of
\tau_{\rm uv}
,
B(\tau_{\rm uv},\varepsilon)
, excluded.

The integral
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
now reads

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}}d\tau\wedge d\bar{\tau}\,\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)\,\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}),

(183)

where we defined the measure
\nu
,

\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)=K_{u}\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-4\ell-2\chi}\,\frac{da}{d\tau}\,A^{\chi}\,B^{\sigma}\,C^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}.

(184)

We express
\nu
more explicitly using (
35
), (
39
), (
42
) and Matone’s relation
[
89
]
for
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory,

\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{du}{d\tau}=\frac{8}{m^{2}}\,\Delta_{\rm phys}\left(\frac{da}{du}\right)^{2}.

(185)

This gives,

\begin{split}\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)&=\frac{16\pi i\,K_{u}\,\alpha^{\chi}\,\beta^{\sigma}}{m^{2}}\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-4\ell-2\chi}\,\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{\frac{\chi}{2}-3}\,\Delta_{\rm phys}^{\frac{\sigma}{8}+1}\,C^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\\
&=\frac{\kappa\,m^{\frac{\sigma}{8}-2}}{4\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2\chi+4\ell}}\left(\frac{4}{\Lambda}\right)^{\frac{3}{8}(2\chi+3\sigma)}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{\frac{\chi}{2}-3}\,\Delta_{\rm phys}^{\frac{\sigma}{8}+1}\,C^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}.\end{split}

(186)

4.6
Single-valuedness of the
u
-plane integrand

In the following subsections, we discuss the single-valuedness of the
integrand under monodromies around the four singularities of the Coulomb branch.

4.6.1
\infty

For the monodromy around
u=\infty
, we have
a\to e^{\pi i}a
and
v\to e^{-\pi i}v
. The different ingredients of the integrand transform as follows:

\begin{split}&\frac{du}{da}\mapsto e^{\pi i}\,\frac{du}{da},\\
&\Delta_{\rm phys}\mapsto e^{6\pi i}\,\Delta_{\rm phys},\\
&C\mapsto e^{-\pi i}\,C.\end{split}

(187)

The sum over fluxes changes by a sign (
175
).

The integrand of
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
therefore changes by a phase

e^{\pi i\,\chi/2+6\pi i\,\sigma/8-\pi i\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}.

(188)

For
b_{2}^{+}=1
, we have
\chi+\sigma=4
, such
that this phase equals

e^{2\pi i\,\sigma/8-\pi i\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}.

(189)

Therefore, if we set
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\in K/2+L
, with
K
a characteristic
vector of
L
(
78
), then (
189
) equals 1. We thus find that
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}

must correspond to a
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure, for the
u
-plane integral to be single-valued with respect to monodromies around
\infty
. We will demonstrate that this requirement also implies single-valuedness with respect to the monodromies around
u_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
.

4.6.2
u_{1}

For the monodromy around
u_{1}=\frac{m^{2}}{4}e_{1}
(or
\tau\to i\infty
), we have using
(
48
) and (
56
)

\begin{split}&\tau\mapsto\tau+2,\\
&v\mapsto v-1,\\
&\frac{du}{da}\mapsto\frac{du}{da},\\
&\Delta_{\rm phys}\mapsto e^{2\pi i}\,\Delta_{\rm phys},\\
&C\mapsto e^{-\pi i}C.\end{split}

(190)

For the sum over fluxes, we have using (
179
) and (
176
)

\begin{split}\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau+2,\bar{\tau}+2,{\boldsymbol{z}}-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})&=e^{2\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K)}\,\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}}-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})\\
&=e^{2\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K+2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})}\,\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}).\end{split}

(191)

The integrand is therefore multiplied by the following phase

e^{2\pi i\,\sigma/8-\pi i\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}+2\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K+2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})}.

(192)

Therefore for
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\in K/2+L
, the integrand is single-valued for the monodromy around
u_{1}
.
This condition on
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
is compatible with the condition found for the monodromy around
\infty
.

4.6.3
u_{2}

For the monodromy around
u_{2}=\frac{m^{2}}{4}e_{2}
, we have using
(
50
) and (
57
)

\begin{split}&\tau\mapsto\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1},\\
&v\mapsto\frac{v-\tau}{-2\tau+1},\\
&\frac{du}{da}\mapsto(-2\tau+1)^{-1}\,\frac{du}{da},\\
&\Delta_{\rm phys}\mapsto e^{2\pi i}\,\Delta_{\rm phys},\\
&C\mapsto e^{-\pi i+2\pi iv-2\pi i(v-\tau)(2v-1)/(-2\tau+1)}\,C.\end{split}

(193)

Finally we need to determine the transformation of

\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.
We have

\begin{split}&\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1},\frac{\bar{\tau}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1},\frac{v-\tau}{-2\tau+1}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\frac{\bar{v}-\bar{\tau}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\right)\\
&=\exp\!\left(2\pi i\left(\frac{\tau\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}-2\,v\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}{-2\tau+1}\right)\right)\,\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1},\frac{\bar{\tau}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1},\frac{v}{-2\tau+1}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\frac{\bar{v}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\right)\\
&=(-2\tau+1)^{b_{2}/2}\,(-2\bar{\tau}+1)^{2}\,\exp\!\left(2\pi i\frac{(\tau-2\,v+2\,v^{2})\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}{-2\tau+1}\right)\\
&\quad\times\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}-K/2}\!\left(\tau,\bar{\tau},v\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\right),\end{split}

(194)

where we used (
176
) for the first equality sign, and
(
179
) for the second.

The weights of the various functions and

d\tau\wedge d\bar{\tau}
cancel among each other. Moreover, the terms
involving
v
cancel beautifully between
C^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}
and
\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.
The remaining phase is

e^{2\pi i\sigma/8-\pi i{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}

(195)

which evaluates to
1
for
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\in K/2+L
. Finally, for this
choice of
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
,
\Psi_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}-K/2}^{J}=\Psi_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{J}
. We
thus conclude that the integrand is single-valued under the monodromy
around
u_{2}
if
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
represents a
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure.

4.6.4
u_{3}

This monodromy must be single-valued too, since

M_{1}\,M_{2}\,M_{3}=M_{\infty}
. As a check, we determine the various
transformations using (
52
) and (
58
)

\begin{split}&\tau\mapsto\frac{-\tau+2}{-2\tau+3},\\
&v\mapsto\frac{v-\tau+1}{-2\tau+3},\\
&\frac{du}{da}\mapsto(-2\tau+3)^{-1}\,\frac{du}{da},\\
&\Delta_{\rm phys}\mapsto e^{2\pi i}\,\Delta_{\rm phys},\\
&C\mapsto e^{-\pi i-2\pi i(\tau-1-2v+v^{2})/(-2\tau+3)}C.\end{split}

(196)

4.7
The limit to
N_{f}=0

Note the sum over fluxes
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})
appears at first sight to differ from the theory with

N_{f}=0
, since there is no insertion
(-1)^{B({\boldsymbol{k}},K)}
.
However, (
197
) demonstrates that this will appear in the limit.

The non-perturbative contributions to
v
and
\xi
vanish in
the limit to the pure SW theory. Equation (
25
) shows that their
perturbative parts evaluates to constants. From (
60
), one obtains for the three couplings

\tau\mapsto\tau,\qquad v=-\frac{1}{2}+O(q_{\rm uv}),\qquad\xi\mapsto-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{3}{2}\log(\Lambda/m)+O(q_{\rm uv}),

(197)

One of the striking effects of this limit is that the non-holomorphic, non-topological coupling

e^{-4\pi ivB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{-},{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})-4\pi i\bar{v}B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{+},{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})}

(198)

in (
171
) and (
180
), becomes holomorphic and
topological,

e^{-2\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},{\boldsymbol{k}})}.

(199)

This phase is crucial in the sum over fluxes of the effective theory
of pure SW theory.

The topological couplings for the
N_{f}=0
theory,
A_{0}
and
B_{0}
are defined as in (
35
), with for
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}

[
49
]

\alpha_{0}=\Lambda^{-1/2},\qquad\beta_{0}=\sqrt{2}\,\Lambda^{-1/2}.

(200)

From (
35
) and (
39
), we deduce that in the
limit to pure
SU(2)
the gravitational couplings
A
,
B
and
C
become

A\to A_{0},\qquad B\to\left(\frac{m}{\Lambda}\right)^{3/8}e^{-\pi i/8}B_{0},\qquad C\to\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{3/2}e^{\pi i/2}.

(201)

The measure
\nu_{0}(\tau)
of the
u
-plane integral for pure SW-theory is

\nu_{0}(\tau)=-\frac{i}{4}\frac{\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)^{13-b_{2}}}{\eta(2\tau)^{9}},

(202)

while the sum over fluxes reads
12
12

12

Both
\nu_{0}

and
\Psi^{J}_{{\rm SW},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
differ by an overall factor 2 and a
factor 2 in the argument from the quantities in
[
24
]

(respectively Equation (4.6) and Equation (4.9/10)). These factors
are due to the
\Gamma_{0}(4)
convention in
[
24
]
and
the
\Gamma(2)
convention in this paper. The overall factors of 2
take into account the change in the measure
d\tau\wedge d\bar{\tau}
.

\Psi^{J}_{0,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\left(\sqrt{4y}B({\boldsymbol{k}},J)\right)e^{\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}},K_{0})}q^{-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{-}^{2}}\bar{q}^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{+}^{2}},

(203)

with
K_{0}
a characteristic vector of
L
, and therefore
K_{0}\in\bar{w}_{2}(X)+2L
. Two different choices
for the characteristic vector,
K_{0}
and
\tilde{K}_{0}
, change

\Psi^{J}_{0,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
by a sign,
(-1)^{B({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K_{0}-\tilde{K}_{0})}
. This
dependence on the lift of
w_{2}(X)
, can be compensated by an
additional sign in
\Psi^{J}_{0,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}

[
5
]
,

e^{\frac{i\pi}{2}((2{\boldsymbol{\mu}})^{2}-B(2{\boldsymbol{\mu}},K_{0}))},

(204)

with
2{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
a lift of
w_{2}(E)
to
L
as before.

13
13

13

Since
K_{0}
is characteristic this factor is a sign. Note that under a shift
K_{0}\to K_{0}+2\beta
with
\beta
an integral class in
L
, the sign changes by
(-1)^{w_{2}(E)\cdot\beta}
. On the other hand, under a shift
2{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\to 2{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+2\beta
the sign changes by
(-1)^{\beta^{2}}
. The phase
(
204
) can be interpreted as a topological term in the action. One cannot define a partition function that depends on just
w_{2}(E)
and
w_{2}(X)
. One must choose a lifting of one of the two classes. This can be interpreted in terms of mixed anomalies and five-dimensional anomaly inflow
[
90
,
91
]
.

With (
204
),
\Psi^{J}_{0,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
is independent of
K_{0}
,
and the change of sign, due to a different choice of
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
,
corresponds to a change of orientation of
instanton moduli space discussed in
[
62
, Section 7.1.6]
.

The
N_{f}=0
limit of
\nu
is

\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)\sim i\,\kappa\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-4\ell-2\chi}\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}-\frac{\sigma}{4})}\,e^{\pi i({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}/2-\sigma/8)}\,\nu_{0}(\tau).

(205)

Moreover, the
N_{f}=0
limit of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
sum over fluxes is

\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})\to(-1)^{B({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K_{0}-2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})}\,\Psi^{J}_{0,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\tau,\bar{\tau}).

(206)

Put differently, the
N_{f}=0
limit leads to a canonical choice of

K_{0}=2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}=c_{\rm uv}
.
Combining the factors, we find that for the
N_{f}=0
limit,
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
and
Z_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

more generally, should be
multiplied by

R_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{X}(K_{0},2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})=e^{-\pi i(\chi+\sigma)/8}\,(-1)^{B({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K_{0}-2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})+({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}/2-\sigma/8)}\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2\chi+4\ell}\times\left(\frac{m}{\Lambda}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}-\frac{\sigma}{4})},

(207)

where we have replaced
-i
by
e^{-\pi i(\chi+\sigma)/8}
, such that
this formula can be applied to study this limit for generic manifolds
of simple type in Section
7.5
. We have also set
\kappa=1
,
which will follow from explicit calculation in Section
6.2
.

For later reference, we define the dimensionless partition function
\underline{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
,

\underline{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})=R_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{X}(K_{0},2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})\,\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}).

(208)

The leading term of
\underline{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
is a constant
\sim q_{\rm uv}^{0}
, since 0 is the smallest instanton number.

4.8
Observables

The
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory gives rise to correlation functions of
non-trivial observables. The observables are proportional to the mass

m
, and vanish in the massless limit.

The point observable

It is straightforward to include the point observable, which
corresponds to the order parameter
u
on the Coulomb
branch. Mathematically, this observable corresponds to a 4-form

\mu_{D}(p)
on the instanton moduli space, where
\mu_{D}:H_{i}(X,\mathbb{Q})\to H^{4-i}(\mathcal{M}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}},\mathbb{Q})
is Donaldson’s

\mu
-map.
Since the limit to pure SW theory (
197
) involves a shift,
the appropriate
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
observable is

u_{\rm D}=u+\frac{1}{8}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})m^{2}.

(209)

We determine the normalization by the limit to the pure theory.

e^{pu_{\rm D}/\Lambda^{2}}.

(210)

If
u
approaches the singularities
u_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
,
u_{\rm D}(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})
approaches

\begin{split}u_{{\rm D},1}&=\tfrac{3}{8}m^{2}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})=m^{2}\left(\tfrac{1}{4}+6\,q_{\rm uv}+O(q_{\rm uv}^{2})\right),\\
u_{{\rm D},2}&=\tfrac{1}{4}m^{2}e_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})+\tfrac{1}{8}m^{2}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})=-2\,m^{2}\,q_{\rm uv}^{1/2}+O(q_{\rm uv})\\
&=\Lambda_{0}^{2}\,(1+O(q_{\rm uv}^{1/2})),\\
u_{{\rm D},3}&=\tfrac{1}{4}m^{2}e_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})+\tfrac{1}{8}m^{2}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})=2\,m^{2}\,q_{\rm uv}^{1/2}+O(q_{\rm uv})\\
&=-\Lambda_{0}^{2}\,(1+O(q_{\rm uv}^{1/2})).\\
\end{split}

(211)

Thus
u_{{\rm D},1}
diverges in the limit to pure SW, while
u_{{\rm D},2}
and

u_{{\rm D},3}
approach the strong coupling singularities of the pure theory.

The surface observable

For
S\in H_{2}(X,\mathbb{Q})
, the surface observable reads

I_{-}(S)=\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}}\int_{S}{\rm Tr}\left[\frac{1}{8}\psi\wedge\psi-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\phi F\right].

(212)

The coupling flows in the infra-red to:

\tilde{I}_{-}(S)=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\pi}\int_{S}\left(\frac{1}{32}\frac{d^{2}u}{da^{2}}\psi\wedge\psi-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}\frac{du}{da}(F_{-}+D)\right).

(213)

Then we add to the effective action

e^{\tilde{I}_{-}(S)/\Lambda}.

(214)

The overall normalization is such that the leading power of
q
of the
Seiberg-Witten limit (
197
) of
\tilde{I}_{-}
is
q^{-1/4}B(S,F/4\pi)=q^{-1/4}B(S,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})
.

After reduction to zero modes and integrating over
D
, the insertion
of the surface operator
e^{\tilde{I}_{-}(S)}
in the effective action is
taken into account by adding an extra term to
v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
in (
180
),

{\boldsymbol{z}}_{S}=\frac{S}{4\pi\,\Lambda}\frac{du}{da},\qquad v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\mapsto v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}+{\boldsymbol{z}}_{S},\qquad\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\mapsto\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},

(215)

In this way, we can easily take the surface observable into account in
the sum over fluxes. This becomes

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}+{\boldsymbol{z}}_{S},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}),

(216)

with
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
as in (
171
). For the monodromy
M_{2}
, (
194
) is modified to
