\begin{split}&\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1},\frac{\bar{\tau}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1},\frac{(v-\tau){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}+{\boldsymbol{z}}_{S}}{-2\tau+1},\frac{(\bar{v}-\bar{\tau}){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1}\right)\\
&=\exp\!\left(2\pi i\frac{(\tau-2\,v+2\,v^{2})\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}+(4v-2)B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},{\boldsymbol{z}}_{S})+2{\boldsymbol{z}}_{S}^{2}}{-2\tau+1}\right)\\
&\quad\times(-2\tau+1)^{b_{2}/2}\,(-2\bar{\tau}+1)^{2}\,\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\!\left(\tau,\bar{\tau},v\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}+{\boldsymbol{z}}_{S},\bar{v}\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}\right).\end{split}

(217)

Contact term
T_{2}

Since
{\boldsymbol{z}}_{S}\sim S
appears in the exponential prefactor, we deduce that
single valuedness of the integrand requires the presence of contact
terms in the effective action
[
8
,
5
,
87
]
. We denote the term for
S^{2}
is by

e^{T_{2}\,S^{2}}
, and for
B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},{\boldsymbol{z}})
by

e^{T_{3}\,B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},{\boldsymbol{z}})}
. We discuss first
T_{2}
, which is given by

T_{2}(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})=\frac{1}{2\pi i\Lambda^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(a,m,\tau_{\rm uv})}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}^{2}},

(218)

where the derivative to
\tau_{\rm uv}
is for fixed
a
and
m
.
We can express this function in terms of
u
and
du/da

[
92
]
,

\begin{split}T_{2}(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})&=\frac{1}{24\Lambda^{2}}\left(-\frac{E_{2}(\tau)}{2}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{2}+2\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,u+\frac{m^{2}}{6}E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})+\frac{m^{2}}{\pi i}\,\frac{dE_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{d\tau_{\rm uv}}\right)\\
&=\frac{1}{24\Lambda^{2}}\left(-\frac{E_{2}(\tau)}{2}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{2}+2\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,u+\frac{m^{2}}{6}\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}\,\right),\end{split}

(219)

where we used (
530
) for the second identity.

We can derive the modular properties also from (
218
). Using
(
43
), we can write
T_{2}
as a derivative of
u
,

\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(a,m,\tau_{\rm uv})}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}^{2}}=\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}u+\tfrac{m^{2}}{12}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right)\right|_{a,m},

(220)

where we consider
u
as a function of
a
,
m
and
\tau_{\rm uv}
. We
next change variables from
a
to
\tau
for
u
, such that

\partial^{2}_{\tau_{\rm uv}}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}
becomes

\begin{split}\frac{\partial\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(a,m,\tau_{\rm uv})}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}^{2}}=&\left.\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}}\left(\tfrac{1}{2}u(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)+\tfrac{m^{2}}{12}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right)\right|_{\tau,m}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\left.\frac{\partial u(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)}{\partial\tau}\right|_{\tau_{\rm uv},m}\,\left.\frac{\partial\tau(a,\tau_{\rm uv},m)}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}}\right|_{a,m}.\end{split}

(221)

Using that the last term equals
\partial\tau/\partial\tau_{\rm uv}=\frac{1}{2}\partial^{2}u/\partial a^{2}
, we can determine the
transformations as in Section
2.7
. This gives

M_{2}:\qquad\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}^{2}}\mapsto\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}^{2}}+\frac{1}{2(-2\tau+1)}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{2},

(222)

where we used
\partial_{a}\mapsto 1/(-2\tau+1)\partial_{a}

(
50
).

We have moreover for
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
transformations of
\tau_{\rm uv}
,

\begin{split}T_{2}\!\left(\tau,\frac{a\tau_{\rm uv}+b}{c\tau_{\rm uv}+d}\right)&=(c\tau_{\rm uv}+d)^{4}\,\left(T_{2}(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})+\frac{1}{2\pi i\,\Lambda^{2}}\frac{c}{c\tau_{\rm uv}+d}\,\left(u+\frac{m^{2}}{6}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right)\right.\\
&\qquad\left.-\frac{m^{2}}{4}\frac{c^{2}}{(c\tau_{\rm uv}+d)^{2}}\right).\end{split}

(223)

where we used (
30
).

The last aspect we mention here is the limit of
T_{2}
to the
N_{f}=0
theory. Denoting this limit by
T_{2,0}
, we find

T_{2,0}(\tau)=\frac{1}{24\Lambda_{0}^{2}}\left(u_{0}-E_{2}(\tau)\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{2}_{0}\right),

where
u_{0}
is as in Equation (
12
).

Contact term
T_{3}

Due to the coupling
B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},{\boldsymbol{z}})
, there must be a third contact
term. We will demonstrate that this contact term is given by

T_{3}(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})=-\frac{2i}{\Lambda}\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}\partial m},

(224)

where the derivatives act for fixed
a
. Again using
(
30
), we can write
T_{3}
as a derivative of
u
to
m
for fixed
a
.
Changing variables from
a
to
\tau
,
we can express
\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}/\partial\tau_{\rm uv}\partial m
as

\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}(a,m,\tau_{\rm uv})}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}\partial m}=\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{m}(u+\tfrac{m^{2}}{6}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv}))|_{\tau,\tau_{\rm uv}}+\tfrac{1}{2}\partial_{\tau}u|_{\tau_{\rm uv},m}\,\partial_{m}\tau|_{a,\tau_{\rm uv}}

(225)

Using that
\partial_{m}\tau=\partial^{2}m_{D}/\partial a^{2}
, we can use
the transformations of
m_{D}
and
a
to derive the transformations of

T_{3}
. We then find using
v=\partial_{a}m_{D}
,

\begin{split}&M_{\infty},M_{1}:\qquad\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}\partial m}\mapsto\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}\partial m},\\
&M_{2}\qquad\,:\qquad\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}\partial m}\mapsto\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}}{\partial\tau_{\rm uv}\partial m}+\frac{2v-1}{2(-2\tau+1)}\frac{du}{da}.\end{split}

(226)

These transformations are of the expected form to match the
transformation of the sum over fluxes (
217
).

To write
T_{3}
more explicitly, we use

\left.\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial m}\right|_{a,\tau_{\rm uv}}=-\frac{a}{m}\left.\frac{\partial\tau}{\partial a}\right|_{m,\tau_{\rm uv}}.

(227)

Moreover, we determine from (
32
) that

a=-m\frac{dv}{d\tau}.

(228)

We then arrive at

T_{3}(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})=-\frac{2i}{\Lambda}\left(\frac{u}{m}+\frac{m}{6}\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})+\frac{1}{2}\frac{dv}{d\tau}\,\frac{du}{da}\right).

(229)

Therefore, under an
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
transformation of
\tau_{\rm uv}
, we have

T_{3}\!\left(\tau,\frac{a\tau_{\rm uv}+b}{c\tau_{\rm uv}+d}\right)=(c\tau_{\rm uv}+d)^{2}\left(T_{3}(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})-\frac{2m}{\pi\Lambda}\frac{c}{c\tau_{\rm uv}+d}\right).

(230)

Finally, we consider the
N_{f}=0
limit. This limit of
T_{3,0}
in fact diverges,

\lim_{\mathcal{N}=2^{*}\to N_{f}=0}T_{3}=\infty.

(231)

On the other hand, the limit of
\partial^{2}\mathcal{F}/\partial\tau_{\rm uv}\partial m
vanishes.
Since the
N_{f}=0
limit of
T_{3}
is infinite, we define an
alternative contact term by replacing
u
with
u_{\rm D}
(
209
) in
(
229
), and subtract the term involving
E_{2}
. We have then

T_{{\rm D},3}=T_{3}+\frac{i\,m}{3\Lambda}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})-\frac{i\,m}{4\Lambda}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv}).

(232)

4.9
Regularization of the
u
-plane integral

The
u
-plane integrand often diverges near the singularities, which will become more clear from the discussion in Section
5.1
. Due to the divergences, it is necessary to reguralize the integral. To this end, we first compactify
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
by
introducing a cut-off
\mathrm{Im}(\tau)\leq Y
for the cusp
\tau=\tau_{1}\to i\infty
, and similarly for the local couplings
\tau_{2}
and
\tau_{3}
near the cusps
\tau=0
and
\tau=1
. We denote this space by

\mathcal{U}_{Y,\varepsilon}
. Then
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
is defined as

\begin{split}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}&=\lim_{Y\to\infty,\varepsilon\to 0}K_{u}\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-4\ell-2\chi}\,\alpha^{\chi}\beta^{\sigma}\\
&\qquad\times\int_{\mathcal{U}_{Y,\varepsilon}}d\tau\wedge d\bar{\tau}\,\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{\frac{\chi}{2}-1}\frac{du}{d\tau}\,\Delta_{\rm phys}^{\frac{\sigma}{8}}\,C^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\,\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}),\end{split}

(233)

assuming that the limits exist. This integral requires careful regularization, especially if
observables are included. We will assume that the
\tau
-dependent of the
integrand is a linear combination of the following terms near the
boundaries
u_{j}
and
\infty
,

\begin{split}&\text{For}\,\,u\to u_{j}:\qquad y^{s}q_{j}^{m}\bar{q}_{j}^{n},\qquad n\geq 0,m-n\in\mathbb{Z}/2,\\
&\text{For}\,\,u\to\infty:\qquad(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})^{m}(\bar{\tau}-\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})^{n},\qquad n\geq 0,m-n\in\mathbb{Z}.\end{split}

(234)

where
q_{j}=e^{2\pi i\tau_{j}}
with
\tau_{1}=\tau
,
\tau_{2}=-1/\tau
and

\tau_{3}=-1/(\tau-1)
. The analysis in Section
5
will
confirm that the expansions of the
u
-plane integrand are of the form in (
234
). For the cusps
u_{j}
, the ”familiar” regularization is then applicable
[
93
,
94
,
95
]
. For example for
u_{1}
, we have

\int^{Y}_{Y_{0}}dy\int_{-1/2}^{3/2}dx\,y^{s}q_{j}^{m}\bar{q}_{j}^{n}=2\delta_{m,n}\int^{Y}_{Y_{0}}y^{s}e^{-4\pi ym},

(235)

which is convergent for
Y\to\infty
for
n>0
, and for
s<-1
if
n=0
. Similarly for
u\to\infty
, we have

\int^{r_{0}}_{\varepsilon}r\,dr\int_{0}^{2\pi}d\varphi\,(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})^{m}(\bar{\tau}-\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})^{n}=2\pi\delta_{m,n}\int^{r_{0}}_{\varepsilon}dr\,r^{2m+1},

(236)

with
\tau-\tau_{\rm uv}=r\,e^{i\varphi}
. This is also convergent for

\varepsilon\to 0
for
n\geq 0
. With this regularization, we thus have a well-defined
u
-plane integral.

It is possible to introduce observables to the path integral, which violate the condition
n\geq 0
in
Equation (
234
) and which are therefore not included in Section
4.8
. An example is the anti-holomorphic surface observable
[
96
,
24
]

\tilde{I}_{+}(S)=-\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}\pi}\int_{S}\frac{1}{2}\frac{d^{2}\bar{u}}{d\bar{a}^{2}}\eta\chi+\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}\frac{d\bar{u}}{d\bar{a}}(F_{+}-D)

Such observables lead to a more severe divergence than the divergences
occuring if (
234
) is satisfied. We therefore had to
make the non-symmetric replacement of
v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
and
\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
in
(
215
). For the
N_{f}=0
theory,
such severe divergences were regularized in

[
97
]
. This regularization also
applies to the cusps
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
and
u_{3}
of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}

theory. We expect that such divergences for
u\to\infty
can be treated in
a similar way, but will leave this for future work.

4.10
Evaluation using Stokes’ Theorem

Having established the regularization of the integral, we proceed with outlining the evaluation using Stokes’ Theorem, which follows
[
96
,
88
,
24
]
. The
u
-plane integral
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}

is schematically of the form

\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\int_{\mathcal{B}}\omega.

(237)

We aim to express
\omega
manifestly as an exact form,

\omega=d\Omega
, with
\Omega
being a smooth 1-form on
\mathcal{B}
.
We can then evaluate
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
on the boundary
\partial\mathcal{B}

\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\int_{\partial\mathcal{B}}\Omega.

(238)

There is an ambiguity in the choice of
\Omega
, since for a closed one-form

\varphi
, replacing
\Omega
by
\Omega+\varphi
does not change the
integral. This is simply a choice of integration constant.
\varphi

can be written as a linear combination of closed, non-exact
one-forms. Since the Coulomb branch of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
is a four
punctured sphere, it has three independent one-cycles, and
consequently three independent closed, non-exact one-forms

\varphi_{j}
,
j=1,2,3
. One can
choose the following representatives

\begin{split}\varphi_{1}&=\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\,d\tau,\\
\varphi_{2}&=\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\,d\tau,\\
\varphi_{3}&=\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}}\,d\tau.\end{split}

(239)

The integrals of
\varphi_{j}
along the boundary arcs in
\partial\mathcal{B}

vanish. The integral of
\varphi_{1}
receives two non-vanishing,
opposite contributions from arcs around
u_{1}
and
u_{2}
. Similarly,
the non-vanishing contributions for
\varphi_{2}
are due to
u_{1}
and
u_{3}
, and for

\varphi_{3}
due to
u_{\infty}
and
u_{1}
.
Using the ambiguity in
\Omega
, one can in principle “fix” the contribution from three out of
four cusps. On the other hand, we can require that the magnitude of
the anti-derivative near each of the boundaries of
\mathcal{B}
is of the same order
as that of the integrand. This fixes the anti-derivative uniquely, and
appears to be the most natural choice from the physical perspective.

Concretely, (
183
) demonstrates that
\omega

reads

d\tau\wedge d\bar{\tau}\,\nu\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}.

(240)

To express the integrand as an exact form, we write the
integrand
\nu\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
as a total deriviative using mock
modular forms,

\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})=\frac{d}{d\bar{\tau}}\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\tau_{\rm uv},m).

(241)

The 1-form
\Omega
from (
238
) then reads

\Omega=-d\tau\,\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\tau_{\rm uv},m),

(242)

and the orientation of
\partial\mathcal{B}
is such that “the interior of

\mathcal{B}
is on the lhs”. The integral can now be written as a sum of contributions from the
various boundary components of the Coulomb branch.
For
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
, the boundaries are a circle of radius
\varepsilon
around

\tau=\tau_{\rm uv}
, and the three arcs around the cusps of

\mathbb{H}/\Gamma(2)
, which we take at

\tau=0,2
and
i\infty
. Reversing the orientation of
\partial\mathcal{B}
,
(
183
) is expressed as

\begin{split}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}&=2[\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\tau_{\rm uv},m)]_{q^{0}}+2[\tau\mapsto-1/\tau]_{q^{0}}+2[\tau\mapsto 1-1/\tau]_{q^{0}}\\
&\quad+\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\oint_{|\tau-\tau_{\rm uv}|=\varepsilon}d\tau\,\widehat{\mathcal{H}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\tau_{\rm uv},m),\end{split}

(243)

with
\tau
circling counter-clockwise around an
\varepsilon
-disc centered at

\tau_{\rm uv}
.

5
Analysis of the
u
-plane integral

This section will analyze the
u
-plane integral. We start with determining the behavior of various quantities near the singularities in Section
5.1
, followed by derivations of
S
-duality, holomorphic anomaly, metric dependence and the effect of blowing-up a point on
X
on
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
.

5.1
Behavior near the boundaries

Since the
u
-plane integral can be expressed as a sum of boundary
contributions, it is important to analyze the behavior of the
couplings
A
,
B
,
C
,
v
and other quantities near
the boundaries.

5.1.1
u\to\infty

We first consider the limit
u\to\infty
or
\tau\to\tau_{\rm uv}
.
To avoid ambiguities with the phases of square roots, we assume that

\mathrm{Im}(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})>0
and
\mathrm{Re}(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})=0
.

The first terms for large
a
are

\begin{split}&\frac{da}{du}=\frac{1}{2a}+O(a^{-5}),\\
&u=a^{2}-\frac{m^{2}}{12}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})+O(a^{-2}),\\
&\Delta_{\rm phys}=a^{6}-\frac{m^{2}}{4}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,a^{4}+O(a^{2}),\\
&v=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left(\frac{m}{a}+\frac{m^{3}}{12a^{3}}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})+O(a^{-5})\right),\\
&C=\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{3/2}\,\left(\frac{m}{2a}+\frac{m^{3}}{16a^{3}}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})+O(a^{-5})\right).\\
\end{split}

(244)

Substitution of (
34
), with
s=-\pi i(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})
, gives for the different ingredients of the
u
-plane integrand

\begin{split}&\frac{da}{du}=\frac{1}{m}\,s^{1/2}\left(1-\frac{E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{4}\,s+O(s^{2})\right),\\
&u=\frac{m^{2}}{4}s^{-1}+\frac{m^{2}}{24}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})+O(s),\\
&\Delta_{\rm phys}=\left(\frac{m}{2}\right)^{6}s^{-3}\left(1+\frac{E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{2}\,s+O(s^{2})\right),\\
&v=\frac{1}{\pi i}\,s^{1/2}\left(1+\frac{E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{12}\,s+O\!\left(s^{2}\right)\right),\\
&C=\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{3/2}s^{1/2}\left(1+\frac{E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{4}\,s+O(s^{2})\right).\\
\end{split}

(245)

With these expansions, we are able to make a verification of the
selection rule (
151
). We find for the behavior of the
measure

\begin{split}\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)&=\frac{\kappa}{4\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2\chi+4\ell}}\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{3\ell}s^{\ell-\frac{3}{2}}\left(1+N(c_{\rm uv})\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,s+O(s^{2})\right),\end{split}

(246)

where we
used
\chi+\sigma=4
, and defined

\begin{split}\ell&=\frac{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}{2}-\frac{(2\chi+3\sigma)}{8}\in\mathbb{Z}\\
N(c_{\rm uv})&=\frac{1}{4}\left(\frac{\chi}{2}-3\right)+\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{\sigma}{8}+1\right)+\frac{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}{4}\\
&=\frac{1}{16}(4-\sigma+c_{\rm uv}^{2}).\end{split}

(247)

For four-manifolds with
b_{2}=b_{2}^{+}=1
,
\ell\geq-1
, while if

b_{2}>1
,
\ell
is unbounded. Moreover,
since
c_{\rm uv}=2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
, we have the identity

2\dim(G)\,\ell={\rm vdim}(\mathcal{M}^{Q}_{k,{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\mathfrak{s}})
(
133
),
such that we find agreement with the selection
rule (
151
). If
\mathfrak{s}
corresponds to an almost complex
structure,
\ell=0
and the dependence on
m/\Lambda
disappears.

The leading behavior
s^{\ell-3/2}
of
\nu
(
246
) can be understood from
the anomalous
U(1)_{R}
symmetry. Integration over the fermion zero
modes in the
u
-multiplet contributes
R
-charge 2 to the
measure. Together with the change of
variables from
(a,\bar{a})
to
(\tau,\bar{\tau})
, it gives rise to
the factor
da/d\tau\sim s^{-3/2}
. The masses of the two massive components of the
SU(2)

vector multiplet diverge for
u\to\infty
. After integrating out,
these components contribute each
(\chi+\sigma)/2
to the
R
-charge
of the measure
[
21
]
. In addition, the masses of two of the three components of the adjoint
hypermultiplet diverge for
u\to\infty
. These two components are charged under
U(1)_{B}
, such that their
R
-charge depends on
c_{\rm uv}
. Each component contributes
-2\,{\rm Index}({\rm\bf D})_{A}=(\sigma-c_{\rm uv}^{2})/4

(
129
) to the
R
-charge of the measure. The total
R
-charge of the measure is thus

2-4\ell
. Since the
R
-charge of
u
is 4, this gives for the leading behavior,

\nu\sim\frac{da}{d\tau}\,A^{\chi}\,B^{\sigma}\,C^{c_{\rm uv}^{2}/4}\sim s^{-3/2}\,u^{\frac{2\chi+3\sigma-c_{\rm uv}^{2}}{8}}\sim s^{\ell-3/2},

(248)

in agreement with (
246
).

Finally, we list the limits of the contact terms
T_{2,\infty}=\lim_{u\to\infty}T_{2}
and
T_{3,\infty}=\lim_{u\to\infty}T_{3}
,

\begin{split}&T_{2,\infty}(\tau_{\rm uv})=\frac{m^{2}}{288\,\Lambda^{2}}\left(E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}-E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right),\\
&T_{3,\infty}(\tau_{\rm uv})=-\frac{i\,m}{6\Lambda}\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv}).\end{split}

(249)

5.1.2
u\to u_{1}

This boundary corresponds to
\tau=\tau_{1}\to i\infty
.
We start by listing the different quantities of the integrand in the
limit
\tau_{1}\to i\infty
.

From (
19
), we have

\frac{du}{da}=m\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}+O(q_{1}^{1/2}),\\

(250)

where
q_{1}=e^{2\pi i\tau_{1}}
. For the leading term in the

q_{1}
-expansion of the discriminant, we find

\Delta_{\rm phys}=(2m)^{6}\frac{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{24}}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{12}}\,q_{1}^{1/2}+O(q_{1}^{3/2}).

(251)

We find for the leading terms of the coupling
v
,

v=-\frac{\tau_{1}}{2}+\frac{1}{2\pi i}\left(2\log(W)-2(W^{-2}-W^{2})\,q_{1}^{\frac{1}{2}}+O(q_{1})\right).

(252)

Substition of this expansion in (
60
) demonstrates that the leading term in the
q_{1}
-expansion of
C
is

\begin{split}C(\tau_{1},\tau_{\rm uv},v)&=\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{3/2}\,\frac{e^{2\pi i(v+\tau_{1}/4)}}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}}+O(1)\\
&=\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{3/2}\frac{\vartheta_{2}(2\tau_{\rm uv})}{2\vartheta_{3}(2\tau_{\rm uv})^{3}}\,q_{1}^{-1/4}+O(1),\end{split}

(253)

where we used the identity

\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{2}=2\,\vartheta_{2}(2\tau)\,\vartheta_{3}(2\tau)
.

With (
250
), (
251
) and (
253
), we find for the leading behavior of the measure
\nu

\begin{split}\nu(\tau_{1},\tau_{\rm uv},m)&=\kappa\,2^{12+\sigma-2\ell}\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{3\ell}\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4\ell+4\chi+9\sigma}\,\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-3\sigma/2-12}\\
&\qquad\times\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{\chi-6}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{\chi-6}\left(\frac{\vartheta_{2}(2\tau_{\rm uv})}{\vartheta_{3}(2\tau_{\rm uv})^{3}}\right)^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\,q_{1}^{-\ell/2}+\dots.\end{split}

(254)

We can understand the leading behavior of
\nu
from the
local
R
-symmetry
[
21
,
22
]
. The local
R
-charges are normalized such
that the local coordinate
a_{1}
has charge 2. Since
u-u_{1}
vanishes linearly with the local
coordinate
a_{1}
for
u
near
u_{1}
, the local
R
-charge of
u-u_{1}

is 2. The massless modes of the
u
-multiplet account for
(\chi+\sigma)/2=2
,
whereas the light quark contributes
-2
Index
({\rm\bf D}_{A})=(\sigma-c_{\rm uv}^{2})/4
(
129
) to the

R
-charge of the measure. The total
R
-charge of the measure is therefore
-2\ell
.
We therefore arrive at,

\nu\sim\frac{da}{d\tau}\,A^{\chi}\,B^{\sigma}\,C^{c_{\rm uv}^{2}/4}\sim(u-m^{2}\,e_{1}/4)^{-\ell}\sim q_{1}^{-\ell/2},

(255)

in agreement with (
254
).

We finish this subsection by listing the limits of the contact terms,
T_{2,1}=\lim_{u\to u_{1}}T_{2}
and
T_{3,1}=\lim_{u\to u_{1}}T_{3}
,

\begin{split}T_{2,1}(\tau_{\rm uv})&=\frac{m^{2}}{144\,\Lambda^{2}}\left(-3\,\vartheta_{3}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,\vartheta_{4}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right.\\
&\left.\quad+\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,(\vartheta_{3}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})+\vartheta_{4}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv}))+E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})+\frac{6}{\pi i}\,\frac{dE_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{d\tau_{\rm uv}}\right),\\
T_{3,1}(\tau_{\rm uv})&=-\frac{i\,m}{6\,\Lambda}\left(\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}+\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}+2\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right)+\frac{i}{2\Lambda}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{1},\end{split}

(256)

where
(du/da)_{1}=\lim_{u\to u_{1}}du/da
is the leading term in (
250
).

5.1.3
u\to u_{2}

We determine the behavior of the various quantities in the
strong-coupling limit
\tau\to 0
. We denote the dual weak coupling by

\tau_{2}=-1/\tau
.
14
14

14

Note
\tau_{2}
is
not
the imaginary
part of
\tau
, which is
y
.

We define the strongly coupled version of
du/da
,

(du/da)_{2}(\tau_{2})=\tau_{2}(du/da)(-1/\tau_{2})
. In the limit
\tau\to 0
, this approaches

\begin{split}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{2}\!\!(\tau_{2})&=m\,\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}+O(q_{2}^{1/2})\\
&=2\sqrt{2}i\,\Lambda_{0}+O(q_{\rm uv}^{1/2},q_{2}^{1/2}).\end{split}

(257)

Similarly, the dual of the discriminant

\Delta_{\rm phys}(\tau)=\Delta_{\rm phys}(-1/\tau_{2})
. The leading term is

\Delta_{\rm phys,2}(\tau_{2}):=\Delta_{\rm phys}(-1/\tau_{2})=-(2m)^{6}\frac{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{24}}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{12}}\,q_{2}^{1/2}+O(q_{2}).

(258)

To determine the dual version,
v_{2}
of
v
, we deduce from the
monodromy (
57
), that the leading term is

\tau_{2}/2
. Using (
67
), we deduce sub-leading terms,

\begin{split}&\tau_{2}=-\frac{1}{\tau}\mapsto i\infty,\qquad v_{2}=-\frac{v}{\tau}\mapsto\frac{\tau_{2}}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi i}\log\!\left(\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv}/2)}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv}/2)}\right)+O\!\left(q_{2}^{1/2}\right).\end{split}

(259)

We let
C_{2}
be the coupling dual to
C
near
u_{2}
, defined as

C_{2}(\tau_{2},\tau_{\rm uv},v_{2})=\exp(-2\pi iv_{2}^{2}/\tau_{2})\,C(-1/\tau_{2},\tau_{\rm uv},v_{2}/\tau_{2}).

(260)

Using (
60
), we find that it is given by

\begin{split}C_{2}(\tau_{2},\tau_{\rm uv},v_{2})&=-\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\,\frac{1}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}}\,\frac{\vartheta_{1}(2\tau_{2},2v_{2})}{\vartheta_{4}(2\tau_{2})}\\
&=i\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{1}{\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}}\,e^{-2\pi iv_{2}}\,q_{2}^{1/4}+O(q_{2}^{1/4})\\
&=i\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv}/2)}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv}/2)^{3}}\,q_{2}^{-1/4}+O(q_{2}^{1/4}).\end{split}

(261)

As expected, the coefficient of
q_{2}^{-1/4}
is the UV
S
-dual of the
coefficient of
q_{1}^{-1/4}
in (
253
).

With (
257
), (
258
) and (
261
), the leading
behavior of
\nu
is

\begin{split}\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)&=\kappa\,(-1)^{\ell}\,2^{10+3\sigma/2}\,\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{3\ell}\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4\ell+4\chi+9\sigma}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-3\sigma/2-12}\\
&\qquad\times\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{\chi-6}\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{\chi-6}\left(\frac{\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv}/2)}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv}/2)^{3}}\right)^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\,q_{2}^{-\ell/2}+\dots.\end{split}

(262)

Analogously to
u_{1}
, we can understand the leading behavior of
\nu
from the
local
R
-symmetry. The massless monopole contributes

(\sigma-c_{\rm uv}^{2})/4
to the
R
-charge of the measure.

We finish this subsection by listing the limits of the contact terms
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
for
u\to u_{2}
,

\begin{split}T_{2,2}(\tau_{\rm uv})&=\frac{m^{2}}{144\,\Lambda^{2}}\left(-3\,\vartheta_{2}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,\vartheta_{3}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right.\\
&\left.\quad-E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,(\vartheta_{2}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})+\vartheta_{3}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv}))+E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})+\frac{6}{\pi i}\,\frac{dE_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{d\tau_{\rm uv}}\right),\\
T_{3,2}(\tau_{\rm uv})&=-\frac{i\,m}{6\,\Lambda}\left(-\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}-\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}+2\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right)-\frac{i}{2\Lambda}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{2}.\end{split}

(263)

5.1.4
u\to u_{3}

We define the dual coupling near this singularity by

\tau_{3}=-\frac{1}{\tau-1}.

(264)

We define the strong coupling version of
(du/da)_{3}=\tau_{3}\,(du/da)(1-1/\tau_{3})
. Near
u_{3}
, this gives

\begin{split}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{\!\!3}(\tau_{3})&=-m\,i\,\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}+O(q_{3}^{1/2})\\
&=2\sqrt{2}\,\Lambda_{0}+O(q_{\rm uv}^{1/2},q_{3}^{1/2})\end{split}

(265)

We have resolved the ambiguity of the square root, by requiring that
the
N_{f}=0
limit matches with the limit of
du/da
of the
N_{f}=0

theory.

The dual discriminant is
\Delta_{{\rm phys},3}(\tau_{3})=\Delta_{\rm phys}(1-1/\tau_{3})
, with leading term

\Delta_{{\rm phys},3}(\tau_{3})=-(2m)^{6}\,\frac{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{24}}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{12}}\,q_{3}^{1/2}+O(q_{3}).

(266)

The strong-coupling limit
\tau\to 1
,

\tau_{3}\mapsto i\infty,\qquad v_{3}=-\frac{v}{\tau-1}\mapsto\frac{\tau_{3}}{2}+\frac{1}{\pi i}\log\!\left(\frac{\vartheta_{4}((\tau_{\rm uv}+1)/2)}{\vartheta_{3}((\tau_{\rm uv}+1)/2)}\right)+O(q_{3}^{1/2}),

(267)

The dependence of
v_{3}
on
\tau_{3}
matches with the monodromy around
u_{3}
(
58
).

For
C_{3}(\tau_{3},\tau_{\rm uv},v_{3})=\exp(-2\pi iv_{3}^{2}/\tau_{3})\,C(1-1/\tau_{3},\tau_{\rm uv},v_{3}/\tau_{3})
,

\begin{split}C_{3}(\tau_{3},\tau_{\rm uv},v_{3})&=\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\,\frac{-1}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}}\,\frac{\vartheta_{1}(2\tau_{3},2v_{3})}{\vartheta_{4}(2\tau_{3})}\\
&=i\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{1}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}}\,e^{-2\pi iv_{3}}q_{3}^{1/4}+O(q_{3}^{1/4})\\
&=i\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{\frac{3}{2}}\frac{\vartheta_{4}((\tau_{\rm uv}+1)/2)}{\vartheta_{4}((\tau_{\rm uv}+1)/2)^{3}}\,q_{3}^{-1/4}+O(q_{3}^{1/4})\end{split}

(268)

With (
265
), (
266
) and (
268
), we find for the
leading behavior of
\nu

\begin{split}\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)&=\kappa\,e^{-\frac{\pi i}{2}(\frac{\chi}{2}-3)}\,(-1)^{\ell}\,2^{10+3\sigma/2}\,\left(\frac{\Lambda}{m}\right)^{3\ell}\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4\ell+4\chi+9\sigma}\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-3\sigma/2-12}\\
&\qquad\times\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{\chi-6}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{\chi-6}\left(\frac{\vartheta_{4}((\tau_{\rm uv}+1)/2)}{\vartheta_{3}((\tau_{\rm uv}+1)/2)^{3}}\right)^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\,q_{3}^{-\ell/2}+\dots.\end{split}

(269)

Analogously to
u_{1}
, we can understand the leading behavior of
\nu
from the
local
R
-symmetry. The massless dyon contributes

(\sigma-c_{\rm uv}^{2})/4
to the
R
-charge of the measure.

We finish this subsection by listing the limits of the contact terms
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
for
u\to u_{3}
,

\begin{split}T_{2,3}(\tau_{\rm uv})&=\frac{m^{2}}{144\,\Lambda^{2}}\left(3\,\vartheta_{2}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,\vartheta_{4}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right.\\
&\left.\quad+E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,(\vartheta_{2}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})-\vartheta_{4}^{4}(\tau_{\rm uv}))+E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})+\frac{6}{\pi i}\,\frac{dE_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{d\tau_{\rm uv}}\right),\\
T_{3,3}(\tau_{\rm uv})&=-\frac{i\,m}{6\,\Lambda}\left(\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}+2\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\right)-\frac{i}{2\Lambda}\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{3}.\end{split}

(270)

5.2
S
-duality of
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}

In this subsection, we will analyze relations and duality properties of

\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv};{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})
. Equation (
175
)
implies a periodicity for
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
,

\Phi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv};{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})=\Phi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv};{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}),\qquad{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in L.

(271)

In other words,
\Phi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
is independent of the choice of lift
\bar{w}_{2}(E)=2{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\in L
of
w_{2}(E)
. For the dependence on
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
, we have the relation,

\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv};-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})=-\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv};{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}),

(272)

thus in particular
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0
for
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}=0
.
(Nevertheless, the topological correlators can be nonzero
when
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}=0
.)

We first consider the
T
-transformation,
\tau_{\rm uv}\mapsto\tau_{\rm uv}+1
. To
determine the effect on
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, we also shift
\tau\mapsto\tau+1
. This changes the integration domain, but using a
\Gamma(2)

transformation on some of the
\mathcal{F}_{\infty}
images, we can map this
back to the original domain. Since the couplings,
A
,
B
and
C
are
unchanged under this transformation, the only change is from
\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{-\chi+3/2\sigma-2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}
and

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
(
178
).
We find

\begin{split}\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv}+1,\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}+1)&=e^{2\pi i({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{2}-B({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K)-(\chi+2\ell)/12)}\,\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})\\
&=e^{-2\pi i({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{2}+(\chi+2\ell)/12)}\,\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}).\end{split}

(273)

We note that while the integrand is invariant under
the shift of the effective coupling constant,
\tau\mapsto\tau+2
,
the integral
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is in general multiplied by a phase for

\tau_{\rm uv}\mapsto\tau_{\rm uv}+2
.

We proceed similarly for the
S
-transformation,
\tau_{\rm uv}\mapsto-1/\tau_{\rm uv}
. We change variables in the integrand,
\tau\mapsto-1/\tau
. Then,

\begin{split}&\frac{da}{du}(-1/\tau,-1/\tau_{\rm uv})=-\frac{\tau}{\tau_{\rm uv}^{2}}\frac{da}{du}(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv}),\\
&\Delta_{\rm phys}(-1/\tau,-1/\tau_{\rm uv})=\tau_{\rm uv}^{6}\,\Delta_{\rm phys}(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv}),\\
&C(-1/\tau,-1/\tau_{\rm uv})=-\tau_{\rm uv}^{-1}\,e^{2\pi i{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}v^{2}/\tau}\,C(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv}).\end{split}

(274)

Therefore,
\nu
transforms as

\begin{split}\nu(-1/\tau,-1/\tau_{\rm uv},m)&=i\,(-i\tau_{\rm uv})^{-\chi/2-4\ell}(-i\tau)^{3-\chi/2}(-1)^{\ell}\\
&\quad\times e^{2\pi i{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}v^{2}/\tau}\,\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m).\end{split}

(275)

Using
\chi+\sigma=4
, and (
175
), (
178
) for the transformation of

\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
, we arrive at

\begin{split}\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(-1/\tau_{\rm uv},-1/\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})&=2^{-b_{2}/2}e^{\pi i({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}/2-\sigma/8)}\,(-i\tau_{\rm uv})^{-\chi/2-4\ell}\\
&\quad\times\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in(L/2)/L}e^{4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})}\,\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}),\end{split}

(276)

Thus
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
forms a vector-valued representation of

SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
for any choice of
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
. Note that for
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}

an almost complex structure,
\ell=0
, the weight reduces to
-\chi/2
, which is
the familiar weight from VW theory
[
15
]
. We make a more
thorough comparison in Section
6.6
.

In Section
4.7
, we introduced the “renormalized” partition function
\underline{\Phi}^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
(
208
). It transforms as (
276
), except that its weight is
\chi/2-2\ell
.

Observables and
S
-duality

We collect all the couplings involving
p
and
S
discussed in
Section
4.8
. These give rise to the factor

P(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},p,S)=e^{T_{2}\,S^{2}+T_{3}B(S,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})+p\,\tilde{u}/\Lambda^{2}-\frac{i}{\Lambda}\frac{du}{da}B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{-},S)}.

(277)

From the transformation properties for
u
,
du/da
,
T_{2}
(
223
)
and
T_{3}
(
230
), we can deduce the
S
-duality action on
p
and

S
. For an element
\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
, we set

\begin{split}&p\mapsto\gamma\cdot p:=\frac{1}{(c\tau_{\rm uv}+d)^{2}}\left(p-\frac{c(c\tau_{\rm uv}+d)}{2\pi i}\,S^{2}\right),\\
&S\mapsto\gamma\cdot S:=\frac{S}{(c\tau_{\rm uv}+d)^{2}}.\end{split}

(278)

Similarly to before we determine the transformation of the simultaneous IR and UV S-duality.
We find that
P
(
277
) transforms as

\begin{split}&P(\gamma\cdot\tau,\gamma\cdot\tau_{\rm uv},\gamma\cdot p,\gamma\cdot S)=\\
&e^{-\frac{m^{2}\,S^{2}}{4\pi^{2}\,\Lambda^{2}}\frac{c^{2}}{(c\tau_{\rm uv}+d)^{2}}-\frac{2m}{\pi\Lambda}\frac{c}{c\tau_{\rm uv}+d}B(S,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})}\,P\!\left(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},p,-\frac{S}{c\tau+d}\right).\end{split}

(279)

The
-S/(c\tau+d)
matches with the duality transformation of the elliptic variable
of
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
.

Then the
S
and
T
transformations of
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
including observables are

\begin{split}&\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\!\left(-1/\tau_{\rm uv},-1/\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv},(p-\tau_{\rm uv}\,S^{2}/2\pi i)/\tau_{\rm uv}^{2},S/\tau_{\rm uv}^{2}\right)=\\
&\qquad e^{-\frac{m^{2}\,S^{2}}{4\pi^{2}\,\Lambda^{2}\,\tau_{\rm uv}^{2}}-\frac{2m}{\pi\Lambda\,\tau_{\rm uv}}B(S,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})}\,2^{-b_{2}/2}e^{\pi i({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}/2-\sigma/8)}\,(-i\tau_{\rm uv})^{-\chi/2-4\ell}\\
&\qquad\times\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in(L/2)/L}e^{4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})}\,\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv},p,S),\\
&\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv}+1,\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}+1,p,S)=e^{-2\pi i({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{2}+(\chi+2\ell)/12)}\,\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv},p,S).\end{split}

(280)

Remark
The transformation laws of
(p,S)
can be understood from
the Lagrangian viewpoint by studying the standard Abelian S-duality
transformation at the path integral level, at least in a simplified
version of the low energy Abelian theory. See
[
17
, pages 41-43]
.

S
-Duality of the
SU(2)
and
SO(3)_{\pm}
theories

Using the transformations under
S
and
T
, we can analyze the

S
-duality properties of the partition functions of the three theories
\mathcal{T}_{SU(2)}
and

\mathcal{T}_{SO(3)_{\pm}}
, which we discussed in Section
2.9
.
Leaving aside the Seiberg-Witten contributions, we define the following partition functions for these theories,

\begin{array}[]{ll}Z_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}[\mathcal{T}_{SU(2)}]=\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J},&\\
Z_{{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}}[\mathcal{T}_{SO(3)_{+}}]=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in(L/2)/L}e^{4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{J},&\\
Z_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}[\mathcal{T}_{SO(3)_{-}}]=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in(L/2)/L}e^{4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})-2\pi i{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{2}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\nu}}^{J}.&\end{array}

(281)

Thus for each of the
\mathcal{T}_{SU(2)}
and
\mathcal{T}_{SO(3)_{\pm}}
theories, we
define a
|L/(L/2)|
partition functions, each with a fixed ’t Hooft
flux
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
.

These partition functions are mapped to each other under
S,T\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
, up to an overall factor due to the non-trivial
weight and phase. We identify partition functions, which differ by
such an overall factor and denote an equivalence class by
\left[Z_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(G)\right]
. The duality diagram for such equivalence classes
is given in Figure
3
. This is in a sense a doubling of the
diagram of theories in Figure
1
, with three extra nodes
with partition functions for ’t Hooft flux
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+\bar{w}_{2}(X)/2\in L/2
. We
consider these as partition functions for one of the three
theories, but with a different ’t Hooft flux. Note that if
X
is a spin
manifold, then
\bar{w}_{2}(X)=0
, and the diagram in in Figure
3

reduces to one in Figure
1
. The diagram in Figure

3
with
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=0
recently also appeared in
[
61
,
98
]
. It was suggested in these papers that the partition
function for
Z^{SU(2)}_{\bar{w}_{2}(X)}
, was that of a different theory,
namely spin-
SU(2)
, and similarly for
SO(3)_{\pm}
, which is slightly
different from our interpretation.

\left[Z_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{SU(2)}\right]
\left[Z_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{SO(3)_{+}}\right]
\left[Z_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{SO(3)_{-}}\right]
\left[Z_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+\bar{w}_{2}/2}^{SO(3)_{-}}\right]
\left[Z_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+\bar{w}_{2}/2}^{SO(3)_{+}}\right]
\left[Z_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}+\bar{w}_{2}/2}^{SU(2)}\right]
T

T
