Figure 3:
Action of the generators
S
and
T
of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
on
equivalence classes
[Z^{G}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}]
of partition functions

Z^{G}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}:=Z_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[\mathcal{T}(G)]
.
\bar{w}_{2}
is an integral lift of

w_{2}(X)
to
L
.

5.3
Holomorphic anomaly

An intriguing aspect is the dependence on the anti-holomorphic coupling
constant
\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}
of the Coulomb path integral
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
. The
\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}

dependence of the Lagrangian (
159
) is contained in its

Q
-exact part. The
\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}
-dependence therefore evaluates to
the one-point function

\frac{\partial}{\partial\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=\left<[Q,G]\right>,

(282)

with
G=\int_{X}\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}W
. Since
Q
is a global
fermionic symmetry of the theory, a non-vanishing value of
\left<[Q,G]\right>
implies the
presence of a
Q
-anomaly. On the other hand, the path integral for such
correlation functions of a
Q
-exact observable can be
expressed as a total derivative on field space, and therefore
relatively easily evaluated at the boundary of field space. We will demonstrate that the result is
non-vanishing. The result suggests that the
Q
-anomaly is due to the contribution from a class
of classical solutions which do not satisfy the
Q
-fixed point
equations. These are the reducible
connections or abelian configurations, whose field strength in the two-dimensional
representation is of the form

\left[\frac{F}{2\pi}\right]=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\boldsymbol{c}&0\\
0&-\boldsymbol{c}\end{array}\right),

(283)

with
{\boldsymbol{c}}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z})
. These are solutions to the equations
of motion but are not anti-self-dual. The instanton
number of these solutions is
k=-{\boldsymbol{c}}^{2}
. Thus
k
can be either
positive or negative, while
k\geq 0

for proper
Q
-fixed solutions. Moreover, the
kinetic part evaluates to

\frac{1}{8\pi^{2}}\int_{X}\mathrm{Tr}_{\bf 2}\left(F\wedge*F\right)=2\,B({\boldsymbol{c}},J)^{2}-{\boldsymbol{c}}^{2},

(284)

which for generic
J
exceeds the lower bound
k=-{\boldsymbol{c}}^{2}
. For the

Q
-fixed anti-self-dual instanton solutions, the lower bound is
saturated. Solutions which exceed the lower bound give necessarily
rise to anti-holomorphic dependence of the path integral.

Solutions which do not saturate the
Q
-bound typically do not
contribute to the path integral due to cancellations between bosonic
and fermionic degrees of freedom. However, for four-manifolds with

b_{2}^{+}=1
, the Coulomb branch is shown to contribute at tree
level
[
5
]
, and the Coulomb branch measure
involves a sum over
all
the harmonic connections
on the low energy line bundle. If we view these as reducible
connections, they are not self-dual.

A complementary viewpoint is given by string
dualities. This is most straightforward for the
\mathcal{N}=4
case, i.e. the massless theory with

{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
an ACS. Then the theory can be related to a two-dimensional
field theory with
(0,4)
supersymmetry. The non-compact field space
of this theory can lead to a spectral asymmetry, which prevents the
precise cancellation among bosonic and fermionic states for non-BPS states

[
99
,
100
,
101
,
102
]
. In our
context,
u\to\infty
gives rise to a non-compact direction in field
space. One can also reduce further to a quantum-mechanical model of
BPS particles of an
\mathcal{N}=2
theory in four dimensions. From this perspective, the non-holomorphic contribution can be obtained as the contribution from multi-particle BPS states to the
Witten index in

[
103
,
104
]
.

Anomaly for
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
an almost complex structure

To determine the dependence of the partition function on
\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}
explicitly, let us start with
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
an almost complex
structure. Changing variables to
\sigma=\tau-\tau_{\rm uv}
in the
integrand, we arrive at

\begin{split}\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})&=\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\int_{\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}-\tau_{\rm uv}}d\sigma\wedge d\bar{\sigma}\,\nu(\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv},\tau_{\rm uv},m)\,\\
&\quad\times\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}),\end{split}

(285)

with
v=v(\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv},\tau_{\rm uv})
. Thus the integrand depends
explicitly on
\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}
through the shift of
\bar{\sigma}
, and
the explicit dependence in
\bar{v}
. The first dependence gives
naturally rise to a total
\bar{\tau}
-derivative.

To understand the dependence through
\bar{v}
, we replace
v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}

by a generic elliptic variable
{\boldsymbol{z}}\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{C})
in the sum over
fluxes
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
(
171
). Then for fixed
\tau
, we have

\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})|_{\tau\,\,{\rm fixed}}=\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\left(\frac{i}{\sqrt{y}}\left(\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}|_{\bar{\tau}\,\,{\rm fixed}}\right)\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}[1](\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})\right).

(286)

where
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}[1]
is the series (
174
) with kernel

\mathcal{K}=1
. In the
u
-plane integral
{\boldsymbol{z}}=v\,{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
. We therefore
arrive at

\begin{split}&\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})=\partial_{\bar{\sigma}}\left(\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})\right.\\
&\quad\left.+\frac{i\,B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},J)}{\sqrt{y_{\rm uv}+\mathrm{Im}(\sigma)}}\left(\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\bar{v}|_{\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}\,\,{\rm fixed}}\right)\,\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}[1](\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})\right).\end{split}

(287)

We can express
\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\bar{v}|_{\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}\,\,{\rm fixed}}
as
(\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}-\partial_{\bar{\sigma}})\overline{v(\tau_{\rm uv}+\sigma,\tau_{\rm uv})}
. The
\partial_{\bar{\sigma}}
-term of this derivative cancels against a term of the
kernel
\partial_{\bar{\tau}}(\sqrt{4y}B({\boldsymbol{k}}+{\boldsymbol{b}},J))
of

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
(
171
) after changing variables to
\tau=\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv}
. The theta series within the brackets in
(
287
) can then be expressed as a theta
series
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}[\mathcal{K}_{0}]
(
174
),

\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})=\partial_{\bar{\sigma}}\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}[\mathcal{K}_{0}](\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\sigma}+\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}),

(288)

with the kernel
\mathcal{K}_{0}
given by

\mathcal{K}_{0}=\frac{i}{2\sqrt{y_{\rm uv}+\mathrm{Im}(\sigma)}}B({\boldsymbol{k}}-{\boldsymbol{b}},J)+\frac{i}{\sqrt{y_{\rm uv}+\mathrm{Im}(\sigma)}}B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},J)\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\overline{v(\sigma+\tau_{\rm uv},\tau_{\rm uv})}.

(289)

Since the integrand is a total derivative in
\bar{\sigma}
, we need to determine the term proportional to

\sigma^{1/2}=(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})^{\frac{1}{2}}
in the expansion near

\tau=\tau_{\rm uv}
, or equivalently near
\sigma=0
.
Given the behavior of
v
for
\tau
near
\tau_{\rm uv}
(
245
),
we realize that the second term does not contribute. For
\tau
near

\tau_{\rm uv}
, the leading term of
\mathcal{K}_{0}\,e^{-4\pi ivB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{-},{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})}
therefore
reads

\frac{1}{\pi i}(-\pi i(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv}))^{1/2}\left(-\frac{B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},J)}{4\,y_{\rm uv}^{3/2}}+\frac{2\pi}{\sqrt{y_{\rm uv}}}B({\boldsymbol{k}},J)\,B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{-},{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})\right).

(290)

Computing the residue integral for
\tau
around
\tau_{\rm uv}
, we arrive at

\boxed{\begin{split}\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})&=\frac{\kappa}{8\pi iy_{\rm uv}^{3/2}\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2\chi}}\\
&\quad\times\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\left[B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},J)-8\pi y_{\rm uv}B({\boldsymbol{k}},J)\,B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{-},{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})\right]q_{\rm uv}^{-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{-}^{2}}\bar{q}_{\rm uv}^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{+}^{2}}.\end{split}}

(291)

For
b_{2}=1
and
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}=3/2
, the second term in the square bracket is necessarily 0,
such that we arrive at,

\begin{split}\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})&=-\frac{3i\kappa}{16\pi y_{\rm uv}^{3/2}}\,\frac{\Theta_{\mu}(-\bar{\tau})}{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{6}},\\
\end{split}

(292)

where
\Theta_{\mu}
is given by

\Theta_{\mu}(\tau)=\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}+\mu}q^{k^{2}}.

(293)

This is in agreement with the known holomorphic anomaly for
\mathbb{P}^{2}

[
15
]
. The comparison fixes the undetermined constant,
\kappa=1
, which we will use in the following. We deduce that
\eta^{6}\,\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
is annihilated by the
Laplacian
\partial_{\tau_{\rm uv}}y_{\rm uv}^{3/2}\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}
, and is thus a harmonic Maass form.

Equation (
291
) is also in agreement with the holomorphic anomaly derived for Hirzebruch surfaces on page 12 of

[
34
]
for
\kappa=1
and
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}=-K/2
with
K
the
canonical class of the Hirzebruch surface. The second term in the brackets of the summand may be
absent in special cases. One case is if
J\sim{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
, and another case is if
J=(1,0,\dots,0)
.
The rational elliptic surface is an interesting case
[
32
,
105
,
106
]
. Since
K^{2}=0
, the period point
J=-K
is at the boundary of the Kähler cone for this four-manifold. The holomorphic anomaly therefore changes from mock modular forms to that of quasi-modular forms. Moreover, T-duality relates the partition function to that of topological strings.

Anomaly for
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
not an almost complex structure

Let us consider the case that
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
is not an ACS. If

{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}>(2\chi+3\sigma)/4
or
\ell>0
, the leading term of
\nu
goes as

\sigma^{c}
, with
c\geq-1/2
. As a result, there are no poles in

\sigma
, and the holomorphic anomaly vanishes.
We discuss below that a non-vanishing holomorphic anomaly is possible for
\ell>0
for correlation functions, since
u
and
du/da
diverge for
\sigma\to 0
.

On the other hand, if
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}<(2\chi+3\sigma)/4
or
\ell<0
, the leading term of
\nu
goes as

\sigma^{c}
, with
c\leq-5/2
, such that there is generically an
holomorphic anomaly. To get an idea of the type of anomaly, let us also determine this for

X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
with
k_{m}=1/2
. Then
\nu
diverges as

\sigma^{-5/2}
, and we need to determine also the first subleading
term in the
\sigma
-expansion of
\nu
and
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
using the
expansions in (
246
). After collecting all the terms,
we find that the holomorphic anomaly becomes

\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})=-\frac{i}{48\pi\,y_{\rm uv}^{3/2}}\,\frac{\widehat{E}_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})\,\Theta_{\mu}(-\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})}{\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}},

(294)

where
\widehat{E}_{2}
is the non-holomorphic Eisenstein series defined
in (
505
) and
\Theta_{\mu}
by (
293
).
Since the theta series
\Theta_{\mu}
is multiplied by
\widehat{E}_{2}
,
the numerator is more general than the class of harmonic Maass forms.

Anomaly for correlations functions

The holomorphic anomaly is generically also present for correlation
functions, even when absent for the partition function. There is an holomorphic anomaly for the coefficients of
S^{r}p^{n}
for

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\ell\leq n+r/2,&\qquad r\,\,{\rm even},\\
\ell\leq n+(r+1)/2,&\qquad r\,\,{\rm odd}.\end{array}\right.

(295)

Thus only for
\ell>0
can the holomorphic anomaly be absent,
and then it is only absent for a finite set of correlators.

As an explicit example, we consider
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
k_{m}=3/2
, and the
one-point function
\left<u\right>
. We find for the
holomorphic anomaly

\partial_{\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}}\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}[u](\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})=-\frac{3i\,m^{2}}{64\pi\,y_{\rm uv}^{3/2}}\,\frac{\widehat{E}_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})\,\,\Theta_{\mu}(-\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})}{\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{6}}.

(296)

This equals (
294
) up to the power of
\eta
and the
prefactor. We leave a more in depth analysis for future work.

Since the space of modular forms is finite dimensional for fixed
weight and multiplier system, combined knowledge of the holomorphic anomaly and

S
-duality of Section
5.2
is very powerful. Let us recall that the weight of the ”normalized” partition function
\underline{\Phi}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
(
208
) is
\chi/2-2\ell
and that
\underline{\Phi}^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is holomorphic with leading term
q_{\rm uv}^{0}
or smaller. Therefore for
\ell>\chi/4
, we have necessarily that
\underline{\Phi}^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
and
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
vanish, since holomorphic vector-valued modular forms of negative weight necessarily vanish. Therefore for
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
is only non-vanishing for
k_{m}=1/2
and
k_{m}=3/2
.

Similarly to the determination of partition functions for topological strings
[
107
]
, a potential approach to determine
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
is to integrate the anomaly
(
291
) with respect to
\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}
, and fix the holomorphic
ambiguity using independent information, . This avenue is taken in
[
108
]
for VW theory,
15
15

15

For VW theory and
X
a rational and ruled surfaces, the
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
can also be determined using algebro-geometric techniques as reviewed in Section
6.6
.
However for generic
c_{\rm uv}
and
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
observables, we do not have enough information to fix the holomorphic ambiguity. The next Section will evaluate
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
by explicit evaluation of the
u
-plane integral.

5.4
Metric dependence and wall-crossing

The metric dependence of the Lagrangian is also contained in the

Q
-exact part. However, the path integral and correlation functions
can still exhibit a metric dependence similarly to what found in the
previous subsection for the dependence on
\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}
. To
determine the metric dependence, the aim of this subsection is to evaluate the difference

\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J^{\prime}}

(297)

for two period points
J
and
J^{\prime}
. To this end, we write the
difference
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J^{\prime}}
as a total derivative to
\bar{\tau}
,

\begin{split}&\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})-\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})=\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}),\end{split}

(298)

with

\begin{split}\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})&=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\left[E(\sqrt{4y}B({\boldsymbol{k}}+{\boldsymbol{b}},J))-E(\sqrt{4y}B({\boldsymbol{k}}+{\boldsymbol{b}},J^{\prime}))\right]\\
&\qquad\times q^{-{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}\,e^{-4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}},{\boldsymbol{z}})},\end{split}

(299)

with
E(t)
is the rescaled error function defined in
(
533
). We also define the holomorphic part

\begin{split}\Theta_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,{\boldsymbol{z}})&=\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\left[\mathrm{sgn}(B({\boldsymbol{k}}+{\boldsymbol{b}},J))-\mathrm{sgn}(\sqrt{4y}B({\boldsymbol{k}}+{\boldsymbol{b}},J^{\prime}))\right]\\
&\qquad\times q^{-{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}\,e^{-4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}},{\boldsymbol{z}})}.\end{split}

(300)

The transformations of
\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
are similar to (
178
),

\begin{split}\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(-1/\tau,-1/\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}}/\tau,\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}/\bar{\tau})&=i(-i\tau/2)^{b_{2}/2}\,\exp(-2\pi i{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}/\tau)\\
&\quad\times\widehat{\Theta}_{0}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau/4,\bar{\tau}/4,{\boldsymbol{z}}/2-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2,\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}/2-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2)\\
&=i(-i\tau/2)^{b_{2}/2}\,\exp(-2\pi i{\boldsymbol{z}}^{2}/\tau)\\
&\quad\times\sum_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}\in(L/2)/L}e^{4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{\mu}},{\boldsymbol{\nu}})}\,\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\nu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}),\\
\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau+1/2,\bar{\tau}+1/2,{\boldsymbol{z}},\bar{\boldsymbol{z}})&=e^{\pi i({\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{2}-B({\boldsymbol{\mu}},K))}\\
&\quad\times\widehat{\Theta}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2-K/4,\bar{\boldsymbol{z}}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2-K/4).\end{split}

(301)

The completed theta series
\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
is a smooth function of
J
and

J^{\prime}
. However, we will see below that the contributions to
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J^{\prime}}
from
u_{1}
,

u_{2}
and
u_{3}
are discontinuous after taking the limit
Y\to\infty
.

Contribution from
\infty

The contribution from
\infty
is

\left[\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\Phi^{J^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right]_{\infty}=\underset{\varepsilon\to 0}{\rm\lim}\oint_{|\tau-\tau_{\rm uv}|=\varepsilon}d\tau\,\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)\,\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}),

(302)

which is non-holomorphic in
\tau_{\rm uv}
. We deduce also that
the metric dependence is smooth at this boundary, since
\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is smooth. If

{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}>(2\chi+3\sigma)/4
, the rhs of (
302
)
vanishes, since
\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
is a Taylor series in

s=(-\pi i(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv}))^{1/2}
.

On the other hand, if we choose
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
to correspond to an almost complex structure, we find

\left[\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\Phi^{J^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}\right]_{\infty}=\frac{1}{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2\chi}}\,\theta^{JJ^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv}),

(303)

with

\begin{split}\widehat{\theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})&=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{v}\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})|_{\tau=\tau_{\rm uv}}\\
&=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}\in L+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}\left[E(\sqrt{4y_{\rm uv}}B({\boldsymbol{k}},J))-E(\sqrt{4y}B({\boldsymbol{k}},J^{\prime}))\right]B({\boldsymbol{k}},{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})\,q_{\rm uv}^{-{\boldsymbol{k}}^{2}}\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{2\pi y_{\rm uv}}\left(B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},J)\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[1](\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})-B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},J^{\prime})\Psi^{J^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[1](\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})\right),\end{split}

(304)

where the elliptic arguments in
\Psi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[1]
and

\Psi^{J^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}[1]
on the last line are set to 0.

The wall-crossing of Seiberg-Witten invariants cancels the contributions to

\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\Phi^{J^{\prime}}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}
from
u_{1}
,
u_{2}
and

u_{3}
. Equation (
302
) gives therefore the full
continuous metric dependence of
Z_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
. This metric dependence
is clearly only present for
b_{2}^{+}=1
, for which

\ell=(c_{\rm uv}^{2}-\sigma)/8-1
. Without observables, there is continuous metric dependence
for
\ell\leq 0
. Including observables, there is continuous metric
dependence for the coefficients of
S^{r}p^{n}
for

\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\ell\leq n+r/2,&\qquad r\,\,{\rm even},\\
\ell\leq n+(r+1)/2,&\qquad r\,\,{\rm odd}.\end{array}\right.

(305)

Thus only for
\ell>0
can continuous metric dependence be absent,
and then it is only absent for a finite set of correlators.

Contribution from
u_{1}

Let us start by determining the contribution from
u_{1}
or

\tau=\tau_{1}\to i\infty
. Since
v\sim-\tau_{1}/2
near
u_{1}
(
252
),
it is useful shift
v
, such that
\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
is expressed as

\begin{split}\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})&=e^{-2\pi i(v+\tau/4){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\,\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},(v+\tau/2){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},(\bar{v}+\bar{\tau}/2){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}).\end{split}

(306)

Since the subscript on the rhs is
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
, the
summation variable
{\boldsymbol{k}}
of the theta series is an element in
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}+L
. We refer to
4{\boldsymbol{k}}
as the infra-red

{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure, thus
4{\boldsymbol{k}}=c_{1}(\mathfrak{s}_{\rm ir})=:c_{\rm ir}\in 4{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-2{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}+4L
.

With (
306
), we find that the wall-crossing at
u_{1}
is given by

\begin{split}&\left[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J^{\prime}}\right]_{1}=\frac{\kappa\,m^{\frac{\sigma}{8}-2}}{4\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2\chi-4\ell}}\left(\frac{4}{\Lambda}\right)^{\frac{3}{8}(2\chi+3\sigma)}\\
&\quad\times\lim_{Y\to\infty}\int_{-\frac{1}{2}+iY}^{\frac{3}{2}+iY}d\tau_{1}\,\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)^{\frac{\chi}{2}-3}\Delta_{\rm phys}^{\frac{\sigma}{8}+1}\left(C\,e^{-2\pi i(v_{1}+\tau_{1}/4)}\right)^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\\
&\quad\times\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau_{1},\bar{\tau}_{1},(v_{1}+\tau_{1}/2){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},(\bar{v}_{1}+\bar{\tau}_{1}/2){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}).\end{split}

(307)

The
Y\to\infty
limit has the effect that the error functions in
\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
reduce to

\mathrm{sgn}
’s in (
299
).
In other words,
\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
is replaced by its holomorphic part
\Theta_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}-\frac{1}{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
(
300
), and the
u_{1}
contribution is thus holomorphic in
\tau_{\rm uv}
.
Given the behavior near
u_{1}
of the various quantities, we see that SW wall-crossing
only occurs for
c_{\rm ir}
and
\sigma
satisfying

\sigma+8-c_{\rm ir}^{2}\leq 0.

(308)

This is the same condition as for the theory without hypermultiplets
[
5
]
.

Contribution from
u_{2}

For the contribution from
u_{2}
, we express the integrand in terms of

\tau_{2}=-1/\tau
, and
v_{2}=-v/\tau
(
259
). The indefinite
theta series
\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
reads in terms of these variables,

\begin{split}&\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(-1/\tau_{2},-1/\bar{\tau}_{2},v_{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}/\tau_{2},\bar{v}_{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}/\bar{\tau}_{2})=i(-i\tau_{2}/2)^{b_{2}/2}\exp(-2\pi iv_{2}^{2}/\tau_{2})\\
&\quad\times\widehat{\Theta}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau_{2}/4,\bar{\tau}_{2}/4,v_{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}/2-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2,\bar{v}_{2}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}/2-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2)\\
&=i(-i\tau_{2}/2)^{b_{2}/2}\,e^{-2\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})}\,\exp(-2\pi iv_{2}^{2}/\tau_{2})\,e^{2\pi i(v_{2}-\tau_{2}/4){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\\
&\quad\times\,\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau_{2}/4,\bar{\tau}_{2}/4,(v_{2}/2-\tau_{2}/4){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2,(\bar{v}_{2}/2-\bar{\tau}_{2}/4){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2).\end{split}

(309)

The contribution from
u_{2}
reads,

\begin{split}&\left[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J^{+}}-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J^{-}}\right]_{2}=\frac{\kappa\,m^{\frac{\sigma}{8}-2}}{4\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2\chi-4\ell}}\left(\frac{4}{\Lambda}\right)^{\frac{3}{8}(2\chi+3\sigma)}\,2^{-b_{2}/2}\,e^{\pi i\sigma/4-2\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})}\\
&\quad\times\lim_{Y\to\infty}\int_{-\frac{1}{2}+iY}^{\frac{3}{2}+iY}d\tau_{2}\,\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{2}^{\frac{\chi}{2}-3}\Delta_{\rm phys,2}^{\frac{\sigma}{8}+1}\,\left(C_{2}\,e^{2\pi i(v_{2}-\tau_{2}/4)}\right)^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\\
&\quad\times\,\Theta_{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau_{2}/4,(v_{2}/2-\tau_{2}/4){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2),\end{split}

(310)

where we have replaced
\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
by
\Theta_{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}
as result of the
Y\to\infty
limit.

Contribution from
u_{3}

For the contribution from
u_{3}
, we express the integrand in terms of

\tau_{3}=-\frac{1}{\tau-1}
, and
v_{3}=-v/(\tau-1)
. We then have

\begin{split}&\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(1-1/\tau_{3},1-1/\bar{\tau}_{3},v_{3}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}/\tau_{3},\bar{v}_{3}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}/\bar{\tau}_{3})\\
&\quad=e^{-2\pi i{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{2}}\,\widehat{\Theta}^{JJ^{\prime}}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}(-1/\tau_{3},-1/\bar{\tau}_{3},v_{3}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}/\tau_{3},\bar{v}_{3}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}/\bar{\tau}_{3})\\
&\quad=i(-i\tau_{3}/2)^{b_{2}/2}\,e^{-2\pi i{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{2}-2\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})}\,\exp(-2\pi iv_{3}^{2}/\tau_{3})\,e^{2\pi i(v_{3}-\tau_{3}/4){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\\
&\qquad\times\,\widehat{\Theta}_{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau_{3}/4,\bar{\tau}_{3}/4,(v_{3}/2-\tau_{3}/4){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2,(\bar{v}_{3}/2-\bar{\tau}_{3}/4){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2).\end{split}

(311)

The contribution from
u_{3}
reads,

\begin{split}&\left[\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J^{+}}-\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J^{-}}\right]_{3}=\frac{\kappa\,m^{\frac{\sigma}{8}-2}}{4\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2\chi-4\ell}}\left(\frac{4}{\Lambda}\right)^{\frac{3}{8}(2\chi+3\sigma)}\,2^{-b_{2}/2}\,e^{\pi i\sigma/4-2\pi i{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{2}-2\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},{\boldsymbol{\mu}})}\\
&\quad\times\lim_{Y\to\infty}\int_{-\frac{1}{2}+iY}^{\frac{3}{2}+iY}d\tau_{3}\,\left(\frac{du}{da}\right)_{3}^{\frac{\chi}{2}-3}\Delta_{\rm phys,3}^{\frac{\sigma}{8}+1}\,\left(C_{3}\,e^{2\pi i(v_{3}-\tau_{3}/4)}\right)^{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}}\\
&\quad\times\,\Theta_{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}}^{JJ^{\prime}}(\tau_{3}/4,(v_{3}/2-\tau_{3}/4){\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}-{\boldsymbol{\mu}}/2).\end{split}

(312)

5.5
Blow-up formula

The
u
-plane integral
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(X)
for a four-manifold
X
and
\Phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{\hat{J}}(\hat{X})
for the blow-up
\hat{X}
of
X

are related by a simple formula. Blowing-up a point of
X
adds another two-cycle
to
X
with self-intersection
-1
, such that
\chi(\hat{X})=\chi(X)+1

and
\sigma(\hat{X})=\sigma(X)-1
.
For simplicity, we restrict to the case that
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
is an ACS,
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}=(2\chi+3\sigma)/4
. We set
\hat{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}=({\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},1/2)
, such

\hat{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
) corresponds to an ACS of
\hat{X}
.

We consider the contribution from the cusp at
u=\infty
. We observe from
(
246
) that the leading terms of the
s
-expansion of the measures
\nu_{X}
and
\nu_{\hat{X}}
are equal up to a power of
\eta
,

\nu_{\hat{X}}=\frac{\nu_{X}}{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}}+O(s^{1/2}).

(313)

Let
\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=({\boldsymbol{\mu}},\tilde{\mu})
, where
\tilde{\mu}\in\{0,\frac{1}{2}\}
is the ’t Hooft flux corresponding to the blow-up cycle.
Then,

\Psi^{\hat{J}}_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v\hat{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}\hat{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})=\Psi^{J}_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m},\bar{v}{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})\,\Theta_{\tilde{\mu}}(\tau,v/2),

(314)

where
\hat{J}=(J,0)
and
\Theta_{0}(\tau,z)=\vartheta_{3}(2\tau,2z)
and
\Theta_{1/2}(\tau,z)=\vartheta_{2}(2\tau,2z)
.
Evaluation of the
u
-plane integrals
\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{J}(X)
and
\Phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{\hat{J}}(\hat{X})
for
\ell=0
detailed in Section
6
, gives

\frac{\Phi_{\hat{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{\hat{J}}(\hat{X})}{\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}}}^{J}(X)}=\frac{\Theta_{\tilde{\mu}}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}},

(315)

which is a known relation for the VW partition function

[
15
,
109
,
110
,
111
]
. This
relation also implies a relation for generating functions of
\hat{X}
,

\frac{\Phi_{({\boldsymbol{\mu}},0)}^{\hat{J}}(\hat{X})}{\Phi_{({\boldsymbol{\mu}},\frac{1}{2})}^{\hat{J}}(\hat{X})}=\frac{\theta_{3}(2\tau_{\rm uv})}{\theta_{2}(2\tau_{\rm uv})}.

(316)

Remarkably due to (
314
) and the identity (
67
), this relation holds in fact for the integrand, not just the integral. For more about the blow-up formula in the
\Omega
-background see
[
50
]
.

6
Evaluation of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
for a special period point
J

This section evaluates the
u
-plane integral
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
for a smooth, compact four-manifold
X
with
(b_{1},b_{2}^{+})=(0,1)
, and a convenient choice of period point
J
. Together
with the formula’s for the metric dependence of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
in Section
5.4
, this provides the result for arbitrary
J
.

6.1
Factoring
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}

Our main technique is to choose
J
such that the sum over fluxes

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
factors in a non-holomorphic and holomorphic part.
This will make it possible to determine an anti-derivative for
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
in terms of mock modular forms. The analysis is similar to

[
24
]
, except for the small differences in

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
and the sum over fluxes in
[
24
]
. We
will restrict here to the case of odd lattices. The case of even lattices will be treated in Section
6.5
.

To factor
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
, we choose for the period point,

J=(1,\boldsymbol{0})\in L\otimes\mathbb{R}.

(317)

With this choice,
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
can be expressed as

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}(\tau,\bar{\tau},{\boldsymbol{\rho}},\bar{\boldsymbol{\rho}})=f_{\mu_{1}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho_{1},\bar{\rho}_{1})\,\Theta_{L_{-},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{-}),

(318)

where
{\boldsymbol{\rho}}=v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}=(\rho_{m,1},{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{m,-})
,
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu_{1},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-})
, with
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}\in(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{b_{2}-1}
,
and

f_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})=\exp(-4\pi y\,b^{2})\,\sum_{k\in\mathbb{Z}+\mu}\tfrac{d}{d\bar{\tau}}(\sqrt{4y}\,(k+b))\,\bar{q}^{k^{2}}\,e^{-4\pi i\,\bar{\rho}\,k},

(319)

and

\Theta_{L_{-},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{-})=\sum_{{\boldsymbol{k}}_{-}\in L_{-}+{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}q^{-{\boldsymbol{k}}_{-}^{2}}\,e^{-4\pi iB({\boldsymbol{k}}_{-},{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{-})}.

(320)

For the evaluation of the
u
-plane integral using Stokes’ theorem, we
aim to determine a family of functions
\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};k_{m,1})
, with
k_{m,1}\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}
, which satisfy the following conditions:

1.

f_{\mu}
is the anti-holomorphic derivative of
\widehat{G}_{\mu}
,

f_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},vk_{m,1},\bar{v}k_{m,1})=\frac{d\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};k_{m,1})}{d\bar{\tau}},

(321)

where
d/d\bar{\tau}
also differentiates
\overline{v(\tau)}
.

2.

The 1-form

\Omega=d\tau\,\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})\,\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};k_{m,1})\,\Theta_{L_{-},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau,v{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m,-}),

(322)

is single-valued on
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
.

3.

Since
\omega=d\Omega
on
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
, the derivative to

\bar{\tau}
of
\nu\,\widehat{G}_{\mu}\Theta_{L_{-},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}
exists at
each point of
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
. In particular,
\widehat{G}_{\mu}
has no
singularities on
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
.

We can arrive at such functions using the Appell-Lerch sum
M(\tau,u,v)

(
531
) and its completion (
534
)

[
112
,
113
]
. More generally, the
\widehat{G}_{\mu}
are part of the class of (Heisenberg harmonic) Jacobi-Maass forms

[
114
]
.
16
16

16

Reference
[
115
]
introduced the notion of mock Jacobi form, which is slightly different from the Jacobi-Maass forms used here. One difference is that the completion of a mock Jacobi form does not depend on the anti-holomorphic elliptic variable, whereas this is the case for a Jacobi-Maass form.
To determine
\widehat{G}_{\mu}
, we first
determine a family of auxiliary functions
\widehat{F}_{\mu}
, which satisfy Condition 1. and
2., namely

\begin{split}\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})&=-\,q^{-\nu^{2}}e^{-4\pi i\rho\nu}\left(\,M(2\tau,2\rho+2\mu\tau+1/2,\tau)\right.\\
&\qquad\left.+\tfrac{i}{2}R(2\tau,2\bar{\tau},2\rho+2\nu\tau+1/2,2\bar{\rho}+2\nu\bar{\tau}+1/2)\,\right)\end{split}

(323)

with
\nu=\mu-\frac{1}{2}
. The holomorphic parts
F_{\mu}
of
\widehat{F}_{\mu}
read

\begin{split}F_{0}(\tau,\rho)&=\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}\,q^{n^{2}}}{1+e^{4\pi i\rho}\,q^{2n}},\\
F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,\rho)&=-\frac{e^{2\pi i\rho}}{\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}\,q^{n^{2}-\frac{1}{4}}}{1+e^{4\pi i\rho}\,q^{2n-1}},\end{split}

(324)

and

\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})=F_{\mu}(\tau,\rho)-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\mu}\left(\,\mathrm{sgn}(n)-E(2(n+b)\sqrt{y})\,\right)e^{-4\pi i\rho n}\,q^{-n^{2}},

(325)

where
b=\mathrm{Im}(\rho)/y
.
We then have the desired relation

f_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})=\frac{d\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho})}{d\bar{\tau}},

(326)

where
d/d\bar{\tau}
also differentiates the elliptic variable
\bar{\rho}=\overline{v(\tau)}\,k_{m,1}
. In the following, we abbreviate the first component
k_{m,1}
to
k_{m}
.

To verify that
\widehat{F}_{\mu}
will lead to a well-defined
anti-derivative on the
u
-plane, we determine its transformations under the monodromies
using (
538
) and (
540
). With
k_{m}\in 1/2+\mathbb{Z}
, we have

•

For
M_{\infty}
,

\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},-vk_{m},-\bar{v}k_{m})=-\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},vk_{m},\bar{v}k_{m}),

(327)

where we abbreviated the first component
k_{m,1}
to
k_{m}
. We will use this in the remainder of this subsection.

•

For
M_{1}
,

\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau+2,\bar{\tau}+2,(v-1)k_{m},(\bar{v}-1)k_{m})=\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},vk_{m},\bar{v}k_{m}).

(328)

•

For
M_{2}
,

\begin{split}&\widehat{F}_{\mu}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1},\frac{\bar{\tau}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1},\frac{v-\tau}{-2\tau+1}k_{m},\frac{\bar{v}-\bar{\tau}}{-2\bar{\tau}+1}k_{m}\right)=\\
&\qquad(-2\tau+1)^{1/2}\exp\!\left(2\pi i\frac{(\tau-2v+2v^{2})\,k_{m}^{2}}{-2\tau+1}\right)\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},vk_{m},\bar{v}k_{m}).\end{split}

(329)

This is precisely the required behavior under monodromies. The function
\widehat{F}_{\mu}
is furthermore quasi-periodic with index
-1
,

\begin{split}&\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho+\tau,\bar{\rho}+\bar{\tau})=e^{4\pi i\rho}q\,\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho}),\\
&\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho+1,\bar{\rho}+1)=\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},\rho,\bar{\rho}).\end{split}

(330)

F_{\mu}
has poles for
\rho=1/4
and
\rho=3/4\mod\mathbb{Z}+\tau\mathbb{Z}
, with residue

\begin{split}&\mu=0:\qquad-\frac{1}{4\pi i\,\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)},\\
&\mu=1/2:\qquad\mp\frac{q^{-1/4}}{4\pi\,\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)}.\\
\end{split}

(331)

If we substitute
\rho=zk_{m}
in
\widehat{F}_{\mu}
,
there will be
8k_{m}^{2}
poles as function of
z
in the parallelogram spanned by 2 and

2\tau
.

The third condition is the absence of singularities on

\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
. The functions
F_{\mu}
do not satisfy this
requirement in general. To see this, note that if we replace

\rho=vk_{m}
with
zk_{m}
, for an arbitrary
z\in\mathbb{C}
,
F_{\mu}

has singularities in
z
for

e^{4\pi izk_{m}}=-q^{n},

(332)

with
n
even for
F_{0}
, and
n
odd for
F_{1/2}
. While many of these
poles can not be assumed by the coupling
v
, due to the identity
(
67
), not all poles are contained in the “excluded”’ set (
69
). The non-singular functions
\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};k_{m})
have the same non-holomorphic part as
\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},vk_{m},\bar{v}k_{m})
but a different holomorphic part to avoid the poles.

Starting with
k_{m}=\pm 1/2
, we deduce all poles (
332
)
are excluded by the identity (
67
). Therefore, the

\widehat{F}_{\mu}
are non-singular for these
k_{m}
, and we define

\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};1/2)=\widehat{F}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v/2,\bar{v}/2).

(333)

Moving on to
k_{m}=\pm 3/2
. Now the
F_{\mu}
have singularities in
v
,
which do also lead to singularities on
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
after
substitution
v=v(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})
. To remove the undesirable
singularities, we add meromorphic functions
D_{\mu}
,

\begin{split}&G_{0}(\tau,z;3/2)=F_{0}(\tau,3z/2)+D_{0}(\tau,z),\\
&G_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,z;3/2)=F_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,3z/2)+D_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,z).\end{split}

(334)

with

\begin{split}D_{0}(\tau,z)&=\frac{i\,\eta(2\tau)^{3}\,\vartheta_{1}(2\tau,2z)\,\vartheta_{3}(2\tau,z)}{\vartheta_{2}(2\tau,3z)\,\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)\,\vartheta_{4}(2\tau,2z)\,\vartheta_{2}(2\tau,z)},\\
D_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,z)&=\frac{i\,\eta(2\tau)^{3}\,\vartheta_{1}(2\tau,2z)\,\vartheta_{2}(2\tau,z)}{\vartheta_{3}(2\tau,3z)\,\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)\,\vartheta_{4}(2\tau,2z)\,\vartheta_{3}(2\tau,z)}.\end{split}

(335)

The holomorphic part can be written as

\begin{split}G_{0}(\tau,z;3/2)&=-\frac{1}{2}+q^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{3\pi iz}\,M(2\tau,2z-\tau,\tfrac{1}{2}-z)\\
&=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{q^{-\frac{3}{4}}e^{-5\pi iz}}{\vartheta_{2}(2\tau,z)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{q^{n^{2}+n}e^{2\pi inz}}{1-e^{-4\pi iz}q^{2n-1}},\\
G_{\frac{1}{2}}(\tau,z;3/2)&=M(2\tau,2z-\tau,\tfrac{1}{2}-\tau-z)\\
&=\frac{q^{-\frac{1}{4}}e^{-3\pi iz}}{\vartheta_{3}(2\tau,z)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{q^{n^{2}}e^{2\pi inz}}{1-e^{-4\pi iz}q^{2n-1}}.\end{split}

(336)

These functions have singularities where
\vartheta_{2}(2\tau,z)=0
,

\vartheta_{3}(2\tau,z)=0
, or for
z_{m,n}=(n-\frac{1}{2})\tau+m/2
, with

m,n\in\mathbb{Z}
. All these values are excluded for the coupling
v

by the identity (
67
). Therefore, the
functions
\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,v;3/2)
provide non-singular
functions on
\mathcal{U}_{\varepsilon}
suitable for the evaluation of
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
.
The functions
\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},z,\bar{z};3/2)

transform as a vector-valued modular form
[
116
]
,

\begin{split}\widehat{G}_{\mu}(-1/\tau,z/\tau;3/2)&=\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}(-i\tau)^{1/2}\,\exp(-18\pi iz^{2}/4)\\
&\quad\times\sum_{\nu=0,\frac{1}{2}}e^{4\pi i\mu\nu}\widehat{G}_{\nu}(\tau,z).\end{split}

(337)

On the other hand, the
\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},z,\bar{z};1/2)

do not transform as a 2-dimensional vector-valued modular form, but are part of a longer of a higher-dimensional vector. Nevertheless, we will confirm in the next subsection that the integral
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
does transform as (
276
).

Generic
k_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2

We briefly comment on more general
k_{m}\in\mathbb{Z}+1/2
. We note
that a suitable anti-derivative can be found, since we are free to
chose functions with singularities in the “allowed” set
(
69
). For sufficiently high degree of the poles such functions do
exist. We leave finding suitable examples for future work.

6.2
Contribution from
\infty
for odd intersection form

This section determines the contribution to
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
from

u=\infty
, which confirms the anomaly (
291
). This contribution is given by

\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\infty}^{J}=\lim_{\varepsilon\to 0}\oint_{|\tau-\tau_{\rm uv}|=\varepsilon}d\tau\,\nu(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv},m)\,\widehat{G}_{\mu_{1}}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};k_{m,1})\,\Theta_{L_{-},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{-}).

(338)

{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure equal to an almost complex structure

We first consider the case that the
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure corresponds to an
almost complex structure such that
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}^{2}=(2\chi+3\sigma)/4
, and
in particular
k_{m,1}=3/2
. We
deduce from (
246
) that the
u
-plane integral is determined
by the term proportional to
\sqrt{-\pi i(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})}
in the expansion of

\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};3/2)\,\Theta_{L_{-},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau,{\boldsymbol{\rho}}_{-})
around

\tau_{\rm uv}
. Given the behavior of
v
in this limit
(
245
), and since
\widehat{G}_{\mu}
is odd as function of

v
while
\Theta_{L_{-},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}
is even, we realize that this is determined by the

v^{1}
term of the Taylor expansion in
v
of
\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};3/2)
. Let
\widehat{g}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{z}\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},z,\bar{z};3/2)|_{z=0}
. The holomorphic part,
g_{\mu}(\tau)=-\frac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{z}G_{\mu}(\tau,z;3/2)|_{z=0}
is

\begin{split}g_{0}(\tau)&=-\frac{3}{\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}\,q^{n^{2}+2n}}{(1+q^{2n})^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\vartheta_{3}(2\tau)^{3}\\
&=-\frac{1}{4}+\frac{3}{2}\,q+3\,q^{2}+4\,q^{3}+\frac{9}{2}\,q^{4}+6\,q^{5}+O(q^{6}),\\
g_{1/2}(\tau)&=-\frac{3}{\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}\,q^{n^{2}+2n-\frac{5}{4}}}{(1+q^{2n-1})^{2}}+\frac{1}{2}\vartheta_{2}(2\tau)^{3}\\
&=q^{\frac{3}{4}}(1+3\,q+3\,q^{2}+6\,q^{3}+3\,q^{4}+O(q^{5})).\end{split}

(339)

These functions contain interesting arithmetic information; they are generating functions of the Hurwitz class numbers
H(n)
,

g_{\mu}(\tau)=3\sum_{n\geq 0}H(4n-2\mu)\,q^{n-\mu/2}.

(340)

We express
\widehat{g}_{\mu}
as a holomorphic
q
-series plus a
non-holomorphic part

\widehat{g}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=g_{\mu}(\tau)-\frac{3i}{4\sqrt{2}\pi}\int_{-\bar{\tau}}^{i\infty}\frac{\Theta_{\mu}(u)}{(-i(\tau+u))^{3/2}}\,du,

(341)

with
\Theta_{\mu}
defined in (
293
).

The behavior of
\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};3/2)
for
\tau
near
\tau_{\rm uv}
is therefore

\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},v,\bar{v};3/2)=-2\sqrt{-\pi i(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})}\,\widehat{g}_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})+O((\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})^{3/2},(\bar{\tau}-\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})^{1/2}).

(342)

The subleading terms in the
(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}-\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})
-expansion do not contribute to the contour integral of
\tau
around

\tau_{\rm uv}
. We therefore find that the contribution to
\Phi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}

from this boundary is

\Phi_{{\boldsymbol{\mu}},\infty}^{J}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})=\frac{\widehat{g}_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})\,\Theta_{L_{-},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}(\tau_{\rm uv})}{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2\chi}},

(343)

with
{\boldsymbol{\mu}}=(\mu,{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-})
. This is compatible with the appropriate
specialization of the holomorphic anomaly equations (
291
)
and (
292
) derived earlier.

For manifolds with
b_{2}=1
, such as
\mathbb{P}^{2}
,
\Theta_{L_{-},{\boldsymbol{\mu}}_{-}}=1
, and we find agreement with the partition function (
393
) of Vafa-Witten theory
[
15
]
.
The
q_{\rm uv}
-series perfectly agree, which is
expected from our discussion in Section
3.4
for
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
an almost
complex structure. Note that for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
, the class numbers have an interpretation as a count of the number of toric fixed points of rank 2 bundles
[
36
]
We will expand more on this agreement in Subsection
6.6
.

{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure different from an almost complex structure

We next proceed with a different
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure, namely

k_{m,1}=1/2
, which provides a non-trivial test for
S
-duality transformations of Section
5.2
.

To express the partition functions, we define
\widehat{g}^{(n)}_{\mu}(\tau,k_{m})
by

\widehat{g}^{(n)}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau};k_{m})=(-2\pi i)^{n}\partial^{n}_{z}\widehat{G}_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},z,\bar{z};k_{m})|_{z=\bar{z}=0},

(344)

and similarly for
g^{(n)}_{\mu}
in terms of
G_{\mu}
. The functions
vanish for
n
even. For
n=1
, we have

\begin{split}g^{(1)}_{0}(\tau;1/2)&=-\frac{1}{\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}\,q^{n^{2}+2n}}{(1+q^{2n})^{2}}\\
&=-\frac{1}{4}-\frac{1}{2}q-q^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\,q^{4}-2\,q^{5}-2\,q^{6}+O(q^{7}),\\
g^{(1)}_{1/2}(\tau;1/2)&=-\frac{1}{\vartheta_{4}(2\tau)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}\,q^{n^{2}+2n-\frac{5}{4}}}{(1+q^{2n-1})^{2}}\\
&=q^{\frac{3}{4}}(-1+q-3\,q^{2}+2\,q^{3}-3\,q^{4}+3\,q^{5}+O(q^{6})),\end{split}

(345)

The function
g^{(1)}_{1/2}(\tau)
appears in Ramanujan’s Lost Notebook

[
117
]
. The coefficients
c_{\mu}(m)
of

g^{(1)}_{\mu}
can be expressed as

\begin{split}c_{0}(m)&=4\,H(m)-H(4m)\\
&=H(4m)-\frac{1}{6}r_{3}(m),\\
c_{1/2}(m-1/4)&=H(4m-1)-\frac{1}{6}r_{3}(4m-1),\end{split}

(346)

where
H(m)
is the Hurwitz class number and
r_{3}(m)
is the number
of representations of
n
as the sum of three squares.

We note that the coefficients of
f_{2\mu}
differ from those of

G_{\mu}
(
346
) by the term with
r_{3}(4n-2\mu)
. More
precisely, we have for
k_{m}=3/2

g^{(1)}_{\mu}(\tau;1/2)=\frac{1}{3}g_{\mu}(\tau)-\frac{1}{6}\,\Theta_{\mu}(\tau)^{3},

(347)

with
\Theta_{\mu}
as in (
293
). The transformations (
515
) and (
516
) demonstrate that
\widehat{g}^{(1)}_{\mu}(\tau;1/2)
does not transform as a 2-dimensional vector-valued modular form, but of a higher dimensional vector.

Similarly,
G_{\mu}(\tau,\bar{\tau},z,\bar{z};1/2)
does not
transform as a modular form.
For
n=3
, the first coefficients of
g^{(3)}_{\mu}
are

\begin{split}g^{(3)}_{0}(\tau;1/2)&=\tfrac{1}{8}(1+2\,q+4\,q^{2}+24\,q^{3}+46\,q^{4}-40\,q^{5}+O(q^{6})),\\
g^{(3)}_{1/2}(\tau;1/2)&=\tfrac{1}{4}q^{\frac{3}{4}}(-1+25\,q-75\,q^{2}+196\,q^{3}-339\,q^{4}+O(q^{5}))\\
g^{(3)}_{0}(\tau;3/2)&=\tfrac{1}{8}(1+18\,q+324\,q^{2}+1208\,q^{3}+2862\,q^{4}+5400\,q^{5}+O(q^{6})),\\
g^{(3)}_{1/2}(\tau;3/2)&=\tfrac{1}{4}q^{\frac{3}{4}}(1+99\,q+459\,q^{2}+1206\,q^{3}+2259\,q^{4}+O(q^{5})).\end{split}

(348)

For simplicity, we specialize to
X=\mathbb{P}^{2}
and
k_{m}=1/2
. Together with
k_{m}=3/2
, this is the only value of
k_{m}
for which
\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
is non-vanishing. For
k_{m}=1/2
, we find that
\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
takes the form,

\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv})=\frac{\widehat{g}_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv},\bar{\tau}_{\rm uv};1/2)}{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}},

(349)

where the non-holomorphic part of
\widehat{g}_{1/2}
follows from (
294
), and
the holomorphic part is given by

g_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};1/2)=-\tfrac{2}{2\pi i}\partial_{\tau_{\rm uv}}g^{(1)}_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};1/2)+\frac{2}{3}\,g^{(3)}_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};1/2)+\frac{4}{3}\,E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,g^{(1)}_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};1/2),

(350)

which gives the following first few coefficients

\begin{split}g_{0}(\tau_{\rm uv};1/2)&=3\,q_{\rm uv}+14\,q_{\rm uv}^{2}+30\,q_{\rm uv}^{3}+54\,q_{\rm uv}^{4}+84\,q_{\rm uv}^{5}+124\,q_{\rm uv}^{6}+\dots,\\
g_{1/2}(\tau_{\rm uv};1/2)&=q_{\rm uv}^{3/4}(1+9\,q_{\rm uv}+19\,q_{\rm uv}^{2}+50\,q_{\rm uv}^{3}+51\,q_{\rm uv}^{4}+131\,q_{\rm uv}^{5}+\dots).\end{split}

(351)

Since
g_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};1/2)
is expressed in terms of quantities for
k_{m}=1/2
, it is a priori unclear whether
the modular completion of this expression satisfies the expected
S
-duality diagram. However, comparison of the first
q_{\rm uv}
-expansion demonstrates that
g_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};1/2)
can be expressed in terms of the functions for

k_{m}=3/2
, which do transform as a 2-dimensional vector-valued modular forms after completion. This reads

\begin{split}g_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};1/2)&=\frac{10}{9}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\partial_{\tau_{\rm uv}}g^{(1)}_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};3/2)+\frac{2}{9}g^{(3)}_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};3/2)\\
&\quad+\frac{1}{9}E_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})g^{(1)}_{\mu}(\tau_{\rm uv};3/2),\end{split}

(352)

and confirms the duality diagram
3
. We
leave an in depth analysis of the modular properties for future work.

6.3
Including point observables for
\mathbb{P}^{2}

We continue by studying correlation functions for point
observables for
\mathbb{P}^{2}
. For comparison with
N_{f}=0
, we consider the correlation
functions
\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}[u_{\rm D}^{n}]=\left<u_{\rm D}^{n}\right>^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{\mu}
with
u_{\rm D}
as in
(
209
). In terms of the exponentiated observable

e^{pu_{\rm D}/\Lambda^{2}}
, we have

\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}[u_{\rm D}^{n}/\Lambda^{2n}]=\frac{\partial^{n}}{\partial p^{n}}\left.\,\Phi_{\mu}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}\!\left[e^{p\,u_{\rm D}/\Lambda^{2}}\right]\right|_{p=0}.

(353)

The correlation functions of the
N_{f}=0

theory equal Donaldson invariants. For an easy comparison, we
introduce the dimensionless correlation functions of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory,

\underline{\Phi}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{\mu}[u_{\rm D}^{n}/(2\Lambda^{2})^{n}]=R_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}(3,2k_{m})\,\,\Phi^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{\mu}[u_{\rm D}^{n}/(2\Lambda^{2})^{n}],

(354)

where
R_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}
is the factor defined in (
207
) relating

\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
to
N_{f}=0
. In the
N_{f}=0
limit,
(2\Lambda)^{2n}
becomes
\Lambda_{0}^{2n}
, such that we arrive at
the appropriate point observable in Donaldson theory.

We list the first terms of the holomorphic parts of
\underline{\Phi}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}_{\mu}
(
354
) for
k_{m}=3/2,1/2
and
0\leq n\leq 4
.
Tables
1
and
2
give the first
few coefficients for
\mu=0
,
\mu=1/2
and
k_{m}=3/2
. For
k_{m}=1/2
, we list observables in Tables
3
and

4
. Up to a factor of
\Lambda^{3\ell}
, the series match with
the formal series (
154
), which demonstrates the geometric
meaning of the coefficients in the
q_{\rm uv}
-series.

Let us consider the limit to pure SW theory.
For
k_{m}=3/2
and
\mu=1/2
, the
N_{f}=0
limit of the series in Table
2
reproduces exactly the
known expressions for Donaldson invariants

[
118
,
24
]
.
On the other hand, the expressions for
\mu=0
in Table
1
diverge for all
n
, while the Donaldson invariant vanishes. We expect that this mismatch is due to
contributions from reducible connections, which are strictly semi-stable. The situation is understood for
n=0
, which is discussed in detail in Section
6.6
: The
q_{\rm uv}
-series for
n=0
is the generating series of weighted Euler characteristics, which receive a contribution from reducible connections. Due to this contribution, the coefficients of the
q_{\rm uv}
-series starts with the constant term
-\frac{1}{4}q_{\rm uv}^{0}
, causing the divergence. Equation (
394
) is the
q_{\rm uv}
-series after correcting for the contribution from reducible connections. For this series, the limit to
N_{f}=0
clearly vanishes. We do not have a similar quantitative understanding for the
q_{\rm uv}
-series with
n>0
at this time, but we expect that this can developed.

For the case of
k_{m}=1/2
, we find that the
N_{f}=0
limit of the expressions in Tables
3
and

4
do reproduce the Donaldson invariants for both
\mu=0
and
\mu=1/2
.

n

Hol. part of
\underline{\Phi}_{0}^{\mathbb{P}^{2}}[u_{\rm D}^{n}/(2\Lambda^{2})^{n}]

0
