8
A broad-brush sketch of the generalization to class S theories

The topological twist of Donaldson-Witten theory
[
1
]
is based purely
on the structure of the
d=4,\mathcal{N}=2
superalgebra and as such can
be implemented in any theory with
d=4,\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetry. Given the
spectacular applications of
SU(2)
supersymmetric Yang-Mills theory (without
matter hypermultiplets) to the theory of four-manifolds it is natural to ask
if anything else can be learned from
topological twists of other
d=4,\mathcal{N}=2
field theories.

We should distinguish between “old invariants” and “new invariants.”
By “old invariants” we mean both the
the cohomology ring
H^{*}(X,\mathbb{Z})
and the Donaldson/Seiberg-Witten
invariants. The cohomology ring characterizes the homotopy
type of
X

[
132
]
. Thanks to the Witten conjecture
(see section 7 above) the Donaldson invariants can be
expressed in terms of the Seiberg-Witten invariants and hence
do not carry new information. The Seiberg-Witten invariants
can distinguish between four-manifolds which are homotopy equivalent
but not diffeomorphic. In 1994 they were new and revolutionary invariants.
Nevertheless, it is clear that there are
many aspects of smooth four-dimensional topology which are not
accessible via the Seiberg-Witten invariants. By “new invariants”
we mean smooth invariants of
X
which cannot be computed
just knowing the cohomology ring and the Seiberg-Witten invariants.

18
18

18

There is an interesting
refinement of the Seiberg-Witten invariants known as
Bauer-Furuta invariants. But we would include these in the
list of “old invariants.”

There are two kinds of new things one might learn from the study of other
topologically twisted
d=4,\mathcal{N}=2
theories. First, one might learn new
facts about the old invariants. This has in fact happened
via the study of
SU(2)
with one fundamental quark hypermultiplet
[
133
,
134
,
135
]
,
where the existence of a superconformal theory with compact moduli space
led to sum rules on Seiberg-Witten invariants.
It is entirely possible that there are other new things to be learned
from other
d=4,\mathcal{N}=2
theories.
Second, one might discover new four-manifold invariants
in the sense described above.

19
19

19

This question was perhaps first raised in
[
5
]
and was the central motivation for
[
87
,
133
,
134
,
88
]

It was also addressed in
[
136
,
137
,
138
,
139
]
.

One way to search for the new invariants is to try to write the partition function
(or generator of topological correlators) in terms of
H^{*}(X;\mathbb{Z})
and the Seiberg-Witten
invariants. As explained at length in the present paper, the correlators of twisted
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}

are all constructed out of the old invariants.
The results of
[
140
,
87
]
show that for
SU(N)
theories without hypermultiplets
the topological correlators can again be written in terms of the old invariants. This result
suggests that in fact all Lagrangian theories will
ultimately be expressed in terms of the old invariants.
We stress that this is
suggested
by the results
[
87
]
but
certainly there is no proof: At the moment the idea that the topological correlators
of Lagrangian theories are always expressible in terms of the old invariants is just folklore.
We also stress again that even if this conjecture proves to be correct the
topological correlation functions of these
theories might teach us interesting lessons about physics, and might also lead to the
discovery of interesting new facts about the known invariants.

The above considerations give a very natural motivation to try to investigate the topological correlation functions of class S theories. Since class S theories are in general not Lagrangian theories, there is
no obvious “UV formulation” of the invariants in terms of intersection theory on moduli spaces of solutions to PDE’s
generalizing the instanton equation. That certainly leaves open the possibility for something really new.
This in turn motivates the study of the Coulomb branch integral of these theories. Focusing on the
Coulomb branch integral makes sense because, as we will see, on the one hand it is something well within reach
using present technology, and yet the method of Section
7
of this paper

20
20

20

also used in
[
5
,
87
,
17
,
88
]

asserts that the full topological correlation functions can be derived from the Coulomb branch integral alone.
The Coulomb branch integral, while subtle and complicated, is expected to depend only on the classical four-manifold invariants. The main question is whether the analog of the condition
(
407
) for cancellation of wall-crossing at finite loci within the Coulomb branch can be implemented using only
Seiberg-Witten invariants. If that is the case, then we expect a generalization of the Witten conjecture
to hold even in these general class S cases. If it is not the case, then there is room for something
really new. Thus the wall-crossing behavior of the Coulomb branch integral is of central importance. If the wall-crossing in strong coupling regimes
always happens at walls corresponding to
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structures then a
complete invariant can be formed using just the Seiberg-Witten invariants.

The result of
[
88
]
for the simplest of the Argyres-Douglas theories (sometimes called the
(A_{1},A_{2})
theory)
casts some doubt on the hope that twisted class S topological correlation functions will provide new invariants, but it
remains an open possibility that this Argyres-Douglas theory is an exceptional case. Therefore, we believe
that the formulation of the Coulomb branch integral for general class
S
theories is an interesting, and
potentially important, open problem. The results of the present paper, along with
[
87
,
5
]
,
constitute important benchmarks for general results for this problem.

In what follows we will give a broad-brush sketch of some of the most obvious
issues that arise in trying to formulate the Coulomb branch measure of
class S theories. In contrast to the rest of this paper, where we have taken
pains to be meticulous about every detail of our computations, the discussion
in this section is broad-brush and programmatic, and we will skate over many
subtleties and details - some of which might be quite important in the final
formulation.

8.1
Setting notation for class S theories

First let us recall the definition of a class S theory
[
141
,
142
,
143
,
144
]
.
For more extended discussions see
[
145
,
146
]
.
Given an ADE Lie algebra
\mathfrak{g}
, a Riemann surface
\mathcal{C}
, without
boundary or punctures, but equipped with “puncture data”
D
, one can define a four-dimensional

d=4
,
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric QFT denoted
S[\mathfrak{g},\mathcal{C},D]
. One considers the six-dimensional
(2,0)
theory

[
147
,
148
,
149
]
on
\mathcal{C}\times X

with “defect data”
D
. (In some cases, these represent transverse five-branes.)
The data
D
consists of a divisor
Supp(D)=\sum_{\alpha}p_{\alpha}
on
\mathcal{C}

and choices of orbits in
\mathfrak{g}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{C}
at each point
p_{\alpha}
. The data of the divisor can be thought of as boundary
conditions for a
\mathfrak{g}_{\mathbb{C}}
-Hitchin system on
\mathcal{C}
with singularities at
Supp(D)
. See

[
150
,
151
,
141
,
142
]
and references therein for detailed discussions.
One then performs a partial topological twist known as the class S twist
[
142
]
. Then
certain physical quantities are formally independent of the Kähler structure on
\mathcal{C}
and one can take the length scales on
\mathcal{C}
to zero.

21
21

21

In a small set of exceptional cases this limit will not be well-defined
[
152
]
.

The Coulomb branch of the resulting four-dimensional theory is well-studied. It is the base of a Hitchin moduli space and
can be identified with the affine space

\mathcal{B}=\oplus_{i}H^{0}(\mathcal{C},K_{\mathcal{C}}^{\otimes d_{i}}\otimes\Delta_{i})

(478)

where
d_{i}
are the exponents of
\mathfrak{g}
and
\Delta_{i}
is a sheaf describing the pole structures of symmetric differentials at the support

p_{\alpha}
of the defects.
The spectral curve
\Sigma\subset T^{*}\mathcal{C}
is identified with the Seiberg-Witten curve and the Seiberg-Witten differential is then just
the pullback of the Liouville form. The physics of the theory on
X
can be described
by the decoupling limit of a single M-theory fivebrane wrapped on
\Sigma\times X

[
144
]
.

8.2
Topological twisting of general
d=4,\mathcal{N}=2
theories

One central aspect of the present paper is that in the presence of matter extra
data must be provided even to define the topological twisting of the theory.
The Donaldson-Witten twist itself
in general will not suffice and we will need to introduce extra data generalizing the
ultraviolet
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure
c_{{\rm uv}}

that plays a significant role in the present paper.

To illustrate this general point let us first consider the topological twisting of the general Lagrangian
d=4,\mathcal{N}=2
theory.
The basic data of such a theory is a choice of compact semisimple gauge group
G
(defining the
vectormultiplets) together with a quaternionic representation
\mathcal{R}
of
G
(defining the hypermultiplets).
The representation is equipped with a hyperkähler metric such that
G
is a subgroup of the
group of hyperkähler isometries. A choice of
\mathcal{N}=1
sub-algebra
determines a choice of complex structure on
\mathcal{R}
. In that case the
\mathcal{N}=1
D-term is written in terms of the
real moment map. The holomorphic symplectic form defines
a moment map which, when contracted with the
\mathcal{N}=1
chiral multiplet in the
\mathcal{N}=2
vectormultiplet gives the
\mathcal{N}=1
superpotential.
The “flavor group,” a.k.a. the group of global symmetries

G^{f}
is the group of hyperkähler isometries commuting with
G
. It can be gauged
using external vectormultiplets. The vev’s of scalars in these vectormultiplets are mass terms.
All together,
\mathcal{R}
is a representation of

\rho:G\times G^{f}\times SU(2)_{R}

(479)

If we choose an isomorphism
\mathcal{R}\cong\mathbb{H}^{n}
then
SU(2)_{R}
is right action by
unit quaternions while
\rho(G\times G^{f})
acts on the left.

When we couple the theory to background fields we choose

1.

A principal
G/Z(G)
-bundle with connection, denoted
(P\to X,\nabla)
. Here
Z(G)
is the
center of
G
.

2.

A principal
SO(3)_{R}
“R-symmetry bundle” with connection
\nabla_{R}
. The Donaldson-Witten twist
means we choose an isomorphism with the structure group of the bundle of self-dual
two forms such that
\nabla_{R}
is identified with the Levi-Civita connection.

3.

A principal bundle with connection for global symmetries. We will be more precise about this below.

For simplicity, we henceforth assume
G
is simply connected.
In the
G
-gauge theory all of these fields are external, nondynamical fields except for the connection
\nabla
on
P
.

Topological twisting identifies, locally,
SU(2)_{R}
with a factor
SU(2)_{+}

of the spin lift of the local frame group
Spin(4)\cong SU(2)_{+}\times SU(2)_{-}
. On non-spin manifolds we cannot define a spin
SU(2)_{+}
principal bundle globally. However, there does exist a principal bundle for
SO(3)_{+}=SU(2)_{+}/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
. As we have said, it can be taken to be the structure group of the bundle of self-dual 2-forms on
X
.
Choosing a complex structure,
\mathcal{R}
is of the form
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}\otimes\textbf{2}

where
\mathcal{R}^{\prime}
is a representation of
G\times G^{f}
and
2
is the fundamental
of
SU(2)_{R}
. Accordingly, when we make a topological twist on a non-spin manifold
we might not be able to define the associated bundle with fiber
\mathcal{R}
.
We can define an element of a twisted
K
-theory of
X
, but not an honest bundle.
By the same token, in the presence of nonzero ’t Hooft flux the gauge bundle
P
is a

G_{\rm adj}=G/Z(G)
-principal bundle that will not lift to a principal
G
-bundle.
If
\mathcal{R}
transforms nontrivially under
Z(G)
then the associated bundle will not
exist, but it will define an element of a twisted
K
-theory of
X
.

In both of these cases, the twisting is torsion and its isomorphism class
is characterized by an element of
H^{2}(X,U(1))
. The basic criterion for a
sensible theory is that
the total twisting of
\mathcal{R}
should vanish, so that the associated
bundle exists as an honest bundle.

To put this in more concrete terms, suppose we choose an atlas
\{\mathcal{U}_{\alpha}\}
for
X

and a trivialization of our putative
G,G^{f}
and
SU(2)_{+}
bundles relative to this atlas.
There are transition functions
g_{\alpha\beta}^{\rm gauge}
for the gauge bundle, while there
will be analogous transition functions
g_{\alpha\beta}^{\rm flavor}
for the global symmetry bundle,
and finally
g_{\alpha\beta}^{s}
for the spin bundle. In all three cases the
bundle will exist locally, but not globally: On triple overlaps of patches
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}

the cocycle condition might fail. But it only fails by a phase:

\begin{split}g_{\alpha\beta}^{\rm gauge}g_{\beta\gamma}^{\rm gauge}g_{\gamma\alpha}^{\rm gauge}&=\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\rm gauge}1_{G}\\
g_{\alpha\beta}^{\rm flavor}g_{\beta\gamma}^{\rm flavor}g_{\gamma\alpha}^{\rm flavor}&=\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\rm flavor}1_{G^{f}}\\
g_{\alpha\beta}^{s}g_{\beta\gamma}^{s}g_{\gamma\alpha}^{s}&=\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{s}1_{SU(2)}\\
\end{split}

(480)

In all three cases
\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{x}
is a function from
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}
to
U(1)
.
(Note that
\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{s}
is valued in the subgroup
\{\pm 1\}\subset U(1)
.)
In global terms
\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\rm gauge}
is a cocycle representing the ’t Hooft flux

w_{2}(P)\in H^{2}(X,Z(G))
of the gauge bundle, while
\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{s}
is a cocycle
representing
w_{2}(X)
. If
\mathcal{R}_{a}
is an irreducible representation of
G\times G^{f}\times SU(2)_{R}
of
the form
\rho_{a}\otimes\rho_{a}^{f}\otimes\rho_{fund}
then
the phases act on
\mathcal{R}_{a}
by the function
\mathcal{U}_{\alpha\beta\gamma}\to U(1)
given by the phase multiplying the identity operator on
\mathcal{R}_{a}
in:

\rho_{a}(\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\rm gauge}1_{G})\rho_{a}^{f}(\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\rm flavor}1_{G^{f}})\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{s}~.

(481)

The criterion we must impose is that for every irreducible component of
\mathcal{R}
this cocyle is
trivializable in cochains with values in
U(1)
.

We illustrate these general considerations with two examples:

1.

In
SU(2)
theory , with
N_{f}
fundamental flavors the twisting of the bundle associated
to
\mathcal{R}
from the doublet of
SU(2)_{R}
is
w_{2}(X)
. Therefore, we must choose

w_{2}(P)=w_{2}(X)
to cancel the twisting
[
5
]
. This means the
cocycle
\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\rm gauge}\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{s}
is
trivializable. The potential dependence of the invariants on a choice of trivialization
has not been explored.

2.

In the case of
SU(2)

\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
investigated in the present paper

\rho_{a}(\zeta_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{\rm gauge})=1
since the center of
SU(2)

acts trivially in the adjoint representation. Thus the twisting must be canceled by
a different mechanism. In our case
G^{f}
contains a
U(1)
factor, but the
U(1)\times SU(2)_{R}

action on
\mathcal{R}
descends to a
\left(U(1)\times SU(2)_{R}\right)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-action. But now
we can choose a homomorphism

{\rm Spin}^{c}(4)\to\left(U(1)\times SU(2)_{R}\right)/\mathbb{Z}_{2}

(482)

given by

(u_{1},u_{2})\rightarrow[\left(\sqrt{\det u_{1}},\frac{u_{2}}{\sqrt{\det u_{2}}})\right)]~.

(483)

This allows us to define
\mathcal{R}
as an honest
representation of
{\rm Spin}^{c}(4)
.
When we make a choice of
{\rm Spin}^{c}(4)
-structure and use the topological
twisting there is an honest associated bundle to the
SO(3)_{\rm gauge}\times{\rm Spin}^{c}(4)

principal bundle over
X
with fiber
\mathcal{R}
.

It is clear that there are many generalizations of the two ideas mentioned above.
For example, if
G^{f}=U(1)\times{\tilde{G}^{f}}
we could choose a principal
{\tilde{G}^{f}}/Z({\tilde{G}^{f}})

bundle with ’t Hooft flux for the external gauge fields and decompose
\mathcal{R}

as a representation of
G\times{\tilde{G}^{f}}
:

\mathcal{R}\cong\oplus_{\alpha,a}D_{\alpha,a}\otimes\rho_{\alpha}\otimes\rho_{a}^{f}

(484)

where
\rho_{a}^{f}
are the irreps of
{\tilde{G}^{f}}
. We twisting of
\rho_{a}^{f}

could cancel that of
\rho_{\alpha}
while we use the
U(1)
factor
and the homomorphism (
482
) to cancel the twisting from

w_{2}(X)
. One could generalize further. On top of this
one could consider other topological twistings based on
other homomorphisms
SU(2)\to G^{f}\times SU(2)_{+}
, as in the multiple
twists of
\mathcal{N}=4
SYM. We will not
try to give a comprehensive treatment of all such twistings, although it
would be interesting to do so.
We will content ourselves with some brief remarks on the class S theories
of
A_{1}
type, all of which are Lagrangian. For simplicity we restrict
attention to regular punctures.

The class S theories of
A_{1}
-type on a curve
\mathcal{C}
with genus
g

with puncture data
D
having support at
n
punctures
p_{\alpha}

can be written as Lagrangian theories in different ways in different
weak coupling regimes. These regimes are determined by a choice of pants
decomposition of
\mathcal{C}

[
141
]
. The (four-dimensional) gauge group is

G=SU(2)^{3g-3+n}
. The matter fields are a collection of
half-hypermultiplets
\Phi_{c_{1},c_{2},c_{3}}
labeled by unordered triples of
cutting curves of the pants decomposition. (We refer to the curves
surrounding punctures as “cutting curves” as well.) It is useful
to distinguish internal cutting curves
c_{i}
associated with gauge groups
from external curves
c_{\alpha}
associated with punctures.

The principal
SO(3)
bundles
P_{c_{i}}
associated with internal cuts have
twistings
\tau_{i}=w_{2}(P_{c_{i}})
. For each trinion with three internal
cutting curves
\Phi_{c_{i},c_{j},c_{k}}
we require

\tau_{i}+\tau_{j}+\tau_{k}+w_{2}(X)=0\in H^{2}(X,\mathbb{Z}_{2})

(485)

For each trinion with a single external puncture
\Phi_{c_{\alpha,c_{i},c_{j}}}
there is a global
U(1)
symmetry (with nonzero mass) so
we choose a
{\rm Spin}^{c}
-structure
\mathfrak{s}_{\alpha}
and use the homomorphism
(
482
). Then we require
\tau_{i}=\tau_{j}
to cancel
the twisting. For trinions with two external punctures there are a variety of
options for canceling the twisting. Putting these rules together one can
see that there is a large set of choices for topologically twisting a class S
theory of type
A_{1}
. How these different twistings interact with the S-duality group is an interesting question
beyond the scope of the present paper.

As far as we know, the topological twisting of general class S theories
has not been written down. The above considerations should make it plain that in general we do expect extra data needs to be specified to define the topological twisting of a general
d=4,\mathcal{N}=2
theory. Filling this gap in the literature presents an interesting challenge since
in general these theories are not Lagrangian.

8.3
The class S Coulomb branch integral

A central idea we are proposing here is that the partition vector of the M5 tensor multiplet on
M_{6}=\Sigma\times X
,
where we perform the Donaldson-Witten twist on
X
(generalized, as per above) and the class
S
twist on
\Sigma
, will
define the Coulomb branch measure on
\mathcal{B}
. (This raises a natural question: Why should the partition vector
of the tensor multiplet naturally define a
measure
on
\mathcal{B}
? ) Thus we expect
a generalized expression of the form

\Phi_{\mu}=\int_{\mathcal{B}}[dud\bar{u}]\,\mathcal{H}\cdot\Psi_{\mu}~.

(486)

Here
[dud\bar{u}]
is the natural translation-invariant measure on
\mathcal{B}
, while

\mu
labels a basis for a Stone-von Neumann representation
of a Heisenberg extension of
H^{3}(M_{6};\Lambda_{wt}(\mathfrak{g})/\Lambda_{rt}(\mathfrak{g}))
.
We expect
\mathcal{H}
to be metric-independent and holomorphic, although duality-frame dependent. We also expect
\Psi_{\mu}

to be metric-dependent, non-holomorphic, and duality-frame dependent. The measure will be singular at the discriminant locus in

\mathcal{B}
, where physical BPS states become massless. Let
\mathcal{B}^{*}
denote the complement in
\mathcal{B}
of this discriminant locus.
A crucial and highly nontrivial criterion on (
486
)
is that the measure should be single-valued, without having to pass to some cover of
\mathcal{B}^{*}
.

22
22

22

In this paper we have used the criterion of single-valuedness as a crucial check on our formula for the Coulomb branch measure. But, given the measure, it could be turned around as a potentially useful criterion on the physical validity of a proposed Coulomb branch in general
d=4,\mathcal{N}=2
theories. In this way the criterion of single-valuedness might be a useful supplement to the criteria used in the program of
[
153
,
154
]
. See
[
155
]
for some preliminary comments along these lines.

We will now review a few of the
more obvious expected properties of
\mathcal{H}
and
\Psi_{\mu}
from the six-, four-, and two-dimensional viewpoints on
M_{6}
,
X
, and

\Sigma
, respectively.

8.4
Six-dimensional perspective

The “partition vector” of the tensor-multiplet theory on
X\times\Sigma
has been discussed in

[
156
,
157
,
158
,
159
,
160
,
161
,
162
,
163
,
164
,
165
]

and elsewhere. It has the form:

Z^{TM}_{\mu}=\mathcal{D}\sum_{x\in\Lambda}\Omega_{\mu}(x)e^{-\frac{\pi\kappa}{2}\int H_{x}*H_{x}}

(487)

where
\Lambda\subset H^{3}(M_{6};\mathbb{Z})
is a maximal Lagrangian sublattice,

23
23

23

We are assuming here that the relevant generalized cohomology
theory for the quantization of the self-dual tensormultiplet is the Eilenberg-Maclane spectrum.

\Omega_{\mu}
is a quadratic refinement of the intersection form (QRIF),
defining the holomorphic line bundle over the space of flat external
C
-fields. Also,

H_{x}
is a harmonic representative
of the class
x
and
\mathcal{D}
is the “determinant of quadratic fluctuations” - a suitable section of a
product of determinant line bundles. The contribution from the self-dual tensor field is
given by Cheeger’s half-torsion
[
156
,
159
]
and the scalar and fermion fields give standard determinant
line bundles and sections of those line bundles. Finally,

\kappa
is a constant defining the “level” of the self-dual system, analogous to the
level of the chiral scalar field in two dimensions. (Roughly speaking, the theta function
will have level
\kappa/2
.)

The most subtle component of the partition function is the QRIF.
The quadratic refinement for the smallest level (sometimes called “level 1/2”)
depends on a choice of Wu structure of
M_{6}
, i.e. a trivialization of
w_{4}
on

W_{8}
where
\partial W_{8}=S^{1}\times M_{6}
. Descriptions can be found in

[
156
,
158
,
163
,
164
]
. Here we are considering the
case where
M_{6}=\Sigma\times X
with
X
not necessarily spin. To define the full tensormultiplet
we do not need a spin structure on
M_{6}
, but we do require a spin structure on

TM_{6}\oplus N
where
N
is the R-symmetry bundle with structure group
SO(5)
. It suffices to make a
choice of
{\rm Spin^{c}}
structure on
TM_{6}
and
N
so that
w_{2}(TM_{6}\oplus N)=0
.
Fortunately a choice of
{\rm Spin^{c}}

structure on
M_{6}
induces a choice of Wu structure, which we can then use to
define the self-dual field.

24
24

24

In
[
166
,
167
]

the authors went to some length to define non-anomalous six-dimensional
supergravity theories
without
making a choice of Wu structure. It is possible similar considerations might be useful in the present context.

We will now attempt to give a generalization of the discussion
in section 5.2 of
[
164
]
to the present case.

In section 5.2 of
[
164
]
one considers the integral class
x_{4}=\theta\cup x
on

S^{1}\times M_{6}
.
25
25

25

Here the cup product is taken on integral cocycles. For a definition see, for examples,
[
168
,
169
,
170
]
.
One then evaluates the level
1/2
Wu-Chern-Simons theory on an 8-manfiold

W_{8}
with
\partial W_{8}=S^{1}\times M_{6}
. This is done by extending
x_{4}
to
W_{8}
and taking

\Omega(x)=\exp\left[{\rm i}\pi\int_{W_{8}}x_{4}\cup(x_{4}+\lambda)\right]

(488)

for a suitable lift
\lambda
of the
4^{\rm th}
Wu class. One needs to choose a trivialization of

\lambda
on
\partial W_{8}
and this leads to a dependence on Wu-structure (much like 3d level 1/2
Chern-Simons theory depends on spin structure). A
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure on
W_{8}

is enough to define a choice of
\lambda
so that
2\lambda=p_{1}-\alpha^{2}
where
\alpha
is
the characteristic class of the
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure. (See footnote 11 of
[
164
]

for a proof.)

In the present case it is natural to fill in
\Sigma
with a handlebody
\mathcal{H}_{3}

so that
\partial\mathcal{H}_{3}=\Sigma
. (Such a choice of handlebody represents a weak-coupling
limit of the theory.) The kernel of the inclusion

\iota_{*}:H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})\to H_{1}(\mathcal{H}_{3};\mathbb{Z})

(489)

defines a maximal Lagrangian sublattice of
H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
.
One then chooses a complementary maximal Lagrangian sublattice in
H_{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
. Then the
sublattice of
H^{1}(\Sigma;\mathbb{Z})
dual to the kernel will be denoted
L_{B}
.
These are the degree one cohomology classes which have nontrivial periods on
\Sigma
and
extend to
\mathcal{H}_{3}
. Similarly
L_{A}
will be the complementary maximal Lagrangian
lattice of classes which do not extend.

We can now choose
W_{8}=S^{1}\times\mathcal{H}_{3}\times X
and our theta function will be a sum over
classes in

\Lambda=L_{B}\otimes H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})

(490)

If
x\in\Lambda
is of the from
\sum_{i}b_{i}\cup k_{i}
where
b_{i}\in L_{B}

and
k_{i}\in H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})
we can extend
b_{i}
to degree one classes
\tilde{b}_{i}
in
\mathcal{H}_{3}
. Then
x_{4}\cup x_{4}=0

because
\theta\cup\theta=0
and Witten’s definition reduces to

\Omega(x)=\exp\left[{\rm i}\pi\int_{\mathcal{H}_{3}\times X}(\sum_{i}\tilde{b}_{i}\cup k_{i})\cup\lambda\right]

(491)

Now, a choice of
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure on
\mathcal{H}_{3}\times M_{6}
allows us to define a class
\tilde{\rho}
on

\mathcal{H}_{3}
with the property that

\begin{split}\int_{\mathcal{H}_{3}}\tilde{\rho}\cup\beta_{I}&=1~\mod 2,\end{split}

(492)

where
\{\alpha^{I},\beta_{I}\}
is a symplectic basis for
L_{A},L_{B}
.
To prove this we note that a
{\rm Spin^{c}}
structure on the disk allows us to
define a characteristic class
\tilde{\alpha}
with
\int_{D}\tilde{\alpha}=1
.
Then we extend to the solid torus
D\times S^{1}
finally we choose such a class on
each torus and take a connected sum.

Now, following
[
164
]
we can write

\lambda(W)=\lambda(X)-\tilde{\rho}\cup c_{\rm uv}
.
Next, recall that in the class
S
theory,
L_{B}
will be,
roughly speaking, the co-root lattice of the gauge group while
L_{A}
will be the weight lattice.
(See
[
171
]
for more precise statements.)
Thus
\tilde{\rho}
functions as a kind of “Weyl vector,”
\rho\in L_{A}

and the above quadratic refinement becomes

\exp[{\rm i}\pi x\cdot(\rho\otimes w_{2}(X))],

(493)

where we use the dual pairing between
L_{A}
and
L_{B}
and the
usual intersection pairing on
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}_{2})
.
This is the 6d derivation
of the phase discussed in
[
8
,
87
,
21
]
.

It is important to note that the quadratic refinement
\Omega
used in Witten’s prescription, and in the generalizations
discussed in
[
156
]
are all
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-valued, whereas the natural quadratic refinement coming from decoupling
the M5-brane is
U(1)
valued and can vary continuously with metric
[
162
]
. This will be important below.

In equation (
455
) we gave the transformation properties of the partition function under S-duality.
It would be interesting to reproduce this from the 6-dimensional perspective using gravitational anomalies.
This is perhaps one of the more accessible of the many open problems suggested in this section.

8.5
Four-dimensional perspective

A careful reduction of an action for the interacting Abelian self-dual tensor multiplet from
M_{6}
to
X

(properly accounting for self-duality using, for example, the PST action or the action described in

[
156
]
), with class S twist, indeed yields the Seiberg-Witten LEET on
X
. (Without the
topological couplings
A,B,C,...
.)
Roughly speaking, the flux-lattice of the
U(1)^{r}
gauge fields is
\Lambda=L_{B}\otimes H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z})

and the reduction of the fieldstrength
H_{x}
is of the form
H_{x}=\beta_{I}\wedge F^{I}
where
\beta_{I}
is
a basis of harmonic forms representing a basis in
L_{B}
. Then

\int_{M_{6}}H_{x}*H_{x}\sim\int_{X}\mathrm{Im}(\tau_{IJ})F^{I}*F^{J}\qquad.

(494)

(For one derivation see section 3.1.5 in
[
142
]
. The result for the remainder of the
action follows from supersymmetry. )

This raises two important issues which need to be understood more clearly:

1.

In this paper it has been very important that
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
is not precisely a theta function. Rather,
it has a nontrivial kernel
\mathcal{K}
, as in equation (
171
) above. It arises from fermion zeromodes and interaction
terms. This kernel becomes somewhat more intricate in the higher rank case. See, for example,

[
87
]
equation (4.17). The higher-rank generalization of the error functions we use in defining

\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
needs to be understood better. It is probably best to start by finding the higher rank
analog of
\widehat{\Theta}^{J,J^{\prime}}
of equation (
299
). Explicit results for the holomorphic and non-holomorphic parts of the
SU(N)
VW partition function
[
124
,
127
,
39
,
30
,
108
]
could be a useful benchmark result for the analysis of the
SU(N)
,
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory.

2.

As we have just discussed, when defining the 5-brane partition function a crucial ingredient is the choice of QRIF. As we have shown above, using Witten’s
prescription for
\Omega(x)
for the level 1/2 case, this descends to Witten’s famous term

(-1)^{B(c_{1},w_{2}(X))}
where
c_{1}
is the first Chern class of the
U(1)
gauge field in the LEET on the Coulomb branch.
On the other hand, in this paper no such factor appears in the lattice sum
\Psi_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}^{J}
. Rather there is a complex weighting
factor

\exp\!\left[-2\pi i(B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{+},c_{{\rm uv}})\,v(\tau,\tau_{{\rm uv}})+B({\boldsymbol{k}}_{-},c_{{\rm uv}})\,\bar{v}(\tau,\tau_{{\rm uv}}))\right]

(495)

which, in the
N_{f}=0
limit becomes Witten’s phase. (Recall the discussion from section 4.7 above.)
This strongly suggests that the appropriate QRIF
relevant to the general class S problem is in fact
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-valued, and it is natural to speculate that it
is related to the metric-dependent,
\mathbb{C}^{*}
-valued QRIF discussed in
[
162
]
.

26
26

26

In the 2018 String-Math lecture by one of us, in Sendai,
equation (
493
) was cited as evidence that twisted class
S
theories will not lead to new four-manifold invariants. In view of the present remark, that claim should be retracted.

Another important lesson from the 4-dimensional perspective concerns the factor
A^{\chi}B^{\sigma}
in the
measure. The arguments of
[
5
,
22
,
21
]
based on R-symmetry
should generalize to all class S examples. Thus the coupling
A^{\chi}
, which is frame-dependent, is given by

A=\alpha\left(\det\frac{\partial u^{I}}{\partial a^{J}}\right)^{1/2}\quad.

(496)

To write
B
we should find a holomorphic function
\Delta_{\rm phys}
on the Hitchin base
\mathcal{B}

with first order zeroes at the discriminant locus and take

B=\beta\Delta_{\rm phys}^{1/8}\quad.

(497)

Now, in class S theory, where there is a nontrivial conformal manifold of superconformal couplings:
It is the Teichmüller space of pairs
(C,D)

[
141
,
144
]
. Just the way we found
the couplings
A,B,C
to be interesting bi-modular forms, we expect
A,B
to be interesting bi-automorphic
objects in the general case. (For Teichmüller space and for the moduli space of Abelian varieties, respectively.) Moreover, it
is natural to ask if
\alpha,\beta
must be interesting automorphic forms on this Teichmüller space.
The results of this paper strongly suggest that the answer is affirmative
since, in our example, the proper couplings to
\chi
and
\sigma
are given by the functions

\tilde{A}
and
\tilde{B}
of equation (
42
). In this example then,
\alpha,\beta
are
nontrivial powers of
\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})
.
Note that there are weak-coupling limits of class S theories
where
C_{g,n}
degenerates to a connected sum of several punctured tori connected by long thin tubes.
This shows that
\alpha,\beta
must be nontrivial in general.

8.6
Two-dimensional perspective

Finally, we can also gain insight by reducing the 6d Abelian interacting TM theory on
X

with Donaldson-Witten twist to obtain a
(0,2)
QFT on
\Sigma
, and we can identify the
partition function, and therefore the Coulomb branch measure with the partition function
of this
d=2
,
(0,2)
QFT on
\Sigma
(with the class
S
twist). We should note that a special case of this computation goes
back to the case of the M5 brane wrapped on an ample divisor in a Calabi-Yau manifold

[
172
,
173
]
in which case it has
(0,4)
supersymmetry.

The field content of the
d=2

(0,2)
theory on
\Sigma
is
a model with the following fields
[
30
,
137
,
174
]
:

From the noncompact normal bundle scalars we get

1.

A noncompact scalar field
dY\in\Omega^{1}(\Sigma)\otimes H^{2,+}(X;\mathbb{R})
.
In deriving the
u
-plane measure these will play a role analogous to the

\mathcal{N}=2
auxiliary fields
D
.

2.

A noncompact scalar field
Y_{z}dz\in\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma)
.
When deriving the
u
-plane measure the zeromodes of these fields will
play the role of the special Kähler coordinates
a^{I}
.

From the self-dual 2-form gauge potential we get

1.

Self-dual, compact, chiral bosons with fieldstrength

F^{+}\in\Omega^{1,0}(\Sigma)\otimes H^{2,+}(X;\mathbb{R})

(498)

satisfying
\bar{\partial}F^{+}=0
.

2.

Anti-self-dual, compact, chiral bosons with fieldstrength

F^{-}\in\Omega^{0,1}(\Sigma)\otimes H^{2,-}(X;\mathbb{R})

(499)

satisfying
\partial F^{-}=0
.

The sum over the zeromodes of these fields becomes the
sum over the fluxes in the “theta function”
\Psi_{\mu}
.
Viewed this way
\Psi_{\mu}
is, roughly speaking, a Siegel-Narain theta function
for the zero-modes of the 2d model.

Finally, we have fermionic fields

1.

\eta,\eta_{\bar{z}}d\bar{z}
. These play the role of the fermion
\eta
in the 4d
twisted theory.

2.

\chi,\chi_{\bar{z}}d\bar{z}
. These are valued in
H^{2,+}(X)
and play the role
of the fermion
\chi
in the 4d twisted theory.

The action one obtains from reducing - for example - the PST action is that of a nontrivial
interacting theory.

27
27

27

The
(0,2)
field multiplets and the interacting theory up to 2-fermion terms
and some of the 4-fermion terms was worked out in unpublished work by G.M. and I. Nidaiev in 2012. A few
details are mentioned in
[
174
]
.

Some of the interactions are very important and account, for example
for the crucial difference between
\Psi_{\mu}
and an actual Siegel-Narain theta function.

Of particular interest from this point of view are the
A,B,C
couplings that appear in the Coulomb branch measure.
We expect that the defects
D\subset\mathcal{C}
in the 6d (0,2) theory will turn into operator insertions in the 2d (0,2)
theory on
\Sigma
. It is possible the suitable operators can be deduced
simply from the singularity structure of the
Higgs fields near the defects. Certainly (
68
) is very suggestive of a correlation function in a 2d CFT.
This point of view is also probably the only practical approach to deriving explicit formulae for the automorphic functions
\alpha
and
\beta
.

Probably the first practical exercise for carrying out this 2d approach is to account for the modular weight

-4\ell-\chi/2
of the
u
-plane integral in the
SU(2)

\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
case.

9
Further open problems

We have given a quite extensive discussion on the partition function of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory on smooth compact four manifolds. Nevertheless, a number of aspects remain to be better understood. We list a few here:

1.

A concrete open problem is a proof of the non-trivial identities (
59
) and (
67
). We also expect similar identities for theories with higher rank gauge groups.

2.

For manifolds with
b_{2}^{+}=1
, we evaluated the
u
-plane integral
\Phi^{J}_{\boldsymbol{\mu}}
only for specific Spin
c
structures. It would be interesting to have a solution for arbitrary
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
. To this end, one needs to determine a suitable anti-derivative
\widehat{G}(\tau,z;{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})
with poles at appropriate positions. We have seen that
for
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
an almost complex structure, the
G(\tau,z;{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})
are
related to the refined VW partition function, enumerating Betti
numbers (or better
\chi_{y}
genera) of instanton moduli spaces. This raises the questions, whether there is a geometric meaning of
G(\tau,z;{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m})

for general
{\boldsymbol{k}}_{m}
.

3.

Throughout this paper we have restricted attention to the case where
X
is simply-connected. It should be possible to extend the discussion to the non-simply connected case. (See
[
175
,
176
]
for the case of the original Donaldson-Witten theory.) It is possible that such an extension can teach us lessons about three-dimensional manifolds, and 3d-3d correspondences
[
177
,
178
]
.

4.

We have demonstrated that when we choose the Spin
c
structure canonically associated to an ACS, the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
partition functions satisfy the same
S
-duality relations as VW partition functions. Moreover, for the canonical
{\rm Spin}^{c}
structure both partition functions formally compute the generating function of the Euler characters of instanton moduli spaces.
This strongly suggests that they are identical for non-Kähler manifolds as well as Kähler manifolds. Our discussion of the relation of the VW and adjoint-SW equations shows that they are not the same in general, but rather are related by a compact deformation. It would be good to have a rigorous mathematical proof that the moduli spaces of these two equations are nevertheless topologically equivalent.

5.

The
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}

u-
plane integral reproduces and generalizes the generating function of Euler numbers of instanton moduli spaces. In the mathematical literature, generating functions for
\chi_{y}
-genera are available
[
19
,
35
,
111
,
179
]
. This involves functions of McMahon type
[
19
]
, which are related to quantum modular forms rather than modular forms.

28
28

28

The relation to the quantum modular forms arises as follows.
In Appendix E of
[
180
]
it was noted that the
logarithmic derivative of the McMahon function gives a “half-Eisenstein function”
E_{3}
. But
E_{3}
is one of the standard examples of the new classes of quantum modular forms described in
[
181
]
.

It would be interesting to the understand the refinement parameter
y
from the perspective of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}

u
-plane integral.

6.

Various string-theoretic descriptions are available for the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory
[
144
,
182
]
. It would be interesting to connect our work to such D-brane and M-brane systems, as well as to integrable systems.

7.

As discussed extensively in Section
8
, an interesting avenue is to develop the
u
-plane integral for other
\mathcal{N}=2
supersymmetric theories, such as theories with higher rank gauge groups and more generally theories of class
S
. Trying to do this raises many open
questions, some of which were pointed out in Section
8
.

8.

Finally, it has been emphasized over the years by Edward Witten that a promising approach to finding new four-manifold invariants using supersymmetric gauge theory is to categorify the VW invariants. In the realm of algebraic surfaces an important step forward (using a mathematical viewpoint) was taken in
[
179
]
. Our discussion of the relation of the VW with the adjoint-Seiberg-Witten equations shows that one can deform the VW equations in such a way as to introduce an
SO(2)
symmetry. We hope this deformation, and the resulting
SO(2)
symmetry will be useful in dealing with the noncompact branches where the
C
and
B
fields are nonzero.

29
29

29

As shown in
[
15
]
the field
C
is generically zero. However
B
need not be zero.

The results of
[
179
]
suggest that it should be interesting
to investigate the equivariant
L^{2}
-kernel of certain Dirac-type operators on the moduli space of the adjoint Seiberg-Witten equations.

Appendix A
Modular forms

We review in this appendix aspects of modular forms used in the main text. After discussing classical modular forms, we discuss the Appell-Lerch sum and bi-modular forms. A few references for modular forms are
[
57
,
183
,
184
,
185
]
.

Eisenstein series

We let
\tau\in\mathbb{H}
and define
q=e^{2\pi i\tau}
. The Eisenstein series
E_{k}:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}
for even
k\geq 2
are defined as the
q
-series

E_{k}(\tau)=1-\frac{2k}{B_{k}}\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\sigma_{k-1}(n)\,q^{n},

(500)

with
\sigma_{k}(n)=\sum_{d|n}d^{k}
the divisor sum. For
k\geq 4
,
E_{k}
is a modular form of

\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
of weight
k
. In other words, it transforms under
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
as

E_{k}\!\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=(c\tau+d)^{k}E_{k}(\tau).

(501)

The first terms of the expansions of
E_{4}
and
E_{6}
are

\displaystyle E_{4}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1+240\,q+\dots,

(502)

\displaystyle E_{6}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 1-504\,q+\dots.

(503)

On the other hand
E_{2}
is a quasi-modular form, which means that the
\operatorname{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
transformation of
E_{2}
includes a shift in addition to the weight,

E_{2}\!\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d}\right)=(c\tau+d)^{2}E_{2}(\tau)-\frac{6i}{\pi}c(c\tau+d).

(504)

The non-holomorphic modular form

\widehat{E}_{2}(\tau,\bar{\tau})=E_{2}(\tau)-\frac{3}{\pi y},

(505)

does transform as a modular form of weight 2.

Dedekind eta function

The Dedekind eta function
\eta:\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}
is defined as

\eta(\tau)=q^{\frac{1}{24}}\prod_{n=1}^{\infty}(1-q^{n}).

(506)

It is a modular form of weight
\frac{1}{2}
under SL
(2,\mathbb{Z})
with a
non-trivial multiplier system
30
30

30

For an unambiguous value of the square root, we define the phase of
z\in\mathbb{C}^{*}
by
\log(z):=\log|z|+i\,\mathrm{arg}(z)
with
-\pi<\arg(z)\leq\pi
.

\varepsilon(\gamma)=\frac{\eta((a\tau+b)/(c\tau+d))}{(c\tau+d)^{1/2}\,\eta(\tau)},

(507)

The phase
\varepsilon(\gamma)
can be explicitly evaluated
[
186
]
.

\eta
transforms under the generators of
SL
(2,\mathbb{Z})
as

\begin{split}&\eta(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\,\eta(\tau),\\
&\eta(\tau+1)=e^{\frac{\pi i}{12}}\,\eta(\tau).\end{split}

(508)

Jacobi theta functions

The four Jacobi theta functions
\vartheta_{j}:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{C}\to\mathbb{C}
are defined by,

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{1}(\tau,z)=i\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(-1)^{n-\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}}e^{2\pi inz},\\
&\vartheta_{2}(\tau,z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}}e^{2\pi inz},\\
&\vartheta_{3}(\tau,z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}}e^{2\pi inz},\\
&\vartheta_{4}(\tau,z)=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}(-1)^{n}q^{\frac{n^{2}}{2}}e^{2\pi inz}.\end{split}

(509)

\vartheta_{1}
is odd as function of
v
, while the
\vartheta_{j}
are even
for
j=2,3,4
. We have
\partial_{v}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v)|_{v=0}=-2\pi\eta(\tau)^{3}
. We use
\vartheta_{j}(\tau,0)=\vartheta_{j}(\tau)
. These functions
satisfy various identities. For their sum, we have

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}=\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4},\\
\end{split}

(510)

and

\begin{split}\vartheta_{3}(\tau/2,z/2)=\vartheta_{3}(2\tau,z)+\vartheta_{2}(2\tau,z),\\
\vartheta_{4}(\tau/2,z/2)=\vartheta_{3}(2\tau,z)-\vartheta_{2}(2\tau,z).\end{split}

(511)

For their product, we have

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{2}(\tau)\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau)\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau)=2\,\eta(\tau)^{3},\\
&\vartheta_{1}(\tau,2z)\,\vartheta_{2}(\tau)\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau)\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau)=2\,\vartheta_{1}(\tau,z)\,\vartheta_{2}(\tau,z)\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau,z)\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau,z).\end{split}

(512)

The function
\vartheta_{1}
transforms for an element
\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
as

\vartheta_{1}\!\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\frac{z}{c\tau+d}\right)=\varepsilon(\gamma)^{3}\,(c\tau+d)^{1/2}\,e^{\pi icz^{2}/(c\tau+d)}\,\vartheta_{1}(\tau,z),

(513)

where
\varepsilon(\gamma)
is the multiplier system of the

\eta
-function (
507
).

For the two generators
S
and
T
of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
, we have

\begin{split}&S:\qquad\vartheta_{1}\!\left(-1/\tau,z/\tau\right)=-i\sqrt{-i\tau}\,e^{\pi iz^{2}/\tau}\,\vartheta_{1}(\tau,z),\\
&T:\qquad\vartheta_{1}\!\left(\tau+1,z\right)=e^{2\pi i/8}\,\vartheta_{1}(\tau,z).\end{split}

(514)

These have the following transformations for modular inversion

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{2}(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau),\\
&\vartheta_{3}(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau),\\
&\vartheta_{4}(-1/\tau)=\sqrt{-i\tau}\,\vartheta_{2}(\tau),\end{split}

(515)

and for the shift

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{2}(\tau+1)=e^{2\pi i/8}\,\vartheta_{2}(\tau),\\
&\vartheta_{3}(\tau+1)=\vartheta_{4}(\tau),\\
&\vartheta_{4}(\tau+1)=\vartheta_{3}(\tau).\end{split}

(516)

The generators of the congruence subgroup
\Gamma(2)
are
\tau\to\tau+2
and
\tau\to\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1}
. Using the above two, we
find for the second transformation

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{2}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1}\right)=\sqrt{-2\tau+1}\,\vartheta_{2}(\tau),\\
&\vartheta_{3}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1}\right)=\sqrt{-2\tau+1}\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau),\\
&\vartheta_{4}\!\left(\frac{\tau}{-2\tau+1}\right)=e^{2\pi i/4}\sqrt{-2\tau+1}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau).\\
\end{split}

(517)

Using these transformations, we can derive the behavior at the cusps of
\mathbb{H}^{2}/\Gamma(2)
.

•

For
\tau\to i\infty
,

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{2}(\tau)\to 0,\\
&\vartheta_{3}(\tau)\to 1,\\
&\vartheta_{4}(\tau)\to 1.\end{split}

(518)

•

For
\tau\to 0
,

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{2}(\tau)\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{-i\tau}},\\
&\vartheta_{3}(\tau)\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{-i\tau}},\\
&\vartheta_{4}(\tau)\to 0.\end{split}

(519)

•

For
\tau\to 1
,

\begin{split}&\vartheta_{2}(\tau)\to\frac{e^{2\pi i/8}}{\sqrt{-i(\tau-1)}},\\
&\vartheta_{3}(\tau)\to 0,\\
&\vartheta_{4}(\tau)\to\frac{1}{\sqrt{-i(\tau-1)}}.\end{split}

(520)

We also have the following quasi-periodicity

\begin{split}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,z+n\tau)&=(-1)^{n}q^{-n^{2}/2}e^{-2\pi inz}\vartheta_{1}(\tau,z),\\
\vartheta_{2}(\tau,z+n\tau)&=q^{-n^{2}/2}e^{-2\pi inz}\vartheta_{2}(\tau,z).\end{split}

(521)

For the other two theta functions,
\vartheta_{3}
transforms as

\vartheta_{2}
, and
\vartheta_{4}
as
\vartheta_{1}
.

We list moreover the expression for the expression for

\vartheta^{-1}
, as a sum over its poles

\frac{i\eta(\tau)^{3}}{\vartheta_{1}(\tau,z)}=-e^{\pi iz}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}q^{n(n+1)/2}}{1-e^{2\pi iz}q^{n}}.

(522)

The Eisenstein series can be expressed in terms of Jacobi theta functions

\displaystyle E_{4}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{8}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{8}+\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{8}}{2},

(523)

\displaystyle E_{6}(\tau)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{(\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4})(\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4})(\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}-\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4})}{2}.

(524)

The half-periods
e_{j}
are defined in terms of the Jacobi theta
functions by

\begin{split}&e_{1}(\tau)=\wp(\tfrac{1}{2},\tau)/\pi^{2}=\frac{1}{3}(\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4})=\frac{2}{3}+16q+\dots,\\
&e_{2}(\tau)=\wp(\tfrac{\tau}{2},\tau)/\pi^{2}=-\frac{1}{3}(\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4})=-\frac{1}{3}-8q^{\frac{1}{2}}+\dots,\\
&e_{3}(\tau)=\wp(\tfrac{1+\tau}{2},\tau)/\pi^{2}=\frac{1}{3}(\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4})=-\frac{1}{3}+8q^{\frac{1}{2}}+\dots,\end{split}

(525)

where
\wp(z,\tau)
is the Weierstrass
\wp
-function.

The half-periods
e_{j}
transform as modular forms of weight 2 under the congruence
subgroup
\Gamma(2)
. They are exchanged under the generators
S
and

T
of
SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
. For
S
,

\begin{split}e_{1}(-1/\tau)&=\tau^{2}e_{2}(\tau),\\
e_{2}(-1/\tau)&=\tau^{2}e_{1}(\tau),\\
e_{3}(-1/\tau)&=\tau^{2}e_{3}(\tau).\end{split}

(526)

and for
T
,

\begin{split}e_{1}(\tau+1)&=e_{1}(\tau),\\
e_{2}(\tau+1)&=e_{3}(\tau),\\
e_{3}(\tau+1)&=e_{2}(\tau).\end{split}

(527)

The
e_{i}
satisfy

\begin{split}&\sum_{i=1}^{3}e_{i}=0,\\
&\sum_{i=1}^{3}e_{i}^{2}=\frac{2}{3}E_{4},\\
&\sum_{i=1}^{3}e_{i}^{3}=\frac{2}{9}E_{6},\\
&e_{1}e_{2}e_{3}=\frac{2}{27}E_{6}.\end{split}

(528)

and they give a three dimensional representation of
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
of weight two.

Let
D=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{d}{d\tau}
. We list the following derivatives

\begin{split}D\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}&=\frac{1}{6}\left(E_{2}(\tau)\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{8}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{8}\right)\\
&=\frac{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}}{6}\left(E_{2}(\tau)+\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}+\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\right),\\
D\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}&=\frac{1}{6}\left(E_{2}(\tau)\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{8}+\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{8}\right)\\
&=\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{6}\left(E_{2}(\tau)+\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\right),\\
D\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}&=\frac{1}{6}\left(E_{2}(\tau)\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}-\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{8}+\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{8}\right)\\
&=\frac{\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}}{6}\left(E_{2}(\tau)-\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}-\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\right),\\
\end{split}

(529)

and

DE_{2}(\tau)=\frac{1}{12}\left(E_{2}(\tau)^{2}-E_{4}(\tau)\right).

(530)

The Appell-Lerch sum

We recall the definition and properties of the Appell-Lerch sum
following Zwegers
[
112
]
. We
define

M(\tau,u,v):=\frac{e^{\pi iu}}{\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v)}\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}\frac{(-1)^{n}q^{n(n+1)/2}e^{2\pi inv}}{1-e^{2\pi iu}q^{n}}.

(531)

With
a=\mathrm{Im}(u)/y
, we introduce also

R(\tau,\bar{\tau},u,\bar{u}):=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}\left(\mathrm{sgn}(n)-E\!\left((n+a)\sqrt{2y}\right)\right)(-1)^{n-\frac{1}{2}}\,e^{-2\pi iun}q^{-n^{2}/2},

(532)

where
E(t)
is the rescaled error function

E(t)=2\int_{0}^{t}e^{-\pi u^{2}}du=\mathrm{Erf}(\sqrt{\pi}t).

(533)

This function is anti-symmetric,
E(-t)=-E(t)
.

The non-holomorphic completion of
M(\tau,u,v)
is then given by

\widehat{M}(\tau,\bar{\tau},u,\bar{u},v,\bar{v})=M(\tau,u,v)+\frac{i}{2}R(\tau,\bar{\tau},u-v,\bar{u}-\bar{v}).

(534)

For an element
\gamma\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})
, it transforms as

\begin{split}&\widehat{M}\!\left(\frac{a\tau+b}{c\tau+d},\frac{a\bar{\tau}+b}{c\bar{\tau}+d},\frac{u}{c\tau+d},\frac{\bar{u}}{c\bar{\tau}+d},\frac{v}{c\tau+d},\frac{\bar{v}}{c\bar{\tau}+d}\right)\\
&\qquad=\varepsilon(\gamma)^{-3}(c\tau+d)^{\frac{1}{2}}e^{-\pi ic(u-v)^{2}/(c\tau+d)}\,\widehat{M}(\tau,\bar{\tau},u,\bar{u},v,\bar{v}),\end{split}

(535)

where
\varepsilon(\gamma)
is the multiplier of the
\eta
-function (
507
).

The anti-holomorphic derivative of
\widehat{M}
is given by

\begin{split}&\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\widehat{M}(\tau,\bar{\tau},u,\bar{u},v,\bar{v})=-i\left(\partial_{\bar{\tau}}\sqrt{2y}\right)e^{-2\pi(a-b)^{2}}\\
&\qquad\times\sum_{n\in\mathbb{Z}+\frac{1}{2}}(n+a-b)(-1)^{n-\frac{1}{2}}\bar{q}^{n^{2}/2}e^{-2\pi i(\bar{u}-\bar{v})n}.\end{split}

(536)

A number of other useful properties are
[
112
]
:

1.

M
is anti-periodic under shifts by 1:

M(\tau,u+1,v)=M(\tau,u,v+1)=-M(\tau,u,v).

(537)

2.

Inversion of the elliptic arguments leaves
M
invariant:

M(\tau,-u,-v)=M(\tau,u,v).

(538)

3.

Simultaneous shifts of
u
and
v
by
z

lead to a simple transformation of
M(\tau,u,v)
. For
u,v,u+z,v+z\neq\mathbb{Z}\tau+\mathbb{Z}
, one has

M(\tau,u+z,v+z)-M(\tau,u,v)=\frac{i\,\eta^{3}(\tau)\,\vartheta_{1}(\tau,u+v+z)\,\vartheta_{1}(\tau,z)}{\vartheta_{1}(\tau,u)\,\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v)\,\vartheta_{1}(\tau,u+z)\,\vartheta_{1}(\tau,v+z)}.

(539)

4.

Quasi-periodicity property in
u
and
v
:

\begin{split}&\widehat{M}(\tau,\bar{\tau},u+k\tau+l,\bar{u}+k\bar{\tau}+l,v+m\tau+n,\bar{v}+m\bar{\tau}+n)\\
&\qquad=(-1)^{k+l+m+n}q^{(k-m)^{2}/2}e^{2\pi i(k-m)(u-v)}\widehat{M}(\tau,\bar{\tau},u,\bar{u},v,\bar{v}).\end{split}

(540)

Bi-modular forms

The physical quantities of the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory are examples of
so-called bi-modular forms. See in particular (
19
). We review the definition due to Stienstra and Zagier of these
functions following
[
42
]
. See
also
[
43
]
. Let
f:\mathbb{H}\times\mathbb{H}\to\mathbb{C}
be a function in two variables,
\tau_{1}\in\mathbb{H}
and

\tau_{2}\in\mathbb{H}
. Let
\Gamma
be a congruence subgroup, and

\chi_{1},\chi_{2}
characters on
\Gamma
. The function
f

is a bi-modular form of weight
(w_{1},w_{2})
on

(\Gamma,SL(2,\mathbb{Z}))
, if

1.

for
\gamma_{1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{1}&b_{1}\\
c_{1}&d_{1}\end{array}\right)
and
\gamma_{2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a_{2}&b_{2}\\
c_{2}&d_{2}\end{array}\right)\in\Gamma

f\!\left(\frac{a_{1}\tau_{1}+b_{1}}{c_{1}\tau_{1}+d_{1}},\frac{a_{2}\tau_{2}+b_{2}}{c_{2}\tau_{2}+d_{2}}\right)=\chi_{1}(\gamma_{1})\,\chi_{2}(\gamma_{2})\,(c_{1}\tau_{1}+d_{1})^{w_{1}}(c_{2}\tau_{2}+d_{2})^{w_{2}}\,f(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(541)

2.

for
\gamma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}a&b\\
c&d\end{array}\right)\in SL(2,\mathbb{Z})

f\!\left(\frac{a\tau_{1}+b}{c\tau_{1}+d},\frac{a\tau_{2}+b}{c\tau_{2}+d}\right)=(c\tau_{1}+d)^{w_{1}}(c\tau_{2}+d)^{w_{2}}\,f(\tau_{1},\tau_{2}).

(542)

For the functions we encounter for the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory,
\Gamma=\Gamma(2)
.
Some of these functions have a pole for
\tau=\tau_{\rm uv}
modulo
images of
\Gamma(2)
. Their proper domain is therefore

\mathbb{H}^{2}-\{(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})|\tau=\gamma(\tau_{\rm uv}),\,\,\gamma\in\Gamma(2)\}
.

Appendix B
Derivation of
\Delta_{\rm phys}
and
da/du

This appendix gives a derivation of the closed
expressions for
u
, the discriminant
\Delta_{\rm phys}
, and
da/du

for the
\mathcal{N}=2^{*}
theory. We will follow the normalization of the
variables as in
[
3
]
and
[
17
]
, which differs from the one used
commonly in instanton counting
[
49
]
. For large
u
,
a
behaves as
a=\sqrt{u}+\dots
.

To determine the IR gauge coupling
\tau
, we first expand the
product on the rhs of (
16
). The SW curve
\Sigma
then reads,

\Sigma:\quad y^{2}=x^{3}-\tfrac{1}{6}E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,m^{2}\,x^{2}-f\,x-g,

(543)

where
E_{4}
is the weight 4 Eisenstein series, and
f
and
g
are functions of
u
,
\tau_{\rm uv}
and
m
obtained by expanding out the product in equation (
16
)

\begin{split}&f(\tau_{\rm uv},u,m)=-\sum_{i<j}(e_{i}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,u+\tfrac{1}{4}e_{i}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}\,m^{2})(e_{j}(\tau_{\rm uv})u+\tfrac{1}{4}e_{j}^{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,m^{2}),\\
&g(\tau_{\rm uv},u,m)=\prod_{i=1}^{3}(e_{i}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,u+\tfrac{1}{4}e_{i}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}\,m^{2}).\end{split}

(544)

We then bring the curve (
543
) to the curve
\tilde{\Sigma}
in Weierstrass form

\tilde{\Sigma}:\quad\tilde{y}^{2}=4\,\tilde{x}^{3}-\tilde{g}_{2}\,\tilde{x}-\tilde{g}_{3}.

(545)

Whereas
x
and
y
have dimension
\Lambda^{2}
and
\Lambda^{3}
,
\tilde{x}
and
\tilde{y}
are dimensionless. The coefficients are proportional to the Eisenstein
series
E_{4}
and
E_{6}
:
\tilde{g}_{2}=\frac{4\pi^{4}}{3}E_{4}(\tau)
and
\tilde{g}_{3}=\frac{8\pi^{6}}{27}E_{6}(\tau)
. This is achieved by the transformation

x=4Z^{-2}\,\tilde{x}+\tfrac{1}{18}m^{2}E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})
and
y=4Z^{-3}\,\tilde{y}
, where
Z
is a dimensionful parameter which we will solve
for below. The coefficients
\tilde{g}_{2}
and
\tilde{g}_{3}
are given
in terms of
Z
and the original parameters as

\begin{split}\tilde{g}_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv},u,m)&=\frac{Z^{4}}{4}\left(\frac{1}{108}E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}m^{4}+f(u,\tau_{\rm uv},m)\right),\\
\tilde{g}_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv},u,m)&=\frac{Z^{6}}{16}\left(g(u,\tau_{\rm uv},m)+\frac{m^{2}}{18}E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})f(u,\tau_{\rm uv},m)+\frac{2}{18^{3}}m^{6}E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{3}\right).\end{split}

(546)

Using these expressions, we can determine the (mathematical) discriminant of the
curve
(2\pi)^{12}\,\Delta(\tau)\equiv\tilde{g}_{2}^{3}-27\tilde{g}_{3}^{2}
, which factorizes as

(2\pi)^{12}\,\Delta(\tau)=Z^{12}\Delta(\tau_{\rm uv})\,(u-\tfrac{m^{2}}{4}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv}))^{2}\,(u-\tfrac{m^{2}}{4}e_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv}))^{2}\,(u-\tfrac{m^{2}}{4}e_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv}))^{2}.

(547)

The discriminant vanishes at the three cusps of

\mathbb{H}/\Gamma(2)
, where
u
approaches

\tfrac{m^{2}}{4}e_{j}(\tau_{\rm uv})
,
j=1,2,3
. To determine which value of
u

corresponds to which cusp, note that in the scaling limit the
singularities for
\tau\to 0,1
should correspond to
u\to\tfrac{m^{2}}{4}e_{j}(\tau_{\rm uv})
with
j
either
2
or 3, since these become the strong coupling
singularities at
u_{0}=\pm\Lambda^{2}
of the pure
SU(2)
theory by
(
20
). Thus for
\tau\to i\infty
, we necessarily have

u\to\tfrac{m^{2}}{4}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})
. Modular transformations fix
the other cusps, giving us

\begin{split}&\lim_{\tau\to i\infty}u(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})=\frac{m^{2}}{4}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv}),\\
&\lim_{\tau\to 0}u(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})=\frac{m^{2}}{4}e_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv}),\\
&\lim_{\tau\to 1}u(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})=\frac{m^{2}}{4}e_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv}).\end{split}

(548)

Using these constraints, we can determine
u
and
Z
explicitly. The
equation for
\tilde{g}_{2}
is a quadratic equation in
u
, which is
solved by

u=\frac{-\frac{1}{12}E_{6}(\tau_{\rm uv})m^{2}\pm\sqrt{-12\Delta(\tau_{\rm uv})m^{4}+12E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})\tilde{g}_{2}(\tau)/Z^{4}}}{E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})}.

(549)

We will find later that the
-
sign of
\pm
matches with the convention used for
u
in the main text. Taking the scaling limit for
u_{0}=u+\frac{1}{8}e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})m^{2}

indeed reproduces
u_{0}
of the pure Seiberg-Witten theory. Moreover, the zero of
E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})
for
\tau_{\rm uv}=i
is a removable singularity.

Equation (
549
) together with the expressions for
u
at the cusps
(
548
), give us expressions for
Z
at the cusps:

•

For
\tau\to\tau_{\rm uv}
,
u\to\infty
. Then

\lim_{\tau\to\tau_{\rm uv}}Z=0.

(550)

•

For
\tau\to i\infty
,
\tilde{g}_{2}\to 4\pi^{4}/3
, and
u\to e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,m^{2}/4
. Then

\lim_{\tau\to i\infty}Z=\pm\frac{4\pi}{m}\frac{1}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}},

(551)

•

For
\tau\to 0
, we have
\tilde{g}_{2}\to 4\pi^{4}/3\,\tau^{-4}
and
u\to e_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,m^{2}/4
. Then

\lim_{\tau\to 0}Z\sim\frac{\pm}{\tau}\frac{4\pi}{m}\frac{1}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}},

(552)

•

For
\tau\to 1
, we have
\tilde{g}_{2}\to 4\pi^{4}/3\,(\tau-1)^{-4}
and
u\to e_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})\,m^{2}/4
. Then

\lim_{\tau\to 1}Z\sim\frac{\pm}{\tau-1}\frac{4\pi}{m}\frac{1}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}}.

(553)

Next we make an Ansatz for
Z
with unknown
K_{i}
’s,

Z^{4}=\left(\frac{4\pi}{m}\right)^{4}\left(\frac{K_{1}}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}}+\frac{K_{2}}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}}+\frac{K_{3}}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}}\right),

(554)

where we assume that in each limit
\tau\to 0,1,i\infty
, one of the
K_{i}
dominates.
To solve for the
K_{i}
’s, we make a further Ansatz:
K_{i}=\alpha_{i}\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{8}+\beta_{i}\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{8}+\gamma_{i}\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{8}
. From the limits (
550
)-(
553
), we deduce the following relations for the
\alpha_{i}
,
\beta_{i}
and
\gamma_{i}
’s:

•

The scaling limit gives that these constants must satisfy
\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}=1
,
\beta_{2}=-\beta_{3}
and
\gamma_{2}=-\gamma_{3}
.

•

The
\tau\to i\infty
limit shows that
\beta_{1}+\gamma_{1}=1
, and
\beta_{2}+\gamma_{2}=0
.

•

The
\tau\to 0
limit shows that
\alpha_{1}+\beta_{1}=0
,
\alpha_{2}+\beta_{2}=1
and
\alpha_{3}+\beta_{3}=0
.

•

The
\tau\to 1
limit shows that
\alpha_{1}+\gamma_{1}=0
,

\alpha_{2}+\gamma_{2}=0
,
\alpha_{3}+\gamma_{3}=1
.

These conditions are solved by

\begin{split}&\alpha_{1}=-1/2,\quad\beta_{1}=1/2,\quad\gamma_{1}=1/2,\\
&\alpha_{2}=1/2,\quad\beta_{2}=1/2,\quad\gamma_{2}=-1/2,\\
&\alpha_{3}=1/2,\quad\beta_{3}=-1/2,\quad\gamma_{3}=1/2.\end{split}

(555)

Using the identity (
510
), we arrive for the
K_{i}
’s at

K_{1}=\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4},\quad K_{2}=\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4},\quad K_{3}=-\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}.

(556)

Multiplying each term by
\vartheta_{i}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}
, we arrive at

Z^{4}=\frac{(\pi/m)^{4}}{\Delta(\tau_{\rm uv})}\left(\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}+\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}-\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{8}\right).

We can simplify this expression by taking the square root

Z^{2}=\frac{\pi^{2}}{m^{2}}\frac{1}{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{12}}\left(\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\,\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}-\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\,\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}\right).

(557)

Using (
529
), we find that in the limit
\tau\to\tau_{\rm uv}
,

Z^{2}
vanishes as

\lim_{\tau\to\tau_{\rm uv}}Z^{2}=-\frac{\pi^{2}}{m^{2}}(16\pi i(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})+O((\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})^{3}).

(558)

To avoid ambiguities in taking a square root, we will assume that

\mathrm{Im}(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})>0
.

Substitution of (
557
) in (
549
), gives
u
as a
bi-modular form with arguments
\tau
and
\tau_{\rm uv}
,

\begin{split}&u(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})=\\
&\frac{-\frac{m^{2}}{12}E_{6}(\tau_{\rm uv})-2m^{2}\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{12}\,\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}(\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}+\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4})+\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}(\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}-\vartheta_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4})}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}-\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}}}{E_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})}.\end{split}

(559)

When
\text{SL}(2,\mathbb{Z})
acts on
\tau_{\rm uv}
and
\tau
simultaneously,
u
is a
bi-modular form of weight two for
\tau_{\rm uv}
and weight 0 for

\tau
. On the other hand,
u
transforms as a modular form for

\Gamma(2)
, if the modular transformation acts on
\tau_{\rm uv}
or
\tau
separately.

By comparing expansions, we find that expression (
559
) agrees with expressions for
u
in the literature
[
44
,
45
]
. The form of
u
in
[
44
, Eq. (5.10)]

is
31
31

31

This expression differs by a sign from

[
44
]
to match our convention.

\begin{split}&u(\tau,\tau_{\rm uv})=-\frac{m^{2}}{4}\\
&\times\frac{e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}(e_{2}(\tau)-e_{3}(\tau))+e_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}(e_{3}(\tau)-e_{1}(\tau))+e_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}(e_{1}(\tau)-e_{2}(\tau))}{e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})(e_{2}(\tau)-e_{3}(\tau))+e_{2}(\tau_{\rm uv})(e_{3}(\tau)-e_{1}(\tau))+e_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})(e_{1}(\tau)-e_{2}(\tau))},\end{split}

(560)

Reference
[
45
, Eq. (4.30)]
gives yet
another expression for
u
as a bi-modular form, if we identify
\tilde{u}
in

[
45
, Eq. (4.30)]
with
4\pi^{2}\,u-m^{2}\pi^{2}\,e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})
in
our notation. The equivalence of the expressions can be verified by
making
(q,q_{{\rm uv}})
-expansions. These expressions confirm moreover
the leading behavior
u\sim a^{2}
using the expression for
\tau
(
33
).

In the scaling limit (
20
), we reproduce

u+\frac{m^{2}}{8}\,e_{1}(\tau_{\rm uv})\to u_{0}.

(561)

The large
\tau_{\rm uv}
expansion is

u=-\frac{m^{2}}{12}-2\,m^{2}\left(2\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}}{\vartheta_{2}(\tau)^{4}}-1\right)\,q_{\rm uv}^{1/2}+O(q_{\rm uv})

(562)

The expressions for
u
allow to write the physical discriminant

\Delta_{\rm phys}
explicitly as a bi-modular form,

\begin{split}\Delta_{\rm phys}&=\prod_{i=1}^{3}(u-\tfrac{m^{2}}{4}e_{i}(\tau_{\rm uv}))=\left(\frac{2\pi}{Z}\right)^{6}\,\frac{\eta(\tau)^{12}}{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{12}}\\
&=(2m)^{6}\,\frac{\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{24}\,\eta(\tau)^{12}}{\left(\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}-\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}\right)^{3}}.\end{split}

(563)

Finally, we can show that the explicit expression for
Z
(
557
) provides

da/du
. To this end, let
a
be given by a contour integral along a one-cycle with
coordinate
z\in[0,1]
. Then,

\begin{split}\frac{da}{du}&=\frac{1}{4\pi}\oint\frac{dx}{y}\\
&=\frac{1}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{dx/dz}{y}\,dz\\
&=\frac{Z}{4\pi}\int_{0}^{1}\frac{d\tilde{x}(z)}{\tilde{y}(z)}\\
&=\frac{Z}{4\pi},\end{split}

(564)

where the
\tilde{x}
and
\tilde{y}
on the third line are the variables of the
Weierstrass curve (
545
). Reference
[
17
]
expressed
da/du
in terms of
u
,

m
,
\tau
and
\tau_{\rm uv}
as

\frac{da}{du}=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{u-\frac{1}{4}m^{2}e_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})}}\frac{\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{2}}{\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{2}}.

(565)

Using the explicit expression for
da/du
(
564
), this provides another equivalent expression for
u
as
a bi-modular form.

We determine also
\frac{da}{d\tau}
, which is useful for the analysis
of the measure of the
u
-plane integral. We have

\begin{split}\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{da}{d\tau}&=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\frac{da}{du}\frac{du}{d\tau}\\
&=\frac{8\,\Delta_{\rm phys}}{m^{2}}\,\left(\frac{da}{du}\right)^{3}\\
&=\frac{8\,m\,\eta(\tau_{\rm uv})^{6}\,\eta(\tau)^{12}}{(\vartheta_{4}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{3}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4}-\vartheta_{3}(\tau)^{4}\vartheta_{4}(\tau_{\rm uv})^{4})^{3/2}},\end{split}

(566)

where we substituted (
185
). The equations demonstrate that
a

has (approximate) weight 1 in
\tau
and weight 0 in
\tau_{\rm uv}
. It furthermore confirms that the expansion of
2a/m
in
\tau-\tau_{\rm uv}
starts as

\frac{2a}{m}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-\pi i(\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})}}+O\!\left((\tau-\tau_{\rm uv})^{1/2}\right),

(567)

which is consistent with the leading term of (
34
).

Appendix C
Almost complex four-manifolds

This appendix reviews a few aspects of almost complex four-manifolds. We refer to
[
6
,
63
,
73
]
for further reading. A four-manifold
X
is almost complex if
X
admits an almost complex structure, i.e. a fiber-preserving automorphism
\mathcal{J}:T_{X}\to T_{X}
of the tangent bundle
T_{X}
such that
\mathcal{J}^{2}=-1
. A metric
g
is compatible with
\mathcal{J}
if
g(\mathcal{J}u,\mathcal{J}v)=g(u,v)
, and such metrics always exist. An almost Hermitian manifold is the triple
(X,g,\mathcal{J})
.
We let
\omega
be the fundamental (1,1)-form compatible with the metric
\omega(u,v)=g(\mathcal{J}u,v)
.
\omega
is self-dual but not necessarily closed for an ACS manifold. It defines the Lee form
\theta
,
d\omega=\theta\wedge\omega

[
73
]
.

For an ACS four-manifold
X
, let
\pi_{p,q}
be the canonical projection from
\Omega^{p+q}(X)
to
\Omega^{p,q}
. We can write
\pi_{0,1}
explicitly as
\pi_{0,1}=\frac{1}{2}(1+i\mathcal{J})
and
\pi_{1,0}=\frac{1}{2}(1-i\mathcal{J})
. We define furthermore the Nijenhuis tensor
N_{\mathcal{J}}
by

N_{\mathcal{J}}(u,v)=[\mathcal{J}u,\mathcal{J}v]-\mathcal{J}[u,\mathcal{J}v]-\mathcal{J}[\mathcal{J}u,v]-[u,v],

(568)

where
u,v\in TX
are two vector fields. This tensor gives the anti-holomorphic component of the commutator of two holomorphic vector fields plus its complex conjugate,

N_{\mathcal{J}}(u,v)=-4\left(\pi_{0,1}[\pi_{1,0}u,\pi_{1,0}v]+\pi_{1,0}[\pi_{0,1}u,\pi_{0,1}v]\right),

(569)

or equivalently,

\pi_{0,1}[\pi_{1,0}u,\pi_{1,0}v]=-\frac{1}{4}\pi_{0,1}N(u,v).

(570)

Thus
N_{\mathcal{J}}
is the sum of a
(2,-1)
and a
(-1,2)
tensor, and
N_{\mathcal{J}}
vanishes if
\mathcal{J}
is integrable, i.e. if
X
is a complex manifold.

The exterior derivative
d:\Omega^{r}(X)\to\Omega^{r+1}(X)
, can be written as the sum

d=\sum_{r+s=p+q+1}\pi_{p,q+1}\circ d.

(571)

In fact, for fixed
r
and
s
, there are only four terms on the rhs

d:\Omega^{r,s}\to\Omega^{r+2,s-1}\oplus\Omega^{r+1,s}\oplus\Omega^{r,s+1}\oplus\Omega^{r-1,s+2}.

(572)

We define Dolbeault operators,

\bar{\partial}:\Omega^{p,q}(X)\to\Omega^{p,q+1}(X),\qquad\bar{\partial}=\pi_{p,q+1}\circ d,

(573)

which together with
\partial
give the projections to the second and third terms on the rhs of (
572
). The projection to the first and last term is given in terms of the Nijenhuis tensor
N_{\mathcal{J}}
(
568
). To see this for a
(0,1)
-form
\alpha\in\Omega^{0,1}(X)
, we calculate for
u,v\in TX

\pi_{2,0}\,d\alpha(u,v)=-\alpha(\pi_{0,1}[\pi_{1,0}u,\pi_{1,0}v])=\frac{1}{4}\alpha(N_{\mathcal{J}}(u,v)),

(574)

where we used
d\alpha(u,v)=u\alpha(v)-v\alpha(u)-\alpha([u,v])
. With respect to an orthonormal frame
e^{a}
, we have in components,

d\alpha=\partial\alpha+\bar{\partial}\alpha+(N_{\mathcal{J}})^{a}_{bc}\,\alpha_{a}\,e^{b}\wedge e^{c}.

(575)

If
N_{\mathcal{J}}
is non-vanishing,
\bar{\partial}^{2}\neq 0
, and
d\neq\partial+\bar{\partial}
acting on generic
(r,s)
-forms. For an ACS manifold,
d=\partial+\bar{\partial}
only when acting
\Omega^{0}(X)
.

We also introduce the adjoint Dolbeault operators,
\bar{\partial}^{\dagger}=-*\partial\,*:\Omega^{p,q}(X)\to\Omega^{p,q-1}(X)
and
\partial^{\dagger}=-*\bar{\partial}\,*:\Omega^{p,q}(X)\to\Omega^{p-1,q}(X)
with
*
the Hodge
*:\Omega^{p,q}\to\Omega^{2-q,2-p}
.

Symbol list
